01
Station 01 · 20 marks

Pre-operative screen in polycythaemia vera

What this station tests: Before you believe an abnormal clotting result, check the sample: too much citrate for the amount of plasma makes clotting times falsely long, and a wrong result that has already been acted on must be formally corrected.

The case

A 64-year-old man with polycythaemia vera is admitted with a fractured neck of femur for urgent hemiarthroplasty. His haematocrit is 0.62 and isovolaemic venesection has been started. Pre-operative coagulation screening is reported as abnormal and surgery is postponed. The biomedical scientist notes that the citrate tube was visibly under-filled. A second sample is drawn by clean peripheral venepuncture into a tube containing a reduced, calculated volume of citrate.

In plain wordsA 64-year-old man with polycythaemia vera (a disorder in which the bone marrow makes too many red cells) has broken his hip and needs urgent surgery. His haematocrit (the fraction of the blood made up of red cells) is 0.62. His first clotting screen looked abnormal, so surgery was postponed. The laboratory then noticed the citrate tube was under-filled, and a repeat sample taken into a tube with less citrate was normal.
Coagulation screen · first and repeat samples
TestSample 1Sample 2Reference
Prothrombin time18.4 s12.6 s11–14 s
Activated partial thromboplastin time44 s30 s27–35 s
Clauss fibrinogen2.9 g/L3.1 g/L2.0–4.0 g/L
Thrombin time15 s14 s12–17 s
Platelet count480 ×10⁹/L474 ×10⁹/L150–400 ×10⁹/L

How to read the data

  • Prothrombin time (PT) 18.4 s and activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) 44 s are prolonged in Sample 1, but both are normal in Sample 2 (12.6 s and 30 s). A change this large between two samples from the same patient points to the sample, not the patient.
  • Clauss fibrinogen is normal in both samples (2.9 and 3.1 g/L). A low fibrinogen does not explain the long clotting times.
  • Thrombin time (TT) is normal in both samples (15 s and 14 s). The TT adds thrombin directly, without the recalcification step of the PT and APTT, so it is expected to be less affected by excess citrate, as here.
  • The platelet count is raised (480 and 474 ×10⁹/L) and almost the same in both samples. It is a genuine result: it is measured on a separate EDTA (full blood count) sample, so citrate problems do not affect it.
  • The haematocrit of 0.62 is well above 0.55, the level above which citrate adjustment is advised.

The five questions

Q1
Give two pre-analytical explanations for the abnormal results in Sample 1. State what the repeat sample proves, and what it cannot.
4 marks

Model answer

1) Under-filled citrate tube. 2) Elevated haematocrit (0.62). Both raise the effective citrate-to-plasma ratio. The normal repeat proves the effect was pre-analytical. It cannot separate the two causes, because the second tube corrected both at once; each alone can be sufficient (a fill below 80% of nominal volume, or a haematocrit above 0.55). The platelet count is genuine and unaffected (separate EDTA sample).

Explained step by step

Step 1 — what “pre-analytical” means. Something went wrong before the sample was analysed: during collection, filling, mixing or transport. The analyser then measures correctly, but it measures a faulty sample.

Step 2 — find the two problems. The biomedical scientist saw that the citrate tube was visibly under-filled. The patient also has a haematocrit of 0.62. Each of these, on its own, leaves too much citrate for the amount of plasma. That falsely prolongs the PT and APTT.

Step 3 — what the repeat proves. Sample 2 was taken by clean peripheral venepuncture into a tube with a reduced, calculated volume of citrate. Its PT and APTT are normal. So the abnormality in Sample 1 came from the sample (pre-analytical), not from a true clotting disorder.

Step 4 — what the repeat cannot prove. The second tube fixed both problems at the same time: it was filled properly and its citrate was adjusted for the haematocrit. When you change two things at once, you cannot tell which one was responsible. Here the short fill and the haematocrit of 0.62 are each enough on their own.

Step 5 — do not forget the platelets. The platelet count is raised in both samples and barely changes. It is measured on a separate EDTA tube, so citrate errors cannot touch it. Full marks require you to say it is genuine.

Try it: is this tube acceptable?

RejectBelow 80% of nominal volume (ICSH Recommendation 9.1).

Under-filling and a high haematocrit both raise citrate per mL of plasma; this one number combines them. The excess is calculated from the ICSH formula's basis (0.185 mL citrate per mL plasma), not from a study.

ICSH 2021, Recommendations 9.1 and 10.1 (PMID 34097805); CLSI GP41 formula as quoted by ICSH; Dacie and Lewis 12e, Ch. 18, p. 375. Validation: Marlar 2006 (PMID 16880137). Open the full calculator with neonatal guidance and the evidence.

Think of it like this
Think of fruit squash. The glass already holds a fixed amount of concentrate (citrate). If you pour in too little water (under-filling), or the “water” is mostly ice (red cells instead of plasma), the drink ends up far too strong.
Common mistake
Saying the repeat proves the short fill was “the” cause. Both variables were corrected together, so one repeat cannot separate them. Candidates also forget to comment on the platelet count.
Remember
Change two things at once and you cannot know which one mattered.

What earns the marks

  • Under-filling of the citrate tube
  • Elevated haematocrit (0.62); both raise the citrate-to-plasma ratio
  • Repeat proves a pre-analytical cause but cannot separate the two, as both were corrected together
  • Platelet count is genuine — measured on a separate EDTA sample
Evidence[3]Kitchen 2021Practical Haemostasis — Pre-Analytical Variables
Evidence check verified against the sources below
How short is too short? Fill-volume thresholds

ICSH gives the laboratory a hard rule: a citrate tube filled to less than 80% of its nominal volume should be rejected and not analysed [S1]. Tubes at 80–90% may be acceptable only if local validation shows patient management would not change [S1].

The APTT is the more fragile test. In Reneke's study the APTT was prolonged in most tubes filled below 90%, while the PT tolerated more under-fill [S2]. That is why under-filled samples often show a long APTT first.

Exam point: the error here should have been stopped at reception. BSH asks that every sample be checked for fill and clots before centrifugation [S3], so the post-analytical failure began as a missed pre-analytical check.

  1. [S1] Kitchen S, Adcock DM, Dauer R, et al. ICSH recommendations for collection of blood samples for coagulation testing. Int J Lab Hematol 2021;43:571-580 PMID 34097805 · doi:10.1111/ijlh.13584Blood tubes with &lt;80% of nominal filling volume should be rejected by the laboratory and should not be analysed.
  2. [S2] Reneke J, Etzell J, Leslie S, et al. Prolonged prothrombin time and activated partial thromboplastin time due to underfilled specimen tubes with 109 mmol/L (3.2%) citrate anticoagulant. Am J Clin Pathol 1998;109:754-757 PMID 9620035 · doi:10.1093/ajcp/109.6.754In contrast, APTT was much less tolerant to underfilling, with prolonged values observed in most specimens filled to less than 90% of capacity.
  3. [S3] Baker P, Platton S, Gibson C, et al. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis. Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776Prior to centrifugation samples should be examined for correct fill and clots.
Q2
Explain the mechanism by which each of these prolongs a clotting time.
4 marks

Model answer

Coagulation samples use trisodium citrate at 1 part to 9 parts blood; citrate chelates ionised calcium. Under-filling (less blood) or high haematocrit (less plasma in a correctly filled tube) raises the citrate-to-plasma ratio. On recalcification in the analyser, calcium is only partly repleted, so PT and APTT are spuriously prolonged. The TT, which adds thrombin directly without a recalcification step, is expected to be less affected — as here. A normal TT also makes significant unfractionated heparin contamination unlikely.

Explained step by step

How a citrate tube works. Coagulation samples are collected into trisodium citrate at 1 part anticoagulant to 9 parts blood. Citrate stops clotting by chelating ionised calcium — it grabs the calcium and holds it. Clotting needs calcium, so the blood stays liquid.

How the test restarts clotting. In the analyser, calcium is added back to the plasma (recalcification) and the time to form a clot is measured. The amount added suits the normal ratio of citrate to plasma.

Mechanism 1 — under-filling. The tube contains a fixed volume of citrate. If less blood goes in, the same citrate is shared by less plasma. The citrate-to-plasma ratio rises.

Mechanism 2 — high haematocrit. The tube is filled correctly, but much of the blood is red cells. Citrate mixes only with the plasma, and there is less plasma than usual. Again the citrate-to-plasma ratio rises. The key word is plasma, not blood.

The result. With extra citrate in the plasma, the calcium added by the analyser only partly replaces what was bound. Clotting is delayed, so the PT and APTT are falsely long. The TT is performed by adding thrombin to the plasma without the recalcification step used in the PT and APTT, so it is expected to be less affected by excess citrate — consistent with Sample 1 (TT 15 s).

Think of it like this
Citrate is a sponge for calcium. The analyser pours in one fixed jug of calcium, enough for a normal-sized sponge. If the sponge is too big for the amount of plasma, the jug is not enough, and clotting stalls.
Common mistake
Saying a high haematocrit changes the ratio of citrate to “blood”. The tube is full of blood; it is the ratio of citrate to plasma that rises.
Remember
Citrate lives in the plasma, not in the red cells.

What earns the marks

  • Citrate 1:9 with blood; anticoagulates by chelating ionised calcium
  • Under-filling or high haematocrit raises the citrate-to-plasma ratio
  • Recalcification only partly repletes calcium, so PT and APTT are spuriously prolonged
  • TT is far less calcium-dependent and is spared, as here
Evidence[3]Kitchen 2021Practical Haemostasis — Pre-Analytical Variables
Q3
Using C = 1.85 × 10⁻³ × (100 − Hct%) × V, calculate the volume of 3.2% trisodium citrate required for a 4.5 mL whole-blood draw in this patient. Show your working.
4 marks

Model answer

Hct 0.62 = 62%. C = 1.85 × 10⁻³ × (100 − 62) × 4.5 = 1.85 × 10⁻³ × 38 × 4.5 = 0.316 mL, approximately 0.32 mL of 3.2% trisodium citrate, against 0.5 mL in a standard 4.5 mL tube. The laboratory must prepare the tube and clearly document the reduced volume.

Explained step by step

Haematocrit 0.62 = 62%
C = 1.85 × 10⁻³ × (100 − Hct%) × V
C = 1.85 × 10⁻³ × (100 − 62) × 4.5
C = 1.85 × 10⁻³ × 38 × 4.5
C = 0.316 mL (approximately 0.32 mL; standard tube 0.5 mL)

Why calculate at all? This patient has less plasma in every millilitre of blood. To keep the correct balance of citrate to plasma, his tube must contain less citrate than a standard tube.

Read the formula. C is the citrate volume in mL. (100 − Hct%) is the percentage of the blood that is plasma. V is the volume of whole blood to be drawn, in mL. 1.85 × 10⁻³ is the fixed constant given in the question.

Convert the haematocrit. The haematocrit is reported as 0.62. The formula needs a percentage, so use 62. Then 100 − 62 = 38: only 38% of this patient's blood is plasma.

Multiply. 1.85 × 10⁻³ × 38 × 4.5 = 0.316 mL, which is approximately 0.32 mL. A standard 4.5 mL tube contains 0.5 mL, so this patient needs clearly less citrate.

Practical step. This is not a stock tube from the shelf. The laboratory must prepare it, and the reduced citrate volume must be clearly documented.

Try it: citrate for a high haematocrit
0.316 mL citrate

0.184 mL
Citrate : blood

Plasma in sample
1.71 mL
Adjust this tubeHaematocrit above 0.55: ICSH Recommendation 10.1 applies.
MethodCitrate (mL)Basis

ICSH 2021, Recommendations 9.1 and 10.1 (PMID 34097805); CLSI GP41 formula as quoted by ICSH; Dacie and Lewis 12e, Ch. 18, p. 375. Validation: Marlar 2006 (PMID 16880137). Open the full calculator with neonatal guidance and the evidence.

Think of it like this
It is like salting soup: if the pot holds less soup, you add less salt. Less plasma means less citrate.
Common mistake
Putting 0.62 instead of 62 into (100 − Hct%). This gives a nonsense answer.
Remember
High haematocrit, less plasma, less citrate.

What earns the marks

  • Uses the haematocrit as 62, not 0.62
  • Correct working: 1.85 × 10⁻³ × 38 × 4.5
  • Answer 0.316 mL, approximately 0.32 mL (versus 0.5 mL standard)
  • Tube prepared by the laboratory; reduced volume documented
Evidence[3]Kitchen 2021Practical Haemostasis — Pre-Analytical Variables (citrate correction formula)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Why the haematocrit formula matters more as the haematocrit rises

The effect of a high haematocrit is not linear. Marlar and colleagues compared adjusted and unadjusted tubes and found PT and APTT differences increased exponentially as the haematocrit rose [S1]. Small rises above 0.55 matter less than very high values, so a haematocrit of 0.62 is well into the range where adjustment changes results.

Two formulas exist: the CLSI formula and C = 1.85 × 10⁻³ × (100 − Hct) × V. ICSH prefers the second because it has been validated for PT, APTT and other tests, and notes the two give practically identical citrate-to-blood ratios [S2].

  1. [S1] Marlar RA, Potts RM, Marlar AA. Effect on routine and special coagulation testing values of citrate anticoagulant adjustment in patients with high hematocrit values. Am J Clin Pathol 2006;126:400-405 PMID 16880137 · doi:10.1309/RRQKT2JEYV33D19DProthrombin time (PT) and activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) results from nonadjusted and adjusted samples were statistically different and exponentially increased with increasing hematocrit values.
  2. [S2] Kitchen S, Adcock DM, Dauer R, et al. ICSH recommendations for collection of blood samples for coagulation testing. Int J Lab Hematol 2021;43:571-580 PMID 34097805 · doi:10.1111/ijlh.13584This formula has been validated for use with PT, APTT and other coagulation tests, so should be preferred although the calculated citrate-to-blood ratio using the two formulas is practically identical.
Q4
Above what haematocrit is citrate adjustment advised, and what pre-analytical factor other than haematocrit should always be checked before repeating?
4 marks

Model answer

Adjust citrate when the haematocrit exceeds 0.55. Before repeating, also confirm the sample: was not drawn from a line containing heparin; was filled to the mark; was correctly mixed and not clotted; reached the laboratory within the acceptable interval — most tests should be performed within 4 h of collection (the PT is stable for 24 h; an APTT for heparin monitoring within 1 h).

Explained step by step

The threshold. Citrate adjustment is advised when the haematocrit is above 0.55. This patient, at 0.62, is clearly above it.

Why look beyond haematocrit? A repeat sample is only useful if it is free of the other common errors. Otherwise you may simply repeat the mistake, or introduce a new one.

The checklist.

  • Heparin — was the sample drawn from a line containing heparin?
  • Fill — was the tube filled to the mark?
  • Mixing and clots — was it mixed correctly, and is it free of clots?
  • Time — did it reach the laboratory within the acceptable interval? Most tests should be performed within 4 hours of collection (the PT is stable for up to 24 hours).

Notice that the repeat in this case was taken by clean peripheral venepuncture. That is exactly what this checklist is designed to achieve.

Think of it like this
Like a pilot's pre-flight checklist: you do not just fix the one warning light — you run through the whole list before take-off.
Common mistake
Giving only the haematocrit threshold and forgetting heparin contamination from a line.
Remember
Above 0.55, adjust — then check line, fill, mix, clot and clock.

What earns the marks

  • Haematocrit above 0.55
  • Not drawn from a line containing heparin
  • Filled to the mark, correctly mixed and not clotted
  • Tested within the acceptable interval (most tests within 4 h of collection; PT stable 24 h)
Evidence[3]Kitchen 2021[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — Pre-Analytical Variables (haematocrit 0.55 threshold, four-hour interval)
Q5
Sample 1 was authorised and reported before the error was recognised. State the post-analytical actions required.
4 marks

Model answer

Retract and amend the authorised report — do not simply add a comment. Telephone the requesting clinician, because the original result changed management (surgery was cancelled). Record the event on the laboratory incident system. Review whether the analyser or middleware should flag short-fill and high-haematocrit samples prospectively.

Explained step by step

What “post-analytical” means. Everything that happens after the result leaves the analyser: authorising, reporting, communicating and following up. Here, a wrong result has already been authorised and acted on — surgery was postponed.

Correct the record. Retract and amend the authorised report. Adding a comment is not enough, because the wrong numbers would still sit in the record and could be read and acted on again.

Tell the clinician directly. Telephone the requesting clinician. The original result changed management, so it is not safe to wait for someone to notice an amended report.

Learn from it. Record the event on the laboratory incident system. Then review whether the analyser or middleware (the software between the analyser and the reporting system) should flag short-fill and high-haematocrit samples prospectively, before results are released.

Why examiners care. Candidates almost always describe the repeat test and stop. The marks are for recognising that an incorrect result already acted upon creates a post-analytical duty: amendment, active communication and incident reporting.

Think of it like this
If a newspaper prints a wrong headline that people have already acted on, a small footnote next week is not enough. It publishes a correction and contacts the people affected.
Common mistake
Describing the repeat test and stopping there.
Remember
Wrong result acted on: amend, phone, report, prevent.

What earns the marks

  • Retract and amend the report — not just a comment
  • Telephone the requesting clinician, because management was changed
  • Record on the laboratory incident system
  • Review prospective flagging of short-fill and high-haematocrit samples
EvidenceConsultant laboratory practice, as set out in the examiner's key; no published guideline is cited for this answer.
Evidence check verified against the sources below
What an amended report must contain

Correcting the number is not enough. Laboratory reports are treated as legal documents, so simply changing the result is inadequate [S1].

The record should show when the clinician was called, who called and who received the amended result, and both the erroneous and the correct values [S2]. Where an erroneous result led to harm or a changed decision, an incident report and root-cause analysis are expected [S1].

In this case the postponed operation is the reason the telephone call and the incident report are mandatory, not optional.

  1. [S1] Favaloro EJ, Gosselin RC, Pasalic L, Lippi G. Post-analytical issues in hemostasis and thrombosis testing: an update. Methods Mol Biol 2023;2663:787-811 PMID 37204753 · doi:10.1007/978-1-0716-3175-1_53Laboratory reports are likely considered to be legal documents, and adequate description of the amended report is required,so merely changing the result is not adequate.
  2. [S2] Favaloro EJ, Gosselin RC, Pasalic L, Lippi G. Post-analytical issues in hemostasis and thrombosis testing: an update. Methods Mol Biol 2023;2663:787-811 PMID 37204753 · doi:10.1007/978-1-0716-3175-1_53There must be ample documentation in the record to indicate date and time of call, name and title of person receiving the amended report,the name and title of person calling the amended report

Take home from this station

  1. Always check the tube before believing an abnormal clotting result.
  2. Under-filling and a high haematocrit both raise the citrate-to-plasma ratio, falsely prolonging the PT and APTT while sparing the TT.
  3. Above a haematocrit of 0.55, use a laboratory-prepared tube with a calculated, reduced citrate volume.
  4. A wrong result that has already been acted on must be retracted, amended, telephoned through and reported as an incident.
02
Station 02 · 20 marks

Spontaneous bruising in a woman of 72

What this station tests: Recognise acquired haemophilia A from the laboratory pattern, prove it is not a lupus anticoagulant, and understand how the factor VIII autoantibody behaves in mixing, dilution and Bethesda tests.

The case

A 72-year-old woman presents with extensive spontaneous bruising and a painful, expanding left thigh haematoma. She takes no anticoagulant and has had two uncomplicated deliveries and an uneventful cholecystectomy at 45. Platelet count and prothrombin time are normal. The activated partial thromboplastin time is 96 s against a reference interval of 27–35 s.

In plain wordsA 72-year-old woman has new, extensive bruising and a painful, growing haematoma (a large collection of blood) in her left thigh. She takes no anticoagulant, and childbirth and surgery in the past caused no bleeding problems. Her platelet count and prothrombin time (PT) are normal, but her activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) is very long at 96 s.
Specialist coagulation panel
InvestigationResult
1:1 mix with normal plasma, immediate43 s
1:1 mix, after 2 h incubation at 37 °C78 s
Factor VIII, one-stage, 1:10 dilution3 IU/dL
Factor VIII, one-stage, 1:20 dilution4 IU/dL
Factor VIII, one-stage, 1:40 dilution4 IU/dL
Factor VIII, chromogenic5 IU/dL
Factor IX, factor XI, factor XIINormal
dRVVT screen / confirmNegative
Nijmegen-modified Bethesda assay12 NBU/mL

How to read the data

  • APTT 96 s (reference 27–35 s) with a normal PT and platelet count: an isolated prolonged APTT. Factors IX, XI and XII are normal, so attention turns to factor VIII.
  • Mixing with normal plasma: the immediate 1:1 mix shortens to 43 s (a substantial correction, though still above the reference range), but after 2 hours at 37 °C it lengthens again to 78 s. This is the mark of a time- and temperature-dependent inhibitor.
  • One-stage factor VIII is severely low (3–4 IU/dL) and stays the same at 1:10, 1:20 and 1:40 dilutions. This is parallelism: the low level is genuine.
  • Chromogenic factor VIII is also low (5 IU/dL), and the dilute Russell's viper venom time (dRVVT) screen/confirm is negative. Together these exclude a lupus anticoagulant as the cause.
  • The Nijmegen-modified Bethesda assay gives 12 NBU/mL (Nijmegen–Bethesda units per mL): a factor VIII inhibitor is present.

The five questions

Q1
State the diagnosis, and give the two findings in this panel that most strongly exclude a lupus anticoagulant as the cause of the low factor VIII.
4 marks

Model answer

Acquired haemophilia A — a neutralising autoantibody against factor VIII: isolated long APTT, normal PT and platelets, severe factor VIII reduction and a positive Nijmegen–Bethesda titre in a previously unaffected elderly woman with spontaneous soft-tissue bleeding. Lupus anticoagulant is made an unlikely explanation by (1) the negative dRVVT and (2) the concordantly low chromogenic factor VIII, because chromogenic assays are largely (not completely) insensitive to lupus anticoagulant. The two conditions can coexist. Bleeding rather than thrombosis is a third pointer.

Explained step by step

Put the pieces together. An older woman with no previous bleeding history now has spontaneous soft-tissue bleeding. Only the APTT is prolonged; the PT and platelet count are normal. Factor VIII is severely reduced, and the Nijmegen–Bethesda assay detects an inhibitor. This is the classic picture of acquired haemophilia A: her immune system has made an autoantibody that neutralises factor VIII.

Why think about lupus anticoagulant at all? A lupus anticoagulant also prolongs the APTT and interferes with phospholipid-dependent clotting assays, including one-stage factor assays. So you must show it is not the explanation for the low factor VIII.

Finding 1 — the negative dRVVT. The dRVVT screen/confirm is a test for lupus anticoagulant. Here it is negative.

Finding 2 — the low chromogenic factor VIII. A lupus anticoagulant interferes with phospholipid-dependent clotting assays but not with the chromogenic method. So a genuinely low chromogenic result (5 IU/dL) cannot be blamed on a lupus anticoagulant.

A third pointer. The clinical picture is bleeding. Lupus anticoagulant is associated with thrombosis, not haematomas.

Think of it like this
If a smoke alarm (the clotting assay) goes off, you need to know whether there is a real fire. The chromogenic assay is a second alarm that a lupus anticoagulant cannot trigger. When it rings too, the fire — a truly low factor VIII — is real.
Common mistake
Naming the diagnosis but not the two specific findings — the negative dRVVT and the low chromogenic factor VIII — that exclude a lupus anticoagulant.
Remember
Low chromogenic plus negative dRVVT: not lupus.

What earns the marks

  • Diagnosis: acquired haemophilia A (neutralising factor VIII autoantibody)
  • Supporting pattern: isolated long APTT, severe factor VIII reduction, positive Bethesda titre, new spontaneous bleeding
  • Negative dRVVT
  • Low chromogenic factor VIII — chromogenic method largely insensitive to lupus anticoagulant
Evidence[1]Tiede 2020[10]Collins 2013Practical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Why a low chromogenic FVIII makes lupus anticoagulant unlikely, not impossible

Chromogenic FVIII assays are usually insensitive to lupus anticoagulant, so a concordantly low chromogenic result argues strongly for a true FVIII deficiency [S1]. But they are not immune: a lupus anticoagulant can artificially lower FVIII in chromogenic as well as one-stage assays [S2].

Two further traps:

  • AHA and a lupus anticoagulant can coexist in the same patient [S1].
  • In a bleeding patient, AHA should be considered likely even if lupus anticoagulant tests are positive [S2].

So say “lupus anticoagulant is an unlikely explanation”, then point to the bleeding phenotype and the positive Bethesda titre.

  1. [S1] Tiede A, Collins P, Knoebl P, et al. International recommendations on the diagnosis and treatment of acquired hemophilia A. Haematologica 2020;105:1791-1801 PMID 32381574 · doi:10.3324/haematol.2019.230771However, it should be noted that AHA and LA are both autoimmune disorders that can co-exist in the same patient.
  2. [S2] Tiede A, Werwitzke S, Scharf RE. Laboratory diagnosis of acquired hemophilia A: limitations, consequences, and challenges. Semin Thromb Hemost 2014;40:803-811 PMID 25299927 · doi:10.1055/s-0034-1390004LA causes a thrombophilic state, but can artificially result in low FVIII:C activity. This is true for one-stage clotting tests but also chromogenic substrate assays.
Q2
Interpret the immediate and incubated mixing studies. What property of the antibody does the difference between them demonstrate?
4 marks

Model answer

The immediate mix corrects substantially (96 s to 43 s); the incubated mix re-prolongs markedly (to 78 s). This shows a time- and temperature-dependent (progressive) inhibitor: factor VIII autoantibodies typically need incubation at 37 °C to neutralise factor VIII, which is why the 2-hour step exists; a high-titre inhibitor can also prolong the immediate mix, so mixing studies cannot establish or exclude AHA. A factor IX inhibitor acts immediately; in a simple deficiency both mixes correct.

Explained step by step

What a mixing study does. The patient's plasma is mixed 1:1 with normal plasma. Normal plasma supplies any missing factor. If the problem is only a shortage of a factor, the mix corrects. If there is an inhibitor, it may also attack the factor supplied by the normal plasma.

The immediate mix. The APTT falls from 96 s to 43 s — a substantial correction. Looked at alone, this could be mistaken for a simple factor shortage.

The incubated mix. After 2 hours at 37 °C, the APTT climbs back to 78 s. Something in the patient's plasma has slowly neutralised the factor VIII supplied by the normal plasma.

The property shown. The antibody is time- and temperature-dependent — it is progressive. Factor VIII autoantibodies typically need incubation at 37 °C to neutralise factor VIII (though a high-titre inhibitor can act quickly). That is why the 2-hour incubation step exists: without it, this inhibitor could be missed.

Compare. A factor IX inhibitor is immediate-acting and needs no incubation. In a simple factor deficiency, both the immediate and the incubated mixes correct.

Think of it like this
A slow leak in a tyre: pump it up (add normal plasma) and it looks fine straight away, but come back two hours later and it is flat again.
Common mistake
Calling the immediate correction a simple factor deficiency without looking at the incubated mix.
Remember
Factor VIII antibodies are slow: incubate before you conclude.

What earns the marks

  • Immediate mix corrects substantially: 96 s to 43 s
  • Incubated mix re-prolongs markedly: to 78 s
  • Time- and temperature-dependent (progressive) inhibitor, typical of factor VIII autoantibodies
  • Contrast: factor IX inhibitor acts immediately; simple deficiency corrects in both mixes
Evidence[1]Tiede 2020[10]Collins 2013Practical Haemostasis — Inhibitor Assays
Q3
The one-stage factor VIII is essentially unchanged across three dilutions. Name this phenomenon, state what it tells you here, and state what the published evidence says about which inhibitors actually cause its opposite.
4 marks

Model answer

Parallelism. The apparent factor VIII does not rise on dilution, so it is a genuine reduction, not a dilution-dependent analytical artefact. Ruinemans-Koerts and colleagues showed that non-parallelism in the one-stage assay is a phenomenon of lupus anticoagulants, not of individual factor inhibitors. A specific factor VIII inhibitor typically gives parallel curves; a high-titre lupus anticoagulant with a lupus-sensitive reagent gives the rising, non-parallel pattern.

Explained step by step

Why test at several dilutions? In a one-stage factor assay, the patient's plasma is tested at more than one dilution. If something in the plasma interferes with the test itself, further dilution weakens the interference, and the apparent factor level rises.

Parallelism. Here factor VIII reads 3, 4 and 4 IU/dL at 1:10, 1:20 and 1:40. It does not rise. This tells you the low factor VIII is a genuine reduction, not a dilution-dependent analytical artefact.

The surprising evidence. Many people assume any inhibitor causes non-parallelism (the opposite pattern, where results rise on dilution). Ruinemans-Koerts and colleagues demonstrated that non-parallelism in the one-stage assay is a phenomenon of lupus anticoagulants, not of individual factor inhibitors.

So, in practice: a specific factor VIII inhibitor typically gives parallel curves — as here. A high-titre lupus anticoagulant tested with a lupus-sensitive reagent gives the rising, non-parallel pattern.

Exam nuance. Ruinemans-Koerts and colleagues found non-parallelism to be a lupus anticoagulant phenomenon rather than one of individual factor inhibitors. The BSH guideline, however, notes that a high-titre specific inhibitor can reduce several one-stage assays and that parallelism may be lost with immediate-acting inhibitors. Qualify the statement; do not deny it outright.

Think of it like this
Looking at a hill through fog: as the fog (interference) thins, the hill seems to grow taller. But if the hill is genuinely small, it stays the same size however clear the air becomes.
Common mistake
Writing “an inhibitor causes non-parallelism” without qualification: it is typical of lupus anticoagulants, whereas specific FVIII inhibitors usually leave the FVIII assay parallel (other intrinsic factors may read falsely low until further diluted).
Remember
Non-parallel? Think lupus. Factor VIII antibody? Usually parallel.

What earns the marks

  • Names parallelism
  • Low factor VIII is a genuine reduction, not a dilution-dependent artefact
  • Ruinemans-Koerts: non-parallelism is a lupus anticoagulant phenomenon, not of individual factor inhibitors
  • Specific factor VIII inhibitors typically parallel; high-titre lupus anticoagulant non-parallel
Evidence[6]Ruinemans-Koerts 2010Practical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays
Q4
The titre is 12 NBU/mL, yet 3 IU/dL of factor VIII is still measurable. Explain this using inhibitor kinetics. In what circumstance would the plasma need heat treatment at 56 °C before the Bethesda assay, and why?
4 marks

Model answer

Type II inhibitor with complex, non-linear kinetics, characteristic of acquired haemophilia autoantibodies: rapid initial inactivation, then a slower phase, never complete even at maximum antibody concentration — so residual factor VIII coexists with a high titre. (Type I, alloantibodies in congenital severe haemophilia A: second-order kinetics, complete inactivation.) Residual factor VIII distorts the Bethesda assay; heating at 56 °C for 30 minutes then centrifuging reduces factor VIII:C and antigen to <0.10 IU/mL while the heat-stable immunoglobulin survives. Recommended whenever endogenous or infused factor VIII is present in the sample — so it applies at presentation in acquired haemophilia (residual endogenous FVIII, as here) as well as after factor VIII concentrate or recombinant porcine factor VIII.

Explained step by step

The puzzle. With a strong inhibitor (12 NBU/mL), you might expect no factor VIII at all. Yet 3 IU/dL is still measured. The answer lies in the antibody's kinetics — how it acts over time.

Type II inhibitors — acquired haemophilia. Autoantibodies in acquired haemophilia show complex, non-linear kinetics. They inactivate factor VIII rapidly at first, then more slowly. Even at maximum antibody concentration they cannot completely inactivate factor VIII. So measurable residual factor VIII coexists with a high titre.

Type I inhibitors — congenital haemophilia. Alloantibodies in congenital severe haemophilia A show second-order inactivation kinetics and complete inactivation of factor VIII. Some textbooks, including Practical Haemostasis, swap the labels and call type I "first-order"; the original description by Gawryl and Hoyer gives type I as second-order and type II as complex.

Why heat the plasma? The Bethesda assay measures how much the patient's plasma neutralises factor VIII in normal plasma. Residual factor VIII already in the test plasma distorts this. Heating at 56 °C for 30 minutes, then centrifuging, reduces factor VIII:C and antigen to <0.10 IU/mL. The antibody is an immunoglobulin, which is heat-stable, so it stays intact.

When is it needed? Whenever factor VIII is present in the test plasma: the patient's own residual factor VIII at presentation (as here, 3 IU/dL), or infused factor VIII concentrate or recombinant porcine factor VIII during treatment.

Think of it like this
A type I antibody is like a sponge that soaks up every drop of spilt water. A type II antibody is like a sponge that saturates quickly: however big it is, some water is always left on the table.
Common mistake
Assuming that measurable factor VIII means a weak inhibitor, or applying type I (complete inactivation) kinetics to acquired haemophilia.
Remember
Type I takes it all; type II always leaves some behind.

What earns the marks

  • Type II inhibitor: complex, non-linear kinetics (acquired haemophilia)
  • Rapid then slower inactivation, never complete — residual factor VIII with a high titre
  • Contrast type I: second-order kinetics, complete inactivation (alloantibodies, congenital severe haemophilia A)
  • Heat 56 °C for 30 minutes and centrifuge to remove residual factor VIII; antibody heat-stable; needed after factor VIII concentrate or recombinant porcine factor VIII
Evidence[10]Collins 2013[22]Gawryl 1982Practical Haemostasis — Inhibitor Assays (Nijmegen modification, type I/II kinetics, heat treatment)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Why residual FVIII survives, and why the titre is only an estimate

Gawryl and Hoyer offered a mechanism for type II behaviour: von Willebrand factor partly shields the epitopes these autoantibodies target. When FVIII was separated from VWF, most type II antibodies inactivated it like type I antibodies [S1].

The practical consequence is in the Bethesda assay. Because type II antibodies follow complex, non-linear kinetics, residual FVIII persists despite a large amount of inhibitor, and the titre may be underestimated [S2].

Exam point: a titre of 12 NBU/mL in AHA is a rough guide to potency, not a precise measure, and it does not predict bleeding.

  1. [S1] Gawryl MS, Hoyer LW. Inactivation of factor VIII coagulant activity by two different types of human antibodies. Blood 1982;60:1103-1109 PMID 6181833three of four type II antibodies had inactivating properties like type I antibodies when they were tested with separated VIII:C instead of plasma.
  2. [S2] Percy C, et al. Br J Haematol 2026;208:67-82 PMID 41243450 · doi:10.1111/bjh.70200Acquired clotting factor inhibitors (autoantibodies) usually present with complex (type II) kinetics, where residual FVIII activity remains in the presence of a large amount of inhibitor.
Q5
She is actively bleeding. Set out the management, naming specific agents for haemostasis and for antibody eradication, and state what else must be investigated.
4 marks

Model answer

Haemostasis first: recombinant activated factor VII (rFVIIa), activated prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC) or recombinant porcine factor VIII; the anti-human titre and factor VIII level do not decide whether to treat (the anti-porcine titre guides use of recombinant porcine factor VIII), and human factor VIII concentrate or desmopressin should not be used unless no other haemostatic option is available. Eradicate the antibody in parallel: corticosteroids alone, or with cyclophosphamide, or with rituximab (median remission about five weeks). Avoid intramuscular injections, arterial punctures and unnecessary procedures. Search for an associated disorder; discuss with a haemophilia centre; preserve pre-treatment samples.

Explained step by step

Step 1 — stop the bleeding. Give a bypassing agent — recombinant activated factor VII (rFVIIa) or activated prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC) — or recombinant porcine factor VIII. The anti-human inhibitor titre and factor VIII level do not determine whether to treat; the anti-porcine titre guides whether recombinant porcine factor VIII is suitable. Human factor VIII concentrate is much less effective than bypassing agents and should be used only if no other option exists.

Step 2 — remove the antibody, at the same time. Start immunosuppression in parallel, not afterwards: corticosteroids alone, or with cyclophosphamide, or with rituximab. The median time to remission is about five weeks, with wide variation. Factor VIII activity at presentation, inhibitor titre and autoantibody isotype predict remission and survival.

Step 3 — avoid causing new bleeds. No intramuscular injections, no arterial punctures and no unnecessary invasive procedures.

Step 4 — look for a cause. Roughly half of cases are idiopathic. The rest are associated with malignancy, autoimmune disease, the post-partum period, infection and, rarely, drugs. Discuss with a haemophilia centre and preserve pre-treatment samples.

Safety point. About 10% of patients with acquired haemophilia A are not bleeding at diagnosis. An unexplained isolated prolonged APTT must never be ignored before an invasive procedure.

Think of it like this
A house fire started by an arsonist: you put out the fire (haemostatic agents) and catch the arsonist (immunosuppression) at the same time — then ask why the arsonist appeared (the underlying disorder).
Common mistake
Choosing treatment by titre, as in congenital haemophilia, or waiting for bleeding to stop before starting immunosuppression.
Remember
Bypass, suppress, search — all at once.

What earns the marks

  • Haemostasis: rFVIIa, aPCC or recombinant porcine factor VIII; titre does not decide; factor VIII concentrate generally ineffective
  • Immunosuppression in parallel: corticosteroids alone, or with cyclophosphamide or rituximab
  • Avoid intramuscular injections, arterial punctures and unnecessary invasive procedures
  • Search for associated disorder; haemophilia centre; preserve pre-treatment samples
Evidence[1]Tiede 2020[10]Collins 2013
Evidence check verified against the sources below
UK 2026 guidance: match immunosuppression to the baseline numbers

The 2026 BSH/UKHCDO guideline does not leave first-line immunosuppression to preference. It stratifies by baseline factor VIII and Bethesda titre [S1][S2]:

  • FVIII 1 iu/dL or more and titre 20 BU/mL or less: consider prednisolone alone [S1].
  • FVIII below 1 iu/dL or titre above 20 BU/mL: consider prednisolone with oral cyclophosphamide [S2].

This patient (FVIII 3 IU/dL, 12 NBU/mL) falls in the first group. The same guideline suggests considering emicizumab to prevent bleeding, especially when immunosuppression must be delayed or is unsuitable [S3]. A strong candidate should name both.

  1. [S1] Percy C, Riddell A, Talks K, et al. Laboratory investigation and clinical management of acquired coagulation factor inhibitors: a joint BSH and UKHCDO guideline. Br J Haematol 2026;208:67-82 PMID 41243450 · doi:10.1111/bjh.70200Consider using prednisolone 1 mg/kg/day alone if the factor VIII activity is 1 iu/dL or more and the Bethesda titre is 20 BU/mL or less (2B).
  2. [S2] Percy C, et al. Br J Haematol 2026;208:67-82 PMID 41243450 · doi:10.1111/bjh.70200Consider using prednisolone combined with cyclophosphamide 1–2 mg/kg/day orally in those with a factor VIII activity less than 1 iu/dL or Bethesda titre more than 20 BU/mL (2B).
  3. [S3] Percy C, et al. Br J Haematol 2026;208:67-82 PMID 41243450 · doi:10.1111/bjh.70200Consider emicizumab for preventing bleeding in AHA, particularly when treatment with immunosuppression has to be delayed or is not feasible due to comorbidities (2B).

Take home from this station

  1. An isolated prolonged APTT with new bleeding in an older person should make you think of acquired haemophilia A.
  2. A low chromogenic factor VIII and a negative dRVVT exclude a lupus anticoagulant as the cause of a low factor VIII.
  3. Factor VIII autoantibodies are slow and incomplete: the incubated mix re-prolongs, dilutions stay parallel, and residual factor VIII persists despite a high titre.
  4. Control bleeding with bypassing agents or recombinant porcine factor VIII, and start immunosuppression at the same time.
03
Station 03 · 20 marks

A new lot of thromboplastin

What this station tests: A wrong ISI makes every INR wrong in the same direction; internal quality control cannot see an error that the controls share, but external quality assessment can.

The case

A district laboratory changes to a new lot of thromboplastin. The new reagent has an ISI of 1.35; the previous lot had an ISI of 1.05. The laboratory information system was not updated and continues to apply 1.05. The geometric mean normal prothrombin time is 12.4 s. A patient on warfarin for atrial fibrillation, target INR 2.0–3.0, has a prothrombin time of 31.0 s. Four weeks later the laboratory receives its UK NEQAS return.

In plain wordsA laboratory has started a new lot (batch) of thromboplastin, the reagent for the prothrombin time (PT). The new lot has an international sensitivity index (ISI) of 1.35, but the laboratory information system still uses the old value of 1.05. A patient on warfarin for atrial fibrillation, with a target international normalised ratio (INR) of 2.0–3.0, has a PT of 31.0 s. Four weeks later the UK NEQAS (UK National External Quality Assessment Scheme) results arrive.
UK NEQAS Blood Coagulation · summary of last three exercises
AnalyteExercise 1Exercise 2Exercise 3
INR — deviation from reagent-group median−21%−19%−23%
Factor VIII assay — gradeDeE

How to read the data

  • Patient PT 31.0 s and geometric mean normal PT 12.4 s are the two numbers needed for the PT ratio.
  • The ISI in use (1.05) is not the ISI of the reagent (1.35). The INR depends on the ISI, so every INR is affected.
  • INR deviation from the reagent-group median is −21%, −19% and −23%: all in the same (negative) direction and all beyond 15%. This is a consistent bias.
  • Factor VIII grades D, e, E: poor and worsening. Capital and small letters denote opposite directions, so this is scatter (imprecision), a separate problem from the INR fault.

The five questions

Q1
Calculate the INR as reported by the laboratory, and the INR that should have been reported. Show your working to two decimal places. Use INR = (patient prothrombin time ÷ geometric mean normal prothrombin time)^ISI.
4 marks

Model answer

PT ratio = 31.0 / 12.4 = 2.50. Reported INR (ISI 1.05) = 2.50^1.05 = 2.62. Correct INR (ISI 1.35) = 2.50^1.35 = 3.45. The laboratory reported 2.62; the true value is 3.45.

Explained step by step

PT ratio = 31.0 / 12.4 = 2.50
INR (reported, ISI 1.05) = 2.50^1.05 = 2.62
INR (correct, ISI 1.35) = 2.50^1.35 = 3.45
The laboratory reports 2.62; the true value is 3.45.

What the INR does. Different thromboplastins give different PTs for the same blood. The INR corrects for this so results can be compared. It uses two numbers: the patient's PT ratio and the reagent's ISI.

Step 1 — the PT ratio. Divide the patient's PT by the geometric mean normal PT: 31.0 ÷ 12.4 = 2.50.

Step 2 — raise the ratio to the power of the ISI. With the ISI the system used (1.05): 2.50^1.05 = 2.62. With the reagent's true ISI (1.35): 2.50^1.35 = 3.45.

Step 3 — compare. The laboratory reported 2.62, but the true INR is 3.45. The PT itself was measured correctly; only the conversion step was wrong.

Think of it like this
Converting money with an out-of-date exchange rate: the cash in your hand (the PT) is right, but the conversion factor (the ISI) is wrong, so the final figure (the INR) is wrong.
Common mistake
Multiplying the PT ratio by the ISI instead of raising it to the power of the ISI.
Remember
Ratio first, then the power of the ISI.

What earns the marks

  • PT ratio 31.0 / 12.4 = 2.50
  • Reported INR 2.50^1.05 = 2.62
  • Correct INR 2.50^1.35 = 3.45
  • Working shown to two decimal places
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — ISI & INR (calculation)
Q2
In which direction is this patient placed at risk, and by what sequence of events does the error cause harm?
4 marks

Model answer

Under-reported, so at risk of haemorrhage. The reported 2.62 sits mid-range, so the clinician maintains the warfarin dose, while the true INR is 3.45 — above the atrial fibrillation target. The error is systematic and affects every warfarin patient the laboratory serves: population-wide exposure. A high-ISI reagent is less sensitive, so a given PT change produces a much larger INR change; ISI errors are amplified at higher ratios.

Explained step by step

Direction. The INR is under-reported (2.62 instead of 3.45). The patient is more anticoagulated than the clinician believes, so the risk is haemorrhage.

The sequence of harm.

  • The report says 2.62 — comfortably within the 2.0–3.0 target.
  • The clinician sees no reason to reduce warfarin, and keeps the same dose.
  • The true INR is 3.45, above the target range for atrial fibrillation.
  • The patient stays over-anticoagulated and at risk of bleeding.

Not just one patient. The error is systematic: the same wrong ISI is applied to every INR. Every warfarin patient the laboratory serves is affected, so the exposure is population-wide, not individual.

Why it is worse at higher ratios. A high-ISI reagent is inherently less sensitive. With an insensitive thromboplastin, a given change in PT produces a much larger change in INR. So ISI errors are amplified at higher PT ratios — the further the PT ratio rises, the bigger the gap between the reported and the true INR.

Think of it like this
A speedometer that reads low: the driver thinks the speed is safe and keeps pressing the accelerator — and the faster the car goes, the bigger the gap between the dial and the truth.
Common mistake
Treating it as a single-patient problem and missing that every INR issued since the lot change is wrong.
Remember
One wrong ISI makes every INR wrong.

What earns the marks

  • Under-reported INR: risk of haemorrhage
  • Reported 2.62 looks in range, so dose maintained; true 3.45 is above target
  • Systematic error: every warfarin patient, population-wide exposure
  • High ISI = less sensitive reagent; ISI errors amplified at higher ratios
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — ISI & INR (ISI sensitivity)
Q3
Define accuracy, precision, bias and coefficient of variation, and state which of these the laboratory's internal quality control has failed to detect.
4 marks

Model answer

Accuracy: closeness of the observed value to the true value. Precision: closeness of repeated measurements of one sample. Bias: systematic displacement from the true value (an assay can be precise yet inaccurate). Coefficient of variation: (SD ÷ mean) × 100, as a percentage — the standard measure of precision; high = poor reproducibility. The failure is of accuracy, through negative bias. Internal quality control missed it because controls pass through the same wrong ISI, so results stay precise and the Levey–Jennings chart stays within limits.

Explained step by step

The four definitions.

  • Accuracy — how close the observed value is to the true value.
  • Precision — how close repeated measurements of a single sample are to each other.
  • Bias — a systematic displacement from the true value. An assay may be precise yet inaccurate.
  • Coefficient of variation (CV) — (standard deviation (SD) ÷ mean) × 100, expressed as a percentage. It is the standard measure of precision; a high CV means poor reproducibility.

What went wrong here. The failure is one of accuracy, caused by negative bias: every INR is pushed downwards by the same mistake.

Why internal quality control (IQC) missed it. The control material is processed through the same wrong ISI as the patients. So the controls are wrong in exactly the same way every day. Results stay precise and internally consistent, and the Levey–Jennings chart (the running plot of control results) stays within limits.

The key idea. IQC answers “are my results today the same as yesterday?” It cannot answer “are they correct?” Only external quality assessment (EQA) can expose a shared systematic error, because it asks a different question: “are my results the same as everyone else's?”

Think of it like this
Darts: precision is how tightly your darts cluster; accuracy is how close they land to the bullseye. This laboratory throws a tight cluster in the wrong place. Watching only your own board (IQC) will not show it; comparing with other players (EQA) will.
Common mistake
Saying internal quality control “should have caught it”. The controls run through the same wrong ISI, so they cannot.
Remember
IQC: same as yesterday? EQA: same as everyone else?

What earns the marks

  • Accuracy and precision correctly defined
  • Bias = systematic displacement; CV = (SD ÷ mean) × 100%
  • Failure is accuracy, through negative bias
  • IQC shares the wrong ISI and stays in limits; only EQA detects a shared systematic error
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — Introduction to Quality Assurance (accuracy/precision/bias/CV)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Could internal QC ever catch a wrong ISI?

The station's logic holds for the usual set-up: control material is run every batch to check precision [S1], and if its target ranges were re-set after the lot change using the wrong ISI, the controls drift with the patients and stay in limits.

The trap is to say IQC can never detect it. Local calibration uses plasmas with known INRs tested on the laboratory's own reagent and coagulometer [S2]; certified plasmas of this kind, or controls with externally assigned INR values, would expose the error.

EQA remains the safety net because it tests comparability with peer laboratories [S3], which is exactly what a shared systematic error fails.

  1. [S1] Baker P, Platton S, Gibson C, et al. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis. Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776should be included in each assay run or sample batch to ensure adequate assay precision and safeguard against reporting invalid results.
  2. [S2] Practical Haemostasis. ISI & INR: Local Calibration (single-author teaching site) This involves testing a set of plasma samples with known INRs with the a laboratory-specific Thromboplastin and on the coagulometers
  3. [S3] Baker P, et al. Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776Clinical laboratories should participate in accredited external quality assurance (EQA) schemes for each analyte to monitor performance and assess comparability with peer group laboratories.
Q4
Define "outwith consensus" and "persistently outwith consensus" for the INR in this scheme. Why is percentage deviation not used to grade factor assays, and what does the return D / e / E signify?
4 marks

Model answer

Outwith consensus (PT, INR, APTT): deviation of more than 15% from the reagent-group median where there are ten or more users, or from the overall median where fewer. Persistently outwith consensus: more than 15% on three consecutive occasions — this laboratory (−21%, −19%, −23%) qualifies. The head of department receives a letter from the UK NEQAS Director offering advice; continued persistent poor performance is referred to the National Quality Assurance Advisory Panel. Factor assays spread too widely (<1 to >150 IU/dL), so results are ranked into unequal quantiles: A/a nearest 25%, then B/b, C/c, D/d, E/e furthest 5% in each direction. D / e / E is itself 'persistently outwith consensus' for a factor assay (two consecutive outwith-consensus pairs across three exercises); the capital and small letters point in opposite directions, suggesting scatter (imprecision) rather than consistent bias.

Explained step by step

Outwith consensus (“outwith” means “outside”). For PT, INR and activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT), a result is outwith consensus if it deviates by more than 15% from the reagent-group median when there are ten or more users, or from the overall median when there are fewer.

Persistently outwith consensus means a deviation of more than 15% on three consecutive occasions. This laboratory scored −21%, −19% and −23%. It is persistently outwith consensus: the head of department receives a letter from the UK NEQAS Director offering advice, and continued poor performance is referred to the National Quality Assurance Advisory Panel (NQAAP).

Why not percentages for factor assays? The spread of results is far too wide: one factor VIII exercise may legitimately span <1 to >150 IU/dL. Instead, results are ranked into unequal quantiles around the consensus median: A/a the nearest 25% above or below, then B/b, C/c, D/d, and E/e the furthest 5% in each direction.

What D / e / E means. Poor and deteriorating performance. E and e denote opposite directions of error, so the laboratory is scattering around the median rather than showing a consistent bias. That points to imprecision in the factor assay — a different problem from the INR fault.

Think of it like this
When finishing times in a race vary enormously, you grade runners by position band — front, middle, back — rather than by a fixed percentage of the winner's time.
Common mistake
Reading D / e / E as a consistent bias like the INR fault. Capital and small letters point in opposite directions, so this is scatter.
Remember
INR: more than 15%, three in a row. Factors: letters, not percentages.

What earns the marks

  • Outwith consensus: more than 15% from reagent-group median (ten or more users) or overall median
  • Persistently: more than 15% on three consecutive occasions; this laboratory qualifies; Director's letter first, then the National Quality Assurance Advisory Panel if poor performance continues
  • Factor assays: spread too wide, so quantile grading A/a (nearest 25%) to E/e (furthest 5%)
  • D / e / E: persistently outwith consensus for a factor assay; opposite directions suggest imprecision, not bias
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — Introduction to Quality Assurance (UK NEQAS consensus definitions, factor-assay quantile grading)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Reading UK NEQAS factor-assay grades

Factor-assay grades rank each laboratory against the overall median rather than a percentage limit. Capital letters (A–E) lie above the median and small letters (a–e) below it [S1].

Two consequences candidates often miss:

  • Because the grades are quantiles, a fixed share of laboratories receives E/e in every exercise, whatever their absolute performance [S1].
  • For factor assays, outwith consensus is a pair of poor grades in two consecutive surveys, and persistently outwith consensus is two such pairs across three exercises — D/e/E is the textbook example [S2].

This description comes from a single-author teaching source; check it against the current UK NEQAS BC participant handbook before the station is used.

  1. [S1] Practical Haemostasis. Introduction to Quality Assurance (single-author teaching site) Finally because of this statistical approach 10% of labs in any test will get an 'E/e' grade.
  2. [S2] Practical Haemostasis. Introduction to Quality Assurance (single-author teaching site) Persistently outwith consensus is defined as TWO consecutive “outwith consensus” performances e.g. D/D/D, e/c/e, D/e/E - from three consecutive performances.
Q5
State the post-analytical and governance actions now required.
4 marks

Model answer

Quarantine the incorrect ISI and correct the laboratory information system; verify against the manufacturer's insert for the reagent–coagulometer combination and perform local calibration. Find the lot-change date and every INR issued since; retrospective clinical risk assessment with the anticoagulation service; recall affected patients; issue amended reports. Raise a formal incident; notify the laboratory director and accreditation body per local policy; add lot-change verification (ISI confirmation, parallel testing across old and new lots) to the standard operating procedure; report the outcome to the EQA scheme.

Explained step by step

1. Fix the system. Quarantine the incorrect ISI and correct the laboratory information system. Verify the ISI against the manufacturer's insert for the specific reagent–coagulometer combination, and perform local calibration. The instrument and its end-point detection affect the PT independently of the reagent.

2. Find the patients. Determine the date the lot changed and identify every INR issued since. With the anticoagulation service, carry out a retrospective clinical risk assessment, recall patients whose management may have been affected, and issue amended reports.

3. Governance. Raise a formal incident. Notify the laboratory director and the accreditation body as local policy requires.

4. Prevent it happening again. Add a lot-change verification step to the standard operating procedure (SOP), including ISI confirmation and parallel testing of patient samples across the old and new lots. Report the outcome back to the EQA scheme.

Think of it like this
Like a product recall: fix the factory line, trace every item sold since the fault began, contact the customers, and add a new check to the factory routine.
Common mistake
Correcting the ISI for future samples but not looking back at every INR issued since the lot changed.
Remember
Fix, find, file, prevent.

What earns the marks

  • Quarantine wrong ISI, correct the information system, verify with manufacturer's insert, local calibration
  • Identify every INR since the lot change; risk assessment with anticoagulation service; recall; amended reports
  • Formal incident; notify laboratory director and accreditation body
  • SOP change: ISI confirmation and parallel testing at lot change; report back to EQA
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — ISI & INR (local calibration); Practical Haemostasis — Introduction to Quality Assurance

Take home from this station

  1. The INR is the PT ratio raised to the power of the ISI, so a wrong ISI makes every INR wrong.
  2. Using too low an ISI under-reports the INR and exposes every warfarin patient to a risk of haemorrhage.
  3. Internal quality control checks consistency, not truth; only external quality assessment detects a shared systematic error.
  4. Every new reagent lot needs ISI confirmation and parallel patient testing before release.
04
Station 04 · 20 marks

Two assays, two different diagnoses

What this station tests: When the one-stage and chromogenic factor VIII results disagree, the chromogenic result matches the bleeding — and a normal von Willebrand screen does not exclude type 2N von Willebrand disease.

The case

A 22-year-old man is referred after prolonged bleeding requiring re-suturing following dental extraction. He bled for four days after tonsillectomy aged eight. A maternal uncle has a similar history. His activated partial thromboplastin time is only marginally prolonged and would have been reported as normal by many laboratories.

In plain wordsA 22-year-old man needed re-suturing for bleeding after a dental extraction, and bled for four days after a tonsillectomy at age eight. His maternal uncle has a similar history. His activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) is only slightly long. One factor VIII test gives 42 IU/dL, but another gives only 18 IU/dL.
Bleeding-disorder panel
TestResultReference
Prothrombin time12.1 s11–14 s
Activated partial thromboplastin time39 s27–35 s
Factor VIII — one-stage clotting42 IU/dL50–150 IU/dL
Factor VIII — chromogenic18 IU/dL50–150 IU/dL
von Willebrand factor antigen88 IU/dL50–150 IU/dL
von Willebrand factor activity (GPIbM)82 IU/dL50–150 IU/dL
Activity / antigen ratio0.93>0.7
Factor IX, XINormal—

How to read the data

  • Prothrombin time (PT) 12.1 s is normal; the APTT of 39 s is only marginally prolonged and many laboratories would have called it normal.
  • Factor VIII by one-stage clotting assay is 42 IU/dL, but by chromogenic assay only 18 IU/dL — a greater than twofold difference. This discrepancy is the centre of the station.
  • Von Willebrand factor (VWF) antigen 88 IU/dL and VWF activity (GPIbM assay, a measure of its platelet-binding function) 82 IU/dL are normal, with an activity/antigen ratio of 0.93 (above 0.7). VWF quantity and platelet-binding function are normal.
  • Factors IX and XI are normal, so the problem is specific to factor VIII.
  • The history — bleeding after two haemostatic challenges and an affected maternal uncle, consistent with X-linked inheritance — supports the lower, chromogenic value.

The five questions

Q1
Name the phenomenon demonstrated by the two factor VIII results, and state which assay correlates better with the bleeding phenotype.
4 marks

Model answer

Discrepant factor VIII: one-stage 42 IU/dL versus chromogenic 18 IU/dL, a greater than twofold difference. In this pattern the chromogenic (or two-stage) assay correlates better with the bleeding phenotype, and management must be based on it. The history — bleeding after two haemostatic challenges and an affected maternal uncle (X-linked) — supports the lower value.

Explained step by step

Name it. This is a factor VIII assay discrepancy (discrepant factor VIII). The same plasma gives 42 IU/dL by the one-stage clotting assay and 18 IU/dL by the chromogenic assay — more than a twofold difference.

Which one to trust? In this pattern, the chromogenic (or two-stage) assay correlates better with the bleeding phenotype — that is, with how much the patient actually bleeds. Management must be based on the chromogenic result.

Check it against the patient. He bled significantly after two haemostatic challenges (tonsillectomy and dental extraction). His maternal uncle is similarly affected, consistent with X-linked inheritance. This history fits the lower, chromogenic value much better than a near-normal one.

Think of it like this
Two thermometers disagree about a child's fever. You trust the one that matches how ill the child actually looks — here, the chromogenic assay matches the bleeding.
Common mistake
Averaging the two results, or trusting the one-stage result because it is the routine test.
Remember
When they disagree, the chromogenic tells the truth about bleeding.

What earns the marks

  • Names discrepant factor VIII (assay discrepancy)
  • Quotes 42 versus 18 IU/dL, greater than twofold
  • Chromogenic (two-stage) correlates better with bleeding phenotype; use it for management
  • History (two bleeds, maternal uncle, X-linked) supports the lower value
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays; Practical Haemostasis — Chromogenic Factor Assays
Q2
Explain the molecular basis of this discrepancy.
4 marks

Model answer

Certain F8 missense mutations, characteristically at the A1–A2 and A2–A3 domain interfaces, give factor VIII that activates normally but whose activated form (FVIIIa) decays abnormally fast through premature A2-subunit dissociation. The one-stage assay is a short single-step clotting reaction that samples activity early, before decay, so it over-reads. The chromogenic assay has a longer two-stage incubation with defined factor IXa, factor X and phospholipid, during which the unstable FVIIIa decays — so it reports the lower, physiologically relevant activity.

Explained step by step

The faulty protein. Certain missense mutations in the F8 gene (single-letter changes that swap one amino acid) sit characteristically at the A1–A2 and A2–A3 domain interfaces — the places where parts of the factor VIII molecule meet.

What goes wrong. The factor VIII is activated normally. But the activated form (factor VIIIa) falls apart too quickly: its A2 subunit dissociates prematurely, so its activity decays abnormally fast.

Why the one-stage assay over-reads. The one-stage assay is a short, single-step clotting reaction. It samples factor VIII activity early, before the decay is apparent. So it reports a higher value.

Why the chromogenic assay reads lower. The chromogenic assay uses a longer, two-stage incubation with defined factor IXa, factor X and phospholipid. During this time the unstable factor VIIIa decays. So it reports the lower activity — the one that reflects what happens in the body.

Think of it like this
A firework that lights normally but burns out in seconds. A photo taken the instant it is lit (one-stage) shows a bright flash; a photo taken a little later (chromogenic) shows how little light is really left.
Common mistake
Blaming an inhibitor or a sample problem instead of an unstable factor VIIIa molecule.
Remember
A2 falls off; the quick test misses it, the slow test catches it.

What earns the marks

  • F8 missense mutations at the A1–A2 and A2–A3 domain interfaces
  • Normal activation, but FVIIIa decays fast through premature A2-subunit dissociation
  • One-stage: short, single-step, measures early, before decay — over-reads
  • Chromogenic: longer two-stage incubation (factor IXa, factor X, phospholipid) — decay occurs, lower relevant value
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — Chromogenic Factor Assays; Practical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays
Evidence check verified against the sources below
The A2 subunit: why incubation time changes the answer

Activated factor VIII is a three-part molecule in which the A2 subunit is held only by a weak ionic interaction; when A2 dissociates, FVIIIa is inactivated [S1]. Discrepant mutations weaken that interface, so A2 falls away faster [S1].

Time is the variable. The chromogenic and two-stage assays incubate longer, allowing more A2 dissociation, so they read lower [S2]. The proof is experimental: prolonging the incubation in the one-stage assay lowers its result too [S2].

Viva line: “The one-stage assay reads the molecule before it falls apart; the chromogenic assay reads it after.”

  1. [S1] Pipe SW, Eickhorst AN, McKinley SH, et al. Hemophilia A mutations associated with 1-stage/2-stage activity discrepancy disrupt protein-protein interactions within the triplicated A domains of thrombin-activated factor VIIIa. Blood 2001;97:685-691 PMID 11157485 · doi:10.1182/blood.v97.3.685Thrombin-activated factor VIII (FVIIIa) is a heterotrimer with the A2 subunit (amino acid residues 373-740) in a weak ionic interaction with the A1 and A3-C1-C2 subunits.
  2. [S2] Kemball-Cook G, Gomez K. Molecular basis of hemophilia A. In: Textbook of Hemophilia, 3rd ed. Wiley; 2014, ch. 3 Prolonging the incubation time in the one-stage assay leads to lower levels, confirming that this is the likely mechanism.
Q3
The von Willebrand screen is normal. Explain why this does not exclude von Willebrand disease in this patient, and name the single test that must now be requested.
4 marks

Model answer

The screen measures VWF quantity and platelet-binding function, both normal in type 2N VWD. In type 2N the defect is in the D′–D3 factor VIII-binding region: VWF antigen, activity and ratio are normal, but factor VIII is not protected from clearance, so factor VIII alone is low. Autosomal recessive; phenocopies mild haemophilia A. Request the VWF–factor VIII binding assay (VWF:FVIIIB), with genetic analysis in parallel (VWF for type 2N; F8 for haemophilia A), because VWF:FVIIIB has no international standard or accredited UK/European EQA. Type 2N needs VWF-containing concentrate, not recombinant factor VIII.

Explained step by step

What the screen checks. The standard VWF screen measures how much VWF there is (antigen) and how well it binds platelets (activity). Both are normal here, and so is their ratio.

What it misses. VWF also holds on to factor VIII and protects it from clearance. In type 2N von Willebrand disease, the defect lies in the D′–D3 region, the part of VWF that binds factor VIII. VWF antigen, activity and ratio are all normal, but factor VIII is not protected, so factor VIII alone is reduced.

Why it fools us. Type 2N is autosomal recessive, but it phenocopies (looks just like) mild haemophilia A in the laboratory.

The test to request. The von Willebrand factor–factor VIII binding assay (VWF:FVIIIB). If it is normal, proceed to F8 sequencing.

Why it matters. The two diagnoses have different inheritance and different treatment. Type 2N responds to VWF-containing concentrate, not to recombinant factor VIII, which is cleared rapidly. Getting this wrong produces a patient who seems refractory to correctly dosed factor VIII.

Think of it like this
VWF is like a bodyguard with two arms: one holds on to platelets, the other escorts factor VIII. In type 2N the bodyguard is present and strong (normal screen), but the arm that holds factor VIII does not grip — so factor VIII is lost.
Common mistake
Accepting a normal VWF antigen, activity and ratio as excluding all von Willebrand disease.
Remember
Low factor VIII with normal VWF: think 2N, test binding.

What earns the marks

  • Screen tests VWF quantity and platelet binding — both normal in type 2N
  • Type 2N: D′–D3 factor VIII-binding defect; factor VIII not protected, so low alone
  • Autosomal recessive; phenocopies mild haemophilia A
  • Request VWF:FVIIIB (VWF–factor VIII binding assay) with genetic analysis (VWF and F8) in parallel
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays (von Willebrand factor checks in unexpected factor VIII deficiency)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Working up type 2N in the UK

The FVIII:C/VWF:Ag ratio here is clearly reduced, which is the UK trigger for genetic testing for type 2N [S1]. The binding assay is useful but limited: there is no international standard and no accredited UK or European EQA scheme for VWF:FVIIIB, so genetic testing runs in parallel unless results are clearly normal [S2].

Interpreting VWF:FVIIIB: homozygotes and compound heterozygotes usually have a ratio below 0.3, while some heterozygotes sit between 0.3 and 0.7 [S2].

The affected maternal uncle favours X-linked haemophilia A, but does not remove the need to exclude 2N, because the two differ in inheritance and in the concentrate required.

  1. [S1] Platton S, Baker P, Bowyer A, et al. Guideline for laboratory diagnosis and monitoring of von Willebrand disease: a joint guideline from UKHCDO and BSH. Br J Haematol 2024;204:1714-1731 PMID 38532595 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19385Genetic testing for suspected type 2N VWD is recommended in individuals with a newly identified reduced FVIII:C/VWF:Ag ratio (1B).
  2. [S2] Platton S, et al. Br J Haematol 2024;204:1714-1731 PMID 38532595 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19385Homozygotes and compound heterozygotes are likely to have a VWF:FVIIIB ratio of &lt;0.3, whereas some heterozygous individuals may have a ratio of 0.3–0.7.
Q4
Had only the one-stage assay been performed, what would the report have concluded, and what specific harm follows?
4 marks

Model answer

“Factor VIII 42 IU/dL — mild reduction, borderline”, or dismissed altogether given the near-normal APTT; the patient classified as having a trivial or no bleeding disorder. Harm: inadequate or absent haemostatic cover for surgery, dental extraction or trauma in a man whose true factor VIII is 18 IU/dL and who has already bled significantly twice. Whenever non-severe haemophilia A is a possibility, a chromogenic factor VIII assay should be performed in addition to the one-stage assay, to detect all cases and assess severity correctly.

Explained step by step

What the report would have said. “Factor VIII 42 IU/dL — mild reduction, borderline.” In many laboratories it might have been dismissed altogether, because the APTT was nearly normal.

The resulting label. The patient would be classified as having a trivial bleeding disorder, or none at all.

The concrete harm. He would receive inadequate or no haemostatic cover for surgery, dental extraction or trauma. Yet his true factor VIII is 18 IU/dL, and he has already bled significantly twice.

The rule that prevents it. Practical Haemostasis is explicit: every individual with a suspected bleeding disorder should have both a one-stage and a chromogenic factor VIII assay, precisely to avoid this misclassification.

Think of it like this
Like checking only the fuel gauge that sticks on half-full: you set off on a long journey without refuelling, and run out on the motorway.
Common mistake
Describing the harm vaguely (“missed diagnosis”) instead of naming the concrete consequence: no haemostatic cover for the next challenge.
Remember
One assay can miss it — always pair one-stage with chromogenic.

What earns the marks

  • Report: factor VIII 42 IU/dL, mild/borderline — or dismissed due to near-normal APTT
  • Classified as trivial or no bleeding disorder
  • Harm: inadequate or absent cover for surgery, dental extraction or trauma (true factor VIII 18 IU/dL)
  • Both one-stage and chromogenic assays needed in every suspected bleeding disorder
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays (“What Test Next” — mandatory paired one-stage and chromogenic assays)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Discrepancy runs both ways

In a single-centre series of mild haemophilia A, about a third of patients had a twofold or greater discrepancy between methods [S1]. Importantly, the inverse pattern (one-stage lower than two-stage) was more frequent than the classical pattern in that series [S1].

The practical risk is missed diagnosis: some patients would not have been diagnosed on the one-stage assay alone [S1], and BSH notes up to 10% of non-severe haemophilia A has a normal APTT and normal one-stage FVIII [S2].

Exam trap: “chromogenic is always right” is too simple. Interpret the discrepancy against the bleeding history, as this station does.

  1. [S1] Bowyer AE, Van Veen JJ, Goodeve AC, Kitchen S, Makris M. Specific and global coagulation assays in the diagnosis of discrepant mild hemophilia A. Haematologica 2013;98:1980-1987 PMID 23812942 · doi:10.3324/haematol.2013.088088Assay discrepancy was observed in 31% of individuals; 12% with lower activity in the two-stage assay and 19% with lower activity in the one-stage assay.
  2. [S2] Baker P, Platton S, Gibson C, et al. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis. Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776Up to 10% of non-severe haemophilia A cases have a normal APTT and normal FVIII by OSCA, but a reduced FVIII by CA.
Q5
Write the laboratory conclusion, and state the haemostatic cover you would advise for his next dental extraction.
4 marks

Model answer

Report: one-stage 42 IU/dL, chromogenic 18 IU/dL — significant discrepancy; chromogenic correlates better with phenotype and should guide management; consistent with mild haemophilia A of discrepant type; type 2N VWD not excluded, binding assay added; genetic counselling and family studies advised. Cover: tranexamic acid (oral and/or mouthwash) started before the procedure and continued for about 7 days (7–10 days in some protocols); formal desmopressin trial in advance (factor VIII at baseline, 30–60 minutes and 4–6 hours); factor concentrate for non-responders, extensive surgical extraction or confirmed type 2N (VWF-containing product). Manage via a haemophilia centre; record the plan in patient-held documentation.

Explained step by step

The laboratory conclusion. “One-stage factor VIII 42 IU/dL, chromogenic factor VIII 18 IU/dL — a significant assay discrepancy. The chromogenic result correlates better with bleeding phenotype and should be used for management. Appearances are consistent with mild haemophilia A of discrepant type; type 2N von Willebrand disease is not excluded by the normal von Willebrand screen and a factor VIII binding assay has been added. Genetic counselling and family studies are advised.”

Cover — the foundation. Tranexamic acid, orally and/or as a mouthwash, started before the procedure and continued for about 7 days (7–10 days in some protocols).

Cover — test desmopressin first. Do a formal desmopressin trial in advance, measuring factor VIII at baseline, 30–60 minutes and 4–6 hours after the dose. The response is variable and cannot be assumed.

Cover — when to use concentrate. Reserve factor concentrate for non-responders to desmopressin, for an extensive surgical extraction, or if type 2N is confirmed — in which case a VWF-containing product is required.

Organisation. Manage through a haemophilia centre, and record the plan in his patient-held documentation.

Think of it like this
Planning a mountain hike: you always carry water (tranexamic acid), test your boots on a short walk first (desmopressin trial), and pack the heavy kit (concentrate) only if the route or the boots demand it.
