Common mistake
Assuming desmopressin will work without a formal trial, or giving recombinant factor VIII before type 2N has been excluded.
Remember
Tranexamic acid always; desmopressin if tested; concentrate if needed.

What earns the marks

  • Report states the discrepancy, uses the chromogenic value, and names mild haemophilia A of discrepant type
  • Type 2N not excluded; binding assay added; genetic counselling and family studies
  • Tranexamic acid from before, for about 7 days; formal desmopressin trial (baseline, 30–60 min, 4–6 h)
  • Concentrate for non-responders, extensive extraction or confirmed 2N (VWF-containing); haemophilia centre; patient-held plan
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays; Practical Haemostasis — Chromogenic Factor Assays

Take home from this station

  1. Every patient with a suspected bleeding disorder needs both a one-stage and a chromogenic factor VIII assay.
  2. When the one-stage result is higher than the chromogenic one, the chromogenic result matches the bleeding and should guide management.
  3. A normal VWF antigen, activity and ratio do not exclude type 2N von Willebrand disease; request the VWF–factor VIII binding assay.
  4. Plan dental cover in advance: tranexamic acid as the foundation, a formal desmopressin trial, and concentrate only when needed.
05
Station 05 · 20 marks

Gastrointestinal bleeding on dabigatran

What this station tests: One drug at a high level, dabigatran, explains every abnormal clotting result here, including a fibrinogen that looks dangerously low but is not.

The case

A 67-year-old man with chronic kidney disease, estimated glomerular filtration rate 28 mL/min/1.73 m², presents with haematemesis and melaena. He takes dabigatran 110 mg twice daily for atrial fibrillation and swallowed his morning dose two hours before admission. He is tachycardic with a haemoglobin of 71 g/L. The on-call biomedical scientist telephones to report a critically low fibrinogen.

In plain wordsA 67-year-old man with poor kidney function is vomiting blood and passing black stools. He takes dabigatran, a blood thinner that blocks thrombin, and swallowed a dose two hours before he arrived. His haemoglobin is low at 71 g/L and his heart is racing. The laboratory telephones to warn that his fibrinogen is critically low.
Urgent coagulation profile
TestResultReference
Platelet count212 ×10⁹/L150–400 ×10⁹/L
Prothrombin time15 s11–14 s
Activated partial thromboplastin time93 s27–35 s
Thrombin time>180 s12–17 s
Reptilase time17 s15–20 s
Clauss fibrinogen0.8 g/L2.0–4.0 g/L
Prothrombin-time-derived fibrinogen3.2 g/L2.0–4.0 g/L
D-dimerNormal—
Dilute thrombin time, dabigatran-calibrated240 ng/mL—

How to read the data

  • Prothrombin time (PT) 15 s and activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) 93 s are both prolonged. They show that a drug is present, but they cannot tell you how much.
  • Thrombin time (TT) is over 180 s (unmeasurable), but the reptilase time is normal at 17 s. This pair points to thrombin being blocked, not to a problem with fibrinogen itself.
  • The two fibrinogen results disagree: Clauss fibrinogen 0.8 g/L (very low) against prothrombin-time-derived fibrinogen 3.2 g/L (normal). A real low fibrinogen would be low by every method, so one result is being distorted.
  • Platelets (212 ×10⁹/L) and D-dimer are normal. These two results argue strongly against disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC).
  • The dabigatran-calibrated dilute thrombin time gives an actual drug level of 240 ng/mL. This is the only result in the table that measures the drug.
  • The estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) of 28 mL/min/1.73 m² explains why the drug has built up: dabigatran is cleared mainly by the kidneys.

The five questions

Q1
Give the single explanation that unifies this entire profile, including the discordant fibrinogen results.
4 marks

Model answer

A high circulating dabigatran concentration, accumulated because of renal impairment (cleared predominantly by the kidneys; eGFR 28), sampled near peak two hours post-dose. Dabigatran inhibits the reagent thrombin in the thrombin-dependent Clauss assay, so the clotting time lengthens and fibrinogen is read off the curve as falsely low. The PT-derived fibrinogen of 3.2 g/L disagrees sharply with the Clauss result, which points to interference, not hypofibrinogenaemia. PT-derived fibrinogen is not recommended for clinical use, so confirm with a fibrinogen antigen assay (or repeat the Clauss assay after reversal) before excluding hypofibrinogenaemia. Dabigatran is contraindicated when creatinine clearance is below 30 mL/min: recommend a Cockcroft–Gault creatinine clearance and review the anticoagulant once bleeding is controlled.

Explained step by step

Start with the drug. Dabigatran is a direct oral anticoagulant (DOAC) that blocks thrombin directly. Most of it leaves the body unchanged through the kidneys. With an eGFR of 28 mL/min/1.73 m², his kidneys cannot clear it properly, so it builds up. The blood was also taken only two hours after a dose, close to the peak level. So the drug level in this sample is high.

Now the fibrinogen puzzle. The Clauss fibrinogen test adds thrombin to the patient's plasma and times how long a clot takes to form. The analyser then turns that time into a fibrinogen value using a calibration curve: a longer time is read as less fibrinogen. Dabigatran in the sample blocks the thrombin in the reagent. The clot forms slowly, and the analyser wrongly reports a low fibrinogen.

The prothrombin-time-derived fibrinogen is worked out from how much clot forms during the PT test, not from the speed of a thrombin-triggered clot. Here it is 3.2 g/L, far from the Clauss result. That gap points to interference, not a real low fibrinogen. But the PT-derived method is not recommended for clinical decisions, so confirm with a fibrinogen antigen assay, or repeat the Clauss assay once the dabigatran has been reversed.

One cause, every result. The prolonged PT, the very long APTT, the unmeasurable TT and the falsely low Clauss fibrinogen all follow from one fact: thrombin is blocked by too much dabigatran. You do not need a second diagnosis.

The prescribing point (worth a mark). Dabigatran is contraindicated when creatinine clearance is below 30 mL/min. An eGFR of 28 mL/min/1.73 m² means it should not have been prescribed. Recommend calculating creatinine clearance by the Cockcroft–Gault formula, and review the choice of anticoagulant once the bleeding is controlled.

Think of it like this
Imagine guessing how much wood is in a pile by timing how fast it burns. If someone sprays water on your match (dabigatran on the reagent thrombin), the fire is slow and you wrongly think there is very little wood.
Common mistake
Believing the Clauss fibrinogen of 0.8 g/L and calling it true hypofibrinogenaemia, then treating it.
Remember
Thrombin-based test plus thrombin-blocking drug equals a false result.

What earns the marks

  • Single cause: high circulating dabigatran concentration
  • Accumulation from renal impairment (predominantly renal clearance, eGFR 28) and near-peak sampling two hours post-dose
  • Clauss fibrinogen falsely low because dabigatran inhibits the reagent thrombin; the discordant PT-derived fibrinogen (3.2 g/L) indicates interference, with confirmation by fibrinogen antigen or a repeat Clauss after reversal
  • Dabigatran contraindicated when creatinine clearance is below 30 mL/min; recommend Cockcroft–Gault clearance and anticoagulant review
Evidence[2]Douxfils 2021[4]Gosselin 2018Practical Haemostasis — DOACs (falsely decreased Clauss fibrinogen)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Why the PT-derived fibrinogen cannot settle the question either

The derived fibrinogen is calculated from the optical change during the PT, using a calibration curve, and results vary with the PT method [S1]. In external quality assessment it ran higher than the Clauss result, especially in warfarinised and critically ill patients and in dysfibrinogenaemia [S2]. BSH therefore states that PT-derived fibrinogen is not recommended for clinical use [S3].

In this station the discordance is still the key clue: a thrombin inhibitor can inhibit Clauss reagents, depending on their thrombin concentration [S4]. But the safe answer is not 'the derived value is true'. It is: interference is likely; confirm with a fibrinogen antigen assay (BSH recommends antigen assays to separate low from abnormal fibrinogen) [S5], or repeat the Clauss assay after idarucizumab.

  1. [S1] Baker P, Platton S, Gibson C, et al 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis. Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776If the PT is measured using a photo-optical system then a 'derived fibrinogen concentration' can be derived from a calibration curve. The results vary according to the PT method.
  2. [S2] Baker P, Platton S, Gibson C, et al 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis. Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776a finding exaggerated in warfarinised and critically ill patients and those with dysfibrinogenaemia in whom the derived fibrinogen is closer to the antigenic value and five times higher than the Clauss assay.
  3. [S3] Baker P, Platton S, Gibson C, et al 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis. Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776PT-derived fibrinogen is not recommended for clinical use.
  4. [S4] Baker P, Platton S, Gibson C, et al 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis. Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776direct thrombin inhibitors (DTI) may inhibit some Clauss assays depending on the thrombin concentration in the reagent, resulting in variable underestimation of fibrinogen concentration.
  5. [S5] Baker P, Platton S, Gibson C, et al 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis. Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776Fibrinogen antigen assays should be used to distinguish between hypofibrinogenaemia and dysfibrinogenaemia.
Q2
Interpret the thrombin time and reptilase time together. What would a normal thrombin time have told you?
4 marks

Model answer

The TT is exquisitely sensitive to direct thrombin inhibition and is unmeasurable. The reptilase time is normal because reptilase (from Bothrops atrox) cleaves fibrinopeptide A directly and is not inhibited by thrombin inhibitors or heparin. The pair localises the defect to thrombin inhibition and exonerates fibrinogen; dysfibrinogenaemia or true hypofibrinogenaemia would prolong both. A normal TT would have excluded clinically significant dabigatran.

Explained step by step

What each test does. In the thrombin time, thrombin is added to the patient's plasma. Thrombin cuts fibrinogen so that it can join up into a clot. Reptilase is an enzyme from the venom of the snake Bothrops atrox. It also cuts fibrinogen directly, by removing fibrinopeptide A, so it also makes a clot. But reptilase is not thrombin.

Why the two results split here. Dabigatran blocks thrombin. So the thrombin time, which depends completely on thrombin, cannot form a clot within the measuring time (over 180 s). Reptilase is not inhibited by thrombin inhibitors or by heparin, so it clots normally (17 s).

What the pair tells you. Both tests need normal fibrinogen to make a clot. If fibrinogen were the problem, either too little of it (hypofibrinogenaemia) or an abnormal form (dysfibrinogenaemia), both tests would be prolonged. Only the thrombin time is prolonged. So the fault is a blocked thrombin, and fibrinogen is cleared of blame. This agrees with the normal PT-derived fibrinogen.

The screening rule. Because the thrombin time is so sensitive to dabigatran, a normal thrombin time would have excluded clinically significant dabigatran. This is the most useful single screening statement about dabigatran in a laboratory without a calibrated assay, and it earns a mark on its own. Note the direction: a normal TT rules the drug out, but a long TT only shows the drug is present, not how much.

Think of it like this
Two different keys open the same door (fibrinogen). Dabigatran jams one key (thrombin) but not the other (reptilase). If the door opens easily with the second key, the door itself is fine.
Common mistake
Reading the very long thrombin time as a fibrinogen problem without looking at the normal reptilase time beside it.
Remember
Long TT with normal reptilase: thrombin is blocked, fibrinogen is fine.

What earns the marks

  • TT unmeasurable because it is exquisitely sensitive to direct thrombin inhibition
  • Reptilase normal: cleaves fibrinopeptide A directly; not inhibited by thrombin inhibitors or heparin
  • Pattern localises the defect to thrombin inhibition and exonerates fibrinogen; a fibrinogen defect would prolong both
  • A normal TT would have excluded clinically significant dabigatran
Evidence[2]Douxfils 2021[4]Gosselin 2018Practical Haemostasis — Thrombin Time; DOACs (screening-test effects table)
Q3
Is disseminated intravascular coagulation an adequate explanation? Give two reasons from the data.
4 marks

Model answer

No. First, the platelet count is normal at 212 ×10⁹/L: consumption severe enough to drop fibrinogen to 0.8 g/L would be expected to consume platelets. Second, the D-dimer is normal, which is very difficult to reconcile with DIC of that severity. Third, decisive: the two fibrinogen methods disagree. Genuine hypofibrinogenaemia is method-independent, so a discrepancy this large indicates interference, not consumption.

Explained step by step

What DIC does. Disseminated intravascular coagulation is a consumptive process. Clotting is switched on all over the body, so platelets and fibrinogen are used up. The many small clots are then broken down, which releases D-dimer into the blood.

Reason one: the platelets. If DIC were bad enough to push fibrinogen down to 0.8 g/L, you would expect platelets to be used up too. The platelet count is normal at 212 ×10⁹/L.

Reason two: the D-dimer. DIC of that severity should produce plenty of D-dimer. Here the D-dimer is normal. That is very difficult to reconcile with DIC.

The decisive third argument: the fibrinogen methods disagree. If fibrinogen were truly low because it had been used up, every method would find it low, because there would simply be less of it in the tube. A true low fibrinogen is method-independent. Here the Clauss method says 0.8 g/L and the PT-derived method says 3.2 g/L. A gap this large means something is interfering with one test, and that is dabigatran acting on the thrombin in the Clauss reagent.

So DIC is not an adequate explanation. A drug effect explains everything; DIC explains almost nothing.

Think of it like this
If a fire had burned the wood (fibrinogen), you would see smoke (D-dimer) and the kindling (platelets) would be gone too. No smoke and the kindling untouched: there was no fire, only a faulty gauge.
Common mistake
Diagnosing DIC from one low fibrinogen and a long APTT, without checking the platelets, D-dimer and the second fibrinogen method.
Remember
Real low fibrinogen is low by every method; disagreement means interference.

What earns the marks

  • Answer: no, DIC is not adequate
  • Normal platelet count (212 ×10⁹/L): consumption this severe would consume platelets
  • Normal D-dimer: very difficult to reconcile with severe DIC
  • Discordant fibrinogen methods: true hypofibrinogenaemia is method-independent, so this is interference
Evidence[2]Douxfils 2021[4]Gosselin 2018Practical Haemostasis — DOACs (falsely decreased Clauss fibrinogen)
Q4
Which results quantify the drug effect and which are only qualitative? If an antithrombin activity assay were requested on this sample, what error would arise and why?
4 marks

Model answer

Quantitative: the dabigatran-calibrated dilute thrombin time (240 ng/mL); the ecarin clotting time or ecarin chromogenic assay is an equally valid alternative. Qualitative only: PT, APTT and standard TT show drug is present but not how much; the APTT flattens at higher concentrations and cannot be used to titrate. An anti-Xa assay is not a dabigatran assay. Antithrombin by a thrombin-based (anti-IIa) method: dabigatran inhibits the reagent thrombin, less residual thrombin remains, so antithrombin is falsely elevated and true deficiency may be masked. Use a factor-Xa-based method.

Explained step by step

Measuring versus detecting. A quantitative test gives you a number for the drug level. A qualitative test only tells you the drug is there. Only one result in this table is quantitative: the dilute thrombin time calibrated for dabigatran, which reports 240 ng/mL. The ecarin clotting time or ecarin chromogenic assay would be an equally valid alternative.

The qualitative tests. The PT, APTT and standard TT are all affected by dabigatran, so they show it is present. But they cannot measure how much. The APTT is a special trap: its curve flattens at higher drug levels, so a big rise in drug causes only a small rise in APTT. It cannot be used to titrate. An anti-Xa assay must not be requested: it is not a dabigatran assay, because dabigatran blocks thrombin, not factor Xa.

The antithrombin trap. In a thrombin-based (anti-IIa) antithrombin activity assay, a set amount of thrombin is added to the plasma. The patient's antithrombin neutralises some of it. The analyser then measures the thrombin that is left over: less left over is read as more antithrombin.

Dabigatran also blocks the reagent thrombin. So even less thrombin is left over, and the analyser gives the credit to antithrombin. The antithrombin result is falsely elevated, and a true antithrombin deficiency could be hidden. In a patient on a direct thrombin inhibitor, use a factor-Xa-based antithrombin method.

The mirror-image trap. The anti-Xa DOACs falsely elevate the factor-Xa-based antithrombin assays. Rule: choose the antithrombin method that the patient's drug does not touch.

Think of it like this
A teacher works out how many sweets one child ate by counting what is left in the jar. If a second child (dabigatran) secretly eats some too, the first child (antithrombin) gets credit for eating more than they really did.
Common mistake
Using the APTT to judge how much dabigatran is present, or ordering an anti-Xa level for dabigatran.
Remember
Only a drug-calibrated assay gives a number; screening tests just say 'present'.

What earns the marks

  • Quantitative: dabigatran-calibrated dilute TT (240 ng/mL); ecarin clotting time or ecarin chromogenic assay equally valid
  • Qualitative only: PT, APTT, standard TT; APTT flattens at higher levels and cannot titrate; anti-Xa is not a dabigatran assay
  • Thrombin-based antithrombin assay falsely elevated: dabigatran inhibits reagent thrombin, leaving less residual thrombin
  • Risk of masking true deficiency; use a factor-Xa-based method
Evidence[2]Douxfils 2021[4]Gosselin 2018Practical Haemostasis — DOACs (dilute thrombin time and ecarin clotting time as quantitative assays, antithrombin substrate interference)
Q5
State the urgent management, naming the specific reversal agent and dose, and one alternative if it is unavailable. Draft the laboratory comment.
4 marks

Model answer

Idarucizumab 5 g intravenously as two consecutive 2.5 g infusions: a humanised monoclonal antibody fragment binding dabigatran far more tightly than thrombin; reversal immediate and near-complete. If idarucizumab is unavailable, consider prothrombin complex concentrate or activated PCC and/or haemodialysis. Dabigatran is the only DOAC removed by dialysis, but dialysis is rarely needed now that idarucizumab is available. Also resuscitate and transfuse, withhold dabigatran, urgent upper gastrointestinal endoscopy, proton pump inhibitor; re-measure dilute TT (rebound possible). Comment: clinically significant dabigatran (240 ng/mL); Clauss fibrinogen 0.8 g/L falsely low from interference, not hypofibrinogenaemia; no evidence of DIC; idarucizumab advised. Never give cryoprecipitate or fibrinogen concentrate.

Explained step by step

Specific reversal. Give idarucizumab 5 g intravenously, as two consecutive 2.5 g infusions. Idarucizumab is a humanised monoclonal antibody fragment. It binds dabigatran far more tightly than thrombin does, so it pulls the drug away and frees thrombin to work. Reversal is immediate and near-complete.

If idarucizumab is not available. Consider prothrombin complex concentrate or activated PCC, and/or haemodialysis. Guidelines list these together rather than in a fixed order. Dabigatran is the only DOAC removed by haemodialysis (about half of it in 4 hours in a study of stable dialysis patients), but dialysis is rarely needed now that idarucizumab is available.

Everything else at the same time. Resuscitation and transfusion support; withhold dabigatran; urgent upper gastrointestinal endoscopy; a proton pump inhibitor; correct any contributory factors. After reversal, re-measure the dilute thrombin time, because dabigatran can redistribute back into the blood (rebound) after idarucizumab.

The laboratory comment (from the model answer). "Results indicate a clinically significant dabigatran concentration, measured at 240 ng/mL by calibrated dilute thrombin time. The Clauss fibrinogen of 0.8 g/L is falsely low because of dabigatran interference with this thrombin-based method and must not be treated as hypofibrinogenaemia — prothrombin-time-derived fibrinogen is 3.2 g/L. There is no laboratory evidence of disseminated intravascular coagulation. Discussed with the medical registrar at 02:40; specific reversal with idarucizumab advised."

The trap this station is built on. A candidate who transfuses cryoprecipitate or fibrinogen concentrate for the reported fibrinogen of 0.8 g/L has failed the station, whatever else they write. The fibrinogen is not low. Treating an artefact is the harm.

Think of it like this
Idarucizumab is like a stronger magnet: it grabs the dabigatran more firmly than thrombin can, so thrombin is let go and can do its job again.
Common mistake
Giving cryoprecipitate or fibrinogen concentrate for a fibrinogen of 0.8 g/L that is an artefact. This fails the station.
Remember
Reverse the drug, not the artefact: idarucizumab, never cryoprecipitate.

What earns the marks

  • Idarucizumab 5 g intravenously as two consecutive 2.5 g infusions, with its mechanism
  • Alternative if no idarucizumab: PCC or activated PCC and/or haemodialysis (dabigatran is the only dialysable DOAC)
  • Supportive care: resuscitation, transfusion support, stop dabigatran, urgent endoscopy, proton pump inhibitor; re-measure dilute TT for rebound
  • Laboratory comment stating the Clauss fibrinogen is falsely low and must not be treated; no cryoprecipitate or fibrinogen concentrate
Evidence[8]Pollack 2017[2]Douxfils 2021[4]Gosselin 2018Practical Haemostasis — DOACs
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Rebound after idarucizumab: who needs a second dose?

In RE-VERSE AD every patient received 5 g as two 2.5 g boluses no more than 15 minutes apart [S1]. Reversal was essentially complete: the median maximum reversal was 100% by dilute thrombin time or ecarin clotting time [S2].

But unbound dabigatran came back to 20 ng/mL or more in 114 patients, probably as drug moved from the tissues back into the blood [S3]. The ANZ guideline warns that patients with high levels and renal impairment (exactly this patient) may need further doses to stop dabigatran reappearing 12–24 h later [S4].

Exam point: a rebound in the laboratory result alone is not an indication for a second dose. The investigators advise considering one only when the clotting time rises again and there is new or recurrent bleeding [S5].

  1. [S1] Glund S, Coble K, Gansser D, et al 2019. Pharmacokinetics of idarucizumab and its target dabigatran in patients requiring urgent reversal of the anticoagulant effect of dabigatran. J Thromb Haemost 2019;17:1319-1328 PMID 31050868 · doi:10.1111/jth.14476All patients received 5 g of intravenous idarucizumab administered as two 50-mL bolus infusions each containing 2.5 g of idarucizumab ≤15 minutes apart.
  2. [S2] Pollack CV Jr, Reilly PA, van Ryn J, et al 2017. Idarucizumab for Dabigatran Reversal - Full Cohort Analysis. N Engl J Med 2017;377:431-441 PMID 28693366 · doi:10.1056/NEJMoa1707278The median maximum percentage reversal of dabigatran was 100% (95% confidence interval, 100 to 100), on the basis of either the diluted thrombin time or the ecarin clotting time.
  3. [S3] Glund S, Coble K, Gansser D, et al 2019. Pharmacokinetics of idarucizumab and its target dabigatran in patients requiring urgent reversal of the anticoagulant effect of dabigatran. J Thromb Haemost 2019;17:1319-1328 PMID 31050868 · doi:10.1111/jth.14476a recurrence of unbound dabigatran concentrations of 20 ng/mL or higher was observed in 114 patients, most likely because of dabigatran being redistributed from the extravascular to the intravascular compartment.
  4. [S4] Tran HA, Merriman E, Baker R, et al 2025. 2025 Guidelines for direct oral anticoagulants: a practical guidance on the prescription, laboratory testing, peri-operative and bleeding management. Intern Med J 2025;55:1174-1183 PMID 40448969 · doi:10.1111/imj.70103In patients with high dabigatran levels, particularly with renal impairment, further doses of idarucizumab may be required to avoid the reappearance of dabigatran in the circulation 12-24 h after the last dose.
  5. [S5] Glund S, Coble K, Gansser D, et al 2019. Pharmacokinetics of idarucizumab and its target dabigatran in patients requiring urgent reversal of the anticoagulant effect of dabigatran. J Thromb Haemost 2019;17:1319-1328 PMID 31050868 · doi:10.1111/jth.14476a second dose of idarucizumab should only be considered in those patients with a recurrent elevation in clotting time who also have new-onset or recurrent bleeding.
When idarucizumab is not available

Dabigatran is unusual among the DOACs: it is the only one removed by haemodialysis, although dialysis is rarely used now that idarucizumab is available [S1]. In a phase I study in end-stage renal disease, four hours of dialysis removed about half of the circulating dabigatran (48.8% and 59.3% at the two blood-flow rates tested) [S2]. Those were stable volunteers, not bleeding patients.

Guidelines do not rank the alternatives. ANZ 2025 says PCC or activated PCC can be considered when a specific agent is unavailable [S3]. For intracerebral haemorrhage on dabigatran, the AHA/ASA algorithm offers PCC or aPCC and/or renal replacement therapy as one weak (2b) option [S4]. Present them as equal options chosen by local availability, not as a fixed second and third line.

  1. [S1] Tran HA, Merriman E, Baker R, et al 2025. 2025 Guidelines for direct oral anticoagulants: a practical guidance on the prescription, laboratory testing, peri-operative and bleeding management. Intern Med J 2025;55:1174-1183 PMID 40448969 · doi:10.1111/imj.70103Of all the DOACs, only dabigatran is removed by haemodialysis, but it is rarely performed because of the availability of idarucizumab.
  2. [S2] Khadzhynov D, Wagner F, Formella S, et al 2013. Effective elimination of dabigatran by haemodialysis. A phase I single-centre study in patients with end-stage renal disease. Thromb Haemost 2013;109:596-605 PMID 23389759 · doi:10.1160/TH12-08-0573Four hours of haemodialysis removed 48.8% and 59.3% of total dabigatran from the central compartment with 200 and 400 ml/minute targeted blood flow, respectively.
  3. [S3] Tran HA, Merriman E, Baker R, et al 2025. 2025 Guidelines for direct oral anticoagulants: a practical guidance on the prescription, laboratory testing, peri-operative and bleeding management. Intern Med J 2025;55:1174-1183 PMID 40448969 · doi:10.1111/imj.70103If not available, pro-haemostatic agents such as prothrombin complex concentrates or activated prothrombin complex concentrates can be considered.
  4. [S4] Greenberg SM, Ziai WC, Cordonnier C, et al 2022. 2022 Guideline for the Management of Patients With Spontaneous Intracerebral Hemorrhage (AHA/ASA). Stroke 2022;53:e282-e361 (quoted from the AHA Clinical Update slide summary held in the library) PMID 35579034 · doi:10.1161/STR.0000000000000407PCCs or aPCC and/or renal replacement therapy (2b)

Take home from this station

  1. When a patient on dabigatran has strange clotting results, think of the drug first: one cause can explain everything.
  2. The Clauss fibrinogen uses thrombin, so dabigatran makes it falsely low; check the PT-derived fibrinogen and the reptilase time before believing it.
  3. A normal thrombin time excludes clinically significant dabigatran; only a drug-calibrated assay, such as the dilute thrombin time, measures how much is there.
  4. Reverse dabigatran with idarucizumab (or haemodialysis if unavailable), and never treat a laboratory artefact with cryoprecipitate or fibrinogen concentrate.
06
Station 06 · 20 marks

Day seven after cardiac surgery

What this station tests: Recognise heparin-induced thrombocytopenia with thrombosis, confirm it with the right tests, then choose, switch and monitor non-heparin anticoagulants safely.

The case

A 58-year-old man is seven days post coronary artery bypass grafting and has received unfractionated heparin throughout. His platelet count was 320 ×10⁹/L pre-operatively and is now 88 ×10⁹/L. He has developed a new symptomatic right femoral deep vein thrombosis. Blood cultures are negative, there is no disseminated intravascular coagulation, and no drug other than heparin has been introduced.

In plain wordsA 58-year-old man had heart bypass surgery seven days ago and has been on heparin the whole time. His platelet count has fallen from 320 to 88 ×10⁹/L. He now has a new clot in the deep vein of his right thigh. Infection, disseminated intravascular coagulation and other drugs have been ruled out.
Investigations
TestResult
Platelet count nadir (day 7)88 ×10⁹/L
Anti-PF4/heparin IgG ELISA, optical density2.35
Prothrombin time / APTTNormal
Fibrinogen, D-dimerNormal / mildly raised

How to read the data

  • Platelet count nadir of 88 ×10⁹/L on day 7: a fall of 72.5% from 320 ×10⁹/L. The size and timing of the fall both fit heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (HIT).
  • Anti-platelet factor 4 (PF4)/heparin immunoglobulin G (IgG) enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) optical density (OD) 2.35: strongly positive. The size of the number matters, not just positive or negative.
  • Prothrombin time (PT) and activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) are normal. Together with the clinical statement of no disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC), this makes consumption an unlikely cause of the low platelets.
  • Fibrinogen normal and D-dimer only mildly raised: consistent with a new deep vein thrombosis (DVT), not with a consumptive process.

The five questions

Q1
Calculate the 4T score, itemising each component, and state the resulting pre-test probability.
4 marks

Model answer

Thrombocytopenia 2 (320 → 88 ×10⁹/L: a fall of more than 50% (72.5%) with nadir ≥20 ×10⁹/L). Timing 2 (day 7, within 5–10 days of exposure). Thrombosis 2 (new proven proximal DVT). oTher cause 2 (none evident: cultures negative, no DIC). Total 8 = high pre-test probability of type II HIT (6 or more high; 4–5 intermediate; 3 or less low, with a high negative predictive value).

Explained step by step

Thrombocytopenia  320 → 88 ×10⁹/L = 72.5% fall, nadir ≥20      → 2
Timing          day 7, within 5–10 days of exposure       → 2
Thrombosis      new proven proximal DVT                    → 2
oTher cause     none evident; cultures negative, no DIC    → 2
                                                    TOTAL   8

What the 4T score is. It is a bedside score that estimates how likely HIT is before any test result comes back. It looks at four things, each starting with T: Thrombocytopenia, Timing, Thrombosis and oTher causes.

Thrombocytopenia. The fall is 320 − 88 = 232 ×10⁹/L. Divide 232 by 320 and you get 0.725, a 72.5% fall. The fall is more than 50% and the lowest count (nadir) of 88 is at least 20 ×10⁹/L. Score 2.

Timing. The fall began on day 7, which is within 5–10 days of heparin exposure. This is the time the immune system needs to make the antibody. Score 2.

Thrombosis. He has a new, proven, proximal DVT (the femoral vein is a proximal vein). Score 2.

oTher cause. Blood cultures are negative, there is no DIC and no new drug. Nothing else explains the fall. Score 2.

Total 8 = high pre-test probability. A score of 6 or more is high; 4–5 is intermediate; 3 or less is low, and a low score has a high negative predictive value (0.998 in the BSH 2024 guideline). So the clinical picture alone already makes type II HIT very likely.

Think of it like this
The 4T score is like a detective's checklist before the forensic report arrives: a big drop, at the right time, with a fresh clot, and no other suspect. Four strong clues point to one culprit.
Common mistake
Giving a total without itemising each T, or miscalculating the percentage fall.
Remember
Big drop, day 5–10, new clot, no other cause: think HIT.

What earns the marks

  • Thrombocytopenia 2: 72.5% fall, nadir 88 ×10⁹/L
  • Timing 2: day 7, within 5–10 days of exposure
  • Thrombosis 2 and oTher cause 2, each justified from the data
  • Total 8 = high pre-test probability (6 or more high; 4–5 intermediate; 3 or less low)
Evidence[9]Cuker 2018Practical Haemostasis — HIT and Anti-PF4 Assays (4T table)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
The 4Ts score after cardiac surgery

This patient is seven days after bypass surgery, where the 4Ts score is least reliable. After cardiopulmonary bypass, a large fall in platelets is very common in the first 72 hours [S1]. So the shape of the platelet curve matters more than the nadir alone: recovery followed by a second fall between days 5 and 14 is much more suspicious of HIT than a low count that simply persists beyond day 4 [S2].

BSH also reports that the positive predictive value of the 4Ts score after cardiopulmonary bypass was only 0.562, and suggests that a low score may not be enough to exclude HIT in this group [S3]. Exam point: ask for the full daily platelet series and score timing from it, not just from the day of the nadir.

  1. [S1] Arachchillage DJ, Thachil J, Anderson JAM, et al 2024. Diagnosis and management of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia: Third edition. Br J Haematol 2024;204:459-475 PMID 38153164 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19180In patients undergoing cardiopulmonary bypass, a significant fall in the platelet count is very common in the first 72 h (h) postsurgery.
  2. [S2] Arachchillage DJ, Thachil J, Anderson JAM, et al 2024. Diagnosis and management of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia: Third edition. Br J Haematol 2024;204:459-475 PMID 38153164 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19180In these patients, platelet recovery followed by a secondary fall in counts between postoperative days 5-14 is much more suspicious of HIT than a low count that persists beyond Day 4.
  3. [S3] Arachchillage DJ, Thachil J, Anderson JAM, et al 2024. Diagnosis and management of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia: Third edition. Br J Haematol 2024;204:459-475 PMID 38153164 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19180The positive predictive value of the 4Ts score in identifying HIT in patients post cardiopulmonary bypass and ECMO was only 0.562 (18/32) and 0.25 (15/60), respectively
Q2
Interpret an optical density of 2.35. What confirmatory testing should follow, and what result would confirm the antibody is heparin-dependent?
4 marks

Model answer

OD 2.35 is strongly positive: in the original study, an OD of 2.0 or more carried a probability of about 90% of a strongly positive serotonin release assay, and each 0.5 unit rise multiplied the odds about 6.4-fold (OR 6.39, one laboratory's IgG ELISA). So the magnitude must be reported, using assay-specific thresholds. Confirm with a functional assay in an expert centre, such as heparin-induced platelet activation. The 14C-serotonin release assay is the reference standard but is not available in the UK. Heparin dependence: repeat the immunoassay at high-dose heparin (100 U/mL); more than 50% reduction in OD is characteristic of true HIT antibodies. A positive immunoassay alone is not a diagnosis.

Explained step by step

What the ELISA measures. This immunoassay detects IgG antibodies that stick to complexes of PF4 and heparin. The more antibody, the stronger the colour, and the colour is reported as an optical density.

Why 2.35 matters. An OD of 2.35 is strongly positive. In the original study, at 2.0 OD units or more the chance that a functional test (the serotonin release assay, SRA) would be strongly positive was about 90%. The relationship is steep: each 0.5 unit rise multiplied the odds of a strongly positive functional assay about 6.4-fold. These figures come from one laboratory's assay, so reports should carry assay-specific thresholds. So the laboratory must report the actual number, not just 'positive'.

Confirming with a functional assay. An immunoassay shows the antibody is there. A functional assay shows it can actually activate platelets. Use a heparin-induced platelet activation test in an expert centre. The 14C-serotonin release assay is the original reference standard but is not available in the UK.

Proving heparin dependence. Repeat the immunoassay with a very high heparin concentration (100 U/mL). A fall in OD of more than 50% shows the antibody is heparin-dependent, which is characteristic of true HIT antibodies.

The iceberg. Many more people make anti-PF4/heparin antibodies than develop the disease. A positive immunoassay alone is not a diagnosis. Read it with the 4T score and, where available, a functional assay.

Think of it like this
The ELISA is like a smoke alarm: it tells you smoke is present, and a louder alarm means more smoke. The functional assay is like checking whether there is an actual fire.
Common mistake
Treating any positive immunoassay as diagnostic of HIT, without the 4T score or a functional test.
Remember
Read the OD number, not just the word 'positive'.

What earns the marks

  • OD 2.35 strongly positive; at 2.0 or more, about 90% probability of a strongly positive serotonin release assay (one laboratory's data)
  • Magnitude matters: about 6.4-fold increase in odds per 0.5 unit rise; use assay-specific thresholds
  • Functional confirmation in an expert centre, e.g. heparin-induced platelet activation (the serotonin release assay is not available in the UK)
  • Heparin dependence: high-dose heparin (100 U/mL) causes more than 50% reduction in OD
Evidence[9]Cuker 2018Practical Haemostasis — HIT and Anti-PF4 Assays (optical density probability table)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Optical density thresholds and the high-dose heparin step: two cautions

The '90% at OD 2.0' figure comes from one referral laboratory. Even there, the probability of HIT antibodies reached 50% only at an OD of 1.40 or more [S1]. BSH therefore recommends that reports carry assay-specific thresholds [S2].

The high-dose heparin step is useful but not infallible. In a validation study, maximal OD and a positive confirmatory step each independently predicted HIT [S3]. But in an earlier series, two patients with clinical HIT were confirm-negative, and all of them had high OD values [S4]. Exam point: a high OD with a negative confirmatory step does not exclude HIT. Send for a functional assay and keep the patient off heparin meanwhile.

  1. [S1] Warkentin TE, Sheppard JI, Moore JC, Sigouin CS, Kelton JG 2008. Quantitative interpretation of optical density measurements using PF4-dependent enzyme-immunoassays. J Thromb Haemost 2008;6:1304-1312 PMID 18489711 · doi:10.1111/j.1538-7836.2008.03025.xIn our laboratory, the probability of HIT antibodies being present reached >or=50% only when the OD level was >or=1.40 units.
  2. [S2] Arachchillage DJ, Thachil J, Anderson JAM, et al 2024. Diagnosis and management of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia: Third edition. Br J Haematol 2024;204:459-475 PMID 38153164 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19180HIT reporting should include assay-specific thresholds (1B).
  3. [S3] Whitlatch NL, Kong DF, Metjian AD, Arepally GM, Ortel TL 2010. Validation of the high-dose heparin confirmatory step for the diagnosis of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia. Blood 2010;116:1761-1766 PMID 20508160 · doi:10.1182/blood-2010-01-262659Maximal optical density and confirmatory positive status independently predicted HIT in multivariate analysis.
  4. [S4] Whitlatch NL, Perry SL, Ortel TL 2008. Anti-heparin/platelet factor 4 antibody optical density values and the confirmatory procedure in the diagnosis of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia. Thromb Haemost 2008;100:678-684 PMID 18841292 · doi:10.1160/th08-02-0118Two confirm- patients were HIT+, one was HIT?; all had high ELISA OD values.
Q3
Heparin is stopped. Name three alternative anticoagulants suitable now, and state why warfarin must not be started at this point.
4 marks

Model answer

Stop all heparin, including flushes and heparin-bonded catheters. Alternatives: argatroban (direct thrombin inhibitor, hepatically cleared, preferred in renal impairment); danaparoid (heparinoid; weak in vitro cross-reactivity is common, but clinically significant cross-reactivity is uncommon, 3.2% in a series of 1,478 treatment episodes, whereas LMWH cross-reacts with most HIT sera, 88% in one study); fondaparinux; bivalirudin (short half-life, favoured in cardiac surgery and percutaneous intervention; dose reduction needed in moderate to severe renal impairment). No warfarin now: protein C falls precipitously before procoagulant factors, worsening the prothrombotic state, causing venous limb gangrene and skin necrosis (typically international normalised ratio (INR) above 4.0). Defer until platelets above 150 ×10⁹/L, then overlap at least five days; if already given, reverse with vitamin K.

Explained step by step

First, remove every source of heparin. This includes line flushes and heparin-bonded catheters, not just the infusion.

Choose a non-heparin anticoagulant.

  • Argatroban: a direct thrombin inhibitor cleared by the liver, so preferred in renal impairment.
  • Danaparoid: a heparinoid. Weak cross-reactivity with HIT antibodies is common in the test tube, but clinically significant cross-reactivity is uncommon (3.2% in a large series), whereas LMWH cross-reacts with most HIT sera (88% in one study), which is why LMWH is not a safe switch.
  • Fondaparinux.
  • Bivalirudin: a direct thrombin inhibitor with a short half-life, largely cleared by enzymes, favoured in cardiac surgery and percutaneous intervention.

Why not warfarin now? Warfarin lowers all the vitamin K-dependent proteins. One of these, protein C, is a natural anticoagulant with a short half-life, so it falls first and fast. The clotting factors fall more slowly. For a few days the balance tips even further towards clotting, in a patient whose blood is already strongly prothrombotic from HIT.

The result can be venous limb gangrene and skin necrosis, typically with an INR above 4.0.

When to start warfarin. Wait until the platelet count has recovered above 150 ×10⁹/L. Then overlap with the parenteral agent for a minimum of five days. If warfarin has already been given, reverse it with vitamin K.

Think of it like this
Starting warfarin in acute HIT is like taking the brakes off a car (protein C) before easing off the accelerator (clotting factors), while it is already speeding downhill.
Common mistake
Switching to low-molecular-weight heparin, or starting warfarin while the platelet count is still low.
Remember
In acute HIT: no heparin of any kind, and no warfarin until platelets are back.

What earns the marks

  • Stop all heparin, including flushes and heparin-bonded catheters
  • Three correct alternatives from argatroban, danaparoid, fondaparinux, bivalirudin
  • Warfarin causes a precipitous fall in protein C, risking venous limb gangrene and skin necrosis
  • Defer warfarin until platelets above 150 ×10⁹/L, overlap at least five days; vitamin K if already given
Evidence[9]Cuker 2018Practical Haemostasis — HIT and Anti-PF4 Assays (warfarin and venous limb gangrene, danaparoid cross-reactivity)
Q4
He is commenced on argatroban. Explain the specific difficulty this creates when transitioning to warfarin, and how it is managed.
4 marks

Model answer

Argatroban prolongs the PT and falsely elevates the INR, because it inhibits the thrombin generated in the PT reaction. The combined INR substantially exceeds the warfarin-only INR, so stopping argatroban at an apparently therapeutic INR leaves the patient unprotected. Manage by overlapping and stopping argatroban only when the combined INR exceeds a locally validated higher threshold (commonly above 4), then repeat the INR four to six hours later to confirm it remains therapeutic on warfarin alone; or monitor with a chromogenic factor X assay (unaffected by argatroban): after at least 5 days of overlap, stop argatroban when chromogenic factor X is 45% or less. Establish the local dose–INR relationship in advance (reagent-dependent).

Explained step by step

The problem. The INR comes from the prothrombin time, which ends with thrombin turning fibrinogen into a clot. Argatroban blocks thrombin, so it lengthens the PT and falsely raises the INR, even though it is not warfarin.

When both drugs are given together, the INR you see is warfarin's effect plus argatroban's effect. It is much higher than the INR from warfarin alone. If you stop argatroban as soon as the INR looks therapeutic, the true warfarin effect underneath may be too low, and the patient is left unprotected.

Option one: a higher INR target, then recheck. Overlap the two drugs. Stop argatroban only when the combined INR exceeds a locally validated higher threshold (commonly above 4). Then repeat the INR four to six hours after the infusion stops, and confirm it is still therapeutic on warfarin alone.

Option two: a test argatroban does not affect. Monitor with a chromogenic factor X assay, which is unaffected by argatroban: after at least 5 days of overlap, stop argatroban when chromogenic factor X is 45% or less.

Plan ahead. The effect of argatroban on the INR depends on the PT reagent. Each laboratory should establish its own relationship between argatroban dose and INR in advance.

Think of it like this
It is like weighing a parcel while someone's hand is pressing on the scale. To know the parcel's real weight, either lift the hand and weigh again (repeat INR off argatroban) or use a scale the hand cannot touch (chromogenic factor X).
Common mistake
Stopping argatroban as soon as the combined INR reaches 2–3, leaving the patient under-anticoagulated.
Remember
On argatroban, the INR lies high: check it again off the drug.

What earns the marks

  • Argatroban prolongs the PT and falsely elevates the INR (inhibits thrombin generated in the PT reaction)
  • Combined INR exceeds warfarin-only INR; stopping at an apparently therapeutic INR leaves the patient unprotected
  • Stop argatroban only above a locally validated higher INR (commonly above 4), then repeat INR four to six hours later
  • Or use chromogenic factor X (unaffected by argatroban): after at least 5 days of overlap, stop argatroban at 45% or less
Evidence[9]Cuker 2018Practical Haemostasis — HIT and Anti-PF4 Assays
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Argatroban to warfarin: the two validated routes

The INR during co-therapy rises in line with the true warfarin INR, but how steeply depends on the argatroban dose and the thromboplastin [S1]. So no single INR target transfers between laboratories.

Route 1 (BSH): set the target INR at 4, stop argatroban once two INRs are in that range, then repeat the INR 4–6 hours later [S2].

Route 2: chromogenic factor X. A level of 45% or less predicted an INR of 2 or more off argatroban, with sensitivity 93% and specificity 78% [S3]. In a later series, 43.2% of patients had subtherapeutic factor X levels when argatroban was stopped [S4]. That supports at least 5 days of overlap plus a level of 45% or less before stopping [S5].

  1. [S1] Sheth SB, DiCicco RA, Hursting MJ, Montague T, Jorkasky DK 2001. Interpreting the International Normalized Ratio (INR) in individuals receiving argatroban and warfarin. Thromb Haemost 2001;85:435-440 PMID 11307810Co-therapy INR increased linearly with monotherapy INR, with slope sensitive to argatroban dose and thromboplastin used.
  2. [S2] Arachchillage DJ, Thachil J, Anderson JAM, et al 2024. Diagnosis and management of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia: Third edition. Br J Haematol 2024;204:459-475 PMID 38153164 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19180the target INR should be set at 4; argatroban is stopped once two INR values are in the desired therapeutic range; and subsequently, a repeat INR should be performed 4-6 h later to obtain an accurate INR.
  3. [S3] Arpino PA, Demirjian Z, Van Cott EM 2005. Use of the chromogenic factor X assay to predict the international normalized ratio in patients transitioning from argatroban to warfarin. Pharmacotherapy 2005;25:157-164 PMID 15767231 · doi:10.1592/phco.25.2.157.56950Use of a chromogenic factor X level of 45% or less to predict an INR of 2 or greater absent of argatroban influence had a sensitivity of 93%, a specificity of 78%, and an accuracy of 89%.
  4. [S4] Austin JH, Stearns CR, Winkler AM, Paciullo CA 2012. Use of the chromogenic factor X assay in patients transitioning from argatroban to warfarin therapy. Pharmacotherapy 2012;32:493-501 PMID 22511112 · doi:10.1002/j.1875-9114.2012.01050.xIn 60 patients (43.2%), chromogenic factor X levels were subtherapeutic at the time of argatroban discontinuation.
  5. [S5] Austin JH, Stearns CR, Winkler AM, Paciullo CA 2012. Use of the chromogenic factor X assay in patients transitioning from argatroban to warfarin therapy. Pharmacotherapy 2012;32:493-501 PMID 22511112 · doi:10.1002/j.1875-9114.2012.01050.xPatients should receive at least 5 days of overlap with warfarin and have a chromogenic factor X level of 45% or less before discontinuing argatroban.
Q5
Had danaparoid or fondaparinux been chosen instead, what assay would you use to monitor, and what single methodological requirement applies to each?
4 marks

Model answer

Both are monitored with an anti-Xa assay. The single requirement, the same in principle and absolute in practice: the calibration curve must be made with the drug the patient is actually receiving (danaparoid curve for danaparoid, fondaparinux curve for fondaparinux). A standard unfractionated or low-molecular-weight heparin curve gives a meaningless number. Danaparoid therapeutic anti-Xa range for treatment of venous thromboembolism (VTE) is quoted as approximately 0.5–0.8 U/mL (Practical Haemostasis; confirm against the local protocol). Fondaparinux is an indirect, antithrombin-dependent, selective factor Xa inhibitor, not a direct one.

Explained step by step

The assay. Both drugs act against factor Xa, so both are monitored with an anti-Xa assay. This test measures how much factor Xa activity is blocked in the patient's plasma.

Why calibration is everything. The anti-Xa assay does not 'see' the drug directly. It sees an effect, and then a calibration curve turns that effect into a drug amount. Each drug has its own curve. So the curve must be built with the drug the patient is actually receiving: a danaparoid curve for danaparoid, a fondaparinux curve for fondaparinux. A standard unfractionated heparin or LMWH curve gives a meaningless number for either.

Useful numbers and effects. The therapeutic anti-Xa range for danaparoid in treatment of VTE is quoted as approximately 0.5–0.8 U/mL (Practical Haemostasis; check the local protocol). Fondaparinux, even at prophylactic dose, prolongs the PT and APTT and interferes with factor VIII assays, but it has no effect on the Clauss fibrinogen or the thrombin time.

Correct the textbook here. Fondaparinux is often called a 'direct' factor Xa inhibitor, including on Practical Haemostasis. It is not. It is an indirect, antithrombin-dependent, selective factor Xa inhibitor. It has no activity without antithrombin, which is why antithrombin deficiency causes fondaparinux failure. Note also that protamine does not reverse it.

Think of it like this
A calibration curve is like a currency exchange rate. Converting rupees with the euro rate gives a confident but wrong answer; each drug needs its own rate.
Common mistake
Reporting a fondaparinux or danaparoid level from the laboratory's routine heparin calibration curve.
Remember
Same assay, different drug: different calibration curve.

What earns the marks

  • Anti-Xa assay for both drugs
  • Calibration curve must use the drug the patient is receiving (drug-specific calibrators)
  • A heparin or LMWH curve gives a meaningless result
  • Danaparoid range approximately 0.5–0.8 U/mL; fondaparinux is indirect and antithrombin-dependent
EvidencePractical Haemostasis — Anti-Xa Assays (drug-specific calibration, danaparoid range, fondaparinux assay effects)

Take home from this station

  1. Score the 4Ts item by item before testing; a score of 6 or more means a high pre-test probability of HIT.
  2. Report the ELISA optical density as a number, and confirm with a functional assay; antibodies are far more common than disease.
  3. Stop every source of heparin, use a non-heparin anticoagulant, and delay warfarin until the platelet count is above 150 ×10⁹/L.
  4. Argatroban falsely raises the INR, and anti-Xa monitoring of danaparoid or fondaparinux needs a calibration curve made with that same drug.
07
Station 07 · 20 marks

Confusion, anaemia and a platelet count of 11

What this station tests: Recognise thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura from the blood count, film and a normal clotting screen, test the von Willebrand factor-cleaving enzyme ADAMTS13 before any plasma is given, treat at once, and refuse platelet transfusion.

The case

A 34-year-old woman presents with a two-day history of confusion and headache. She is afebrile. There is no diarrhoeal prodrome and she is not pregnant. The house officer has requested two units of platelets.

In plain wordsA 34-year-old woman has had confusion and headache for two days. She has no fever, no diarrhoea and is not pregnant. She is anaemic, her platelet count is very low at 11 ×10⁹/L, and her red cells are being broken. A junior doctor has asked for two units of platelets.
Admission bloods
TestResultReference
Haemoglobin78 g/L115–165 g/L
Platelet count11 ×10⁹/L150–400 ×10⁹/L
Red cell fragments on film3%<0.5%
Lactate dehydrogenase1450 U/L135–225 U/L
Direct antiglobulin testNegative—
Creatinine96 µmol/L45–90 µmol/L
Prothrombin time / APTT12.4 s / 31 sNormal
Clauss fibrinogen3.4 g/L2.0–4.0 g/L

How to read the data

  • Haemoglobin 78 g/L and platelets 11 ×10⁹/L: anaemia with severe thrombocytopenia (very low platelets).
  • Red cell fragments 3% and lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) 1450 U/L: red cells are being cut up inside small blood vessels. This is a microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia (MAHA).
  • Direct antiglobulin test (DAT) negative: the red cells are not being destroyed by antibodies, so this is not an immune haemolysis.
  • Creatinine 96 µmol/L: only slightly above the reference range, so kidney function is near-normal.
  • Prothrombin time (PT), activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) and Clauss fibrinogen are all normal: clotting factors are not being used up, which argues strongly against disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC).

The five questions

Q1
Interpret this profile. Why do the normal prothrombin time, activated partial thromboplastin time and fibrinogen argue strongly against disseminated intravascular coagulation?
4 marks

Model answer

Microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia with severe thrombocytopenia and neurological involvement: anaemia, 3% fragments, markedly raised LDH, negative DAT excluding immune haemolysis. With near-normal renal function and no diarrhoeal prodrome, this is thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura (TTP) until proved otherwise. DIC consumes clotting factors and fibrinogen and generates fibrin degradation products; normal PT, APTT and fibrinogen are incompatible with DIC severe enough to cause a platelet count of 11 ×10⁹/L. TTP microthrombi are platelet- and von Willebrand factor-rich, not fibrin-rich, so the coagulation screen is preserved.

Explained step by step

Step 1: put the findings together. She has anaemia, 3% red cell fragments and a very high LDH. Fragments mean red cells are being sliced as they squeeze past small clots in tiny vessels; LDH leaks out of the broken cells. The DAT is negative, so antibodies are not the cause. This is a microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia.

Step 2: add the rest. Add severe thrombocytopenia and confusion (neurological involvement). Kidney function is near-normal and there was no diarrhoea before the illness. Put together, this is thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura (TTP) until proved otherwise.

Step 3: why not DIC? DIC is a consumptive coagulopathy. Clotting is switched on everywhere, so clotting factors and fibrinogen are used up and fibrin degradation products appear. A DIC bad enough to drive the platelets down to 11 ×10⁹/L would be expected to lengthen the PT and APTT and lower the fibrinogen. Here all three are normal, which is incompatible with DIC of that severity.

Step 4: why is the clotting screen normal in TTP? In TTP the small clots (microthrombi) are made mainly of platelets and von Willebrand factor (VWF), a sticky protein that glues platelets together. They are not made of fibrin. So platelets are used up, but the clotting factors measured by the PT and APTT are left alone.

Think of it like this
In DIC the whole kitchen is used up: flour, eggs and sugar (clotting factors and fibrinogen) all run out. In TTP only one ingredient runs out (platelets), stuck together by too much glue (VWF), while the rest of the kitchen stays full.
Common mistake
Labelling low platelets with fragments as DIC without looking at the normal clotting screen.
Remember
Fragments plus low platelets plus a normal clotting screen: think TTP.

What earns the marks

  • Microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia: anaemia, fragments, raised LDH, negative DAT
  • Severe thrombocytopenia with neurological involvement, near-normal renal function, no diarrhoea: TTP until proved otherwise
  • DIC consumes clotting factors and fibrinogen; normal PT, APTT and fibrinogen are incompatible with severe DIC
  • TTP microthrombi are platelet- and VWF-rich, not fibrin-rich, so the screen is preserved
Evidence[5]Zheng 2020Practical Haemostasis — ADAMTS13 Assays
Q2
Name the single confirmatory test, the diagnostic threshold, and state precisely when the sample must be drawn.
4 marks

Model answer

ADAMTS13 activity. Severe deficiency, defined as <10 IU/dL (<10% of normal), confirms TTP. Draw the sample before plasma exchange or any plasma infusion: once exogenous ADAMTS13 is given the result is uninterpretable and the diagnostic chance is lost permanently. Take it at presentation, with an anti-ADAMTS13 antibody sample. Do not wait for the result to start treatment.

Explained step by step

The test. ADAMTS13 is the enzyme that trims VWF into smaller, less sticky pieces. When ADAMTS13 is missing, very sticky VWF builds up and platelets clump in small vessels. The confirmatory test is ADAMTS13 activity.

The threshold. Severe deficiency, defined as less than 10 IU/dL (less than 10% of normal), confirms TTP.

The timing. Draw the sample before plasma exchange or any plasma infusion. Donor plasma contains normal ADAMTS13. Once it has been given, the patient's own level is hidden, the result cannot be interpreted, and the chance to make the diagnosis is lost permanently. Take the sample at presentation, together with a sample for anti-ADAMTS13 antibody.

Do not wait for the result. ADAMTS13 assays are not available everywhere and rarely come back within hours. Treatment starts on clinical grounds, supported by a validated clinical prediction score. The assay confirms the diagnosis and guides how long to treat; it does not decide whether to start.

Think of it like this
It is like photographing a crime scene before the cleaners arrive. Once donor plasma has 'cleaned up' the evidence, you can never see what was there.
Common mistake
Sending the ADAMTS13 sample after the first plasma exchange, or waiting for the result before starting treatment.
Remember
Bleed the patient for ADAMTS13 before the first bag of plasma.

What earns the marks

  • ADAMTS13 activity is the confirmatory test
  • Severe deficiency below 10 IU/dL (below 10% of normal) confirms TTP
  • Sample drawn before plasma exchange or any plasma infusion; otherwise uninterpretable
  • Take at presentation with an anti-ADAMTS13 antibody sample; do not delay treatment for the result
Evidence[5]Zheng 2020Practical Haemostasis — ADAMTS13 Assays (pre-exchange sampling)
Q3
ADAMTS13 activity returns at <5 IU/dL. What further testing distinguishes immune-mediated from congenital disease, and what does each result imply for long-term management?
4 marks

Model answer

Anti-ADAMTS13 antibody assay. An ELISA detects both inhibitory and non-inhibitory antibodies; a Bethesda-type assay detects only inhibitory ones, so a negative Bethesda-type result alone does not exclude immune TTP. No antibody: congenital TTP (Upshaw–Schulman syndrome) is confirmed by activity below 10 IU/dL, no antibody and biallelic ADAMTS13 variants; 62% of UK registry cases were diagnosed in adulthood. Immune: immunosuppression; relapse risk driven by antibody return. Congenital: regular ADAMTS13 replacement (plasma, an ADAMTS13-containing intermediate-purity factor VIII concentrate, or recombinant ADAMTS13 where available) and sibling screening; immunosuppression is useless.

Explained step by step

Why ask the question? An activity below 10 IU/dL (here below 5) confirms severe deficiency. But there are two very different reasons for it: the body is making an antibody against ADAMTS13 (immune-mediated TTP), or the person was born unable to make enough (congenital TTP).

The test: anti-ADAMTS13 antibodies. Two kinds of assay are used. An ELISA detects both inhibitory antibodies, which block the enzyme in the tube, and non-inhibitory antibodies, which do not block it but make the body clear it faster. A Bethesda-type assay (mixing study) detects only the inhibitory ones. So a negative Bethesda-type result on its own never excludes immune disease.

If no antibody is found. Consider congenital TTP (Upshaw–Schulman syndrome): it is confirmed by activity below 10 IU/dL, no antibody and two disease-causing ADAMTS13 variants. Do not dismiss it in an adult: in the UK TTP Registry, 62% of congenital cases were diagnosed in adulthood.

Why the distinction matters.

  • Immune-mediated: needs immunosuppression. Relapse risk is driven by the antibody coming back.
  • Congenital: needs regular ADAMTS13 replacement (plasma, an ADAMTS13-containing intermediate-purity factor VIII concentrate, or recombinant ADAMTS13 where available), and screening of siblings. Immunosuppression is useless, because there is no antibody to suppress.

Think of it like this
If the dishes are not getting washed, either someone keeps switching off the dishwasher (an antibody, immune TTP) or the house never had a dishwasher (congenital TTP). The first needs you to stop the saboteur; the second needs a regular supply of a replacement.
Common mistake
Excluding immune TTP on a negative Bethesda-type assay alone, missing non-inhibitory antibodies detectable only by ELISA.
Remember
Antibody present: suppress it. No antibody: sequence the gene and replace the enzyme.

What earns the marks

  • Anti-ADAMTS13 antibody testing distinguishes the two
  • ELISA detects inhibitory and non-inhibitory antibodies; a Bethesda-type assay detects inhibitory only, so a negative Bethesda alone does not exclude immune TTP
  • No antibody: congenital TTP (activity <10 IU/dL, no antibody, biallelic ADAMTS13 variants); often diagnosed in adults
  • Immune: immunosuppression, relapse with antibody return; congenital: ADAMTS13 replacement, sibling screening, no immunosuppression
Evidence[5]Zheng 2020Practical Haemostasis — ADAMTS13 Assays (neutralising versus non-neutralising antibodies, Bethesda assay for ADAMTS13)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Which antibody test actually excludes immune TTP?

BSH describes two methods. An ELISA detects both inhibitory and non-inhibitory anti-ADAMTS13 antibodies, whereas a Bethesda-style assay detects only inhibitory ones [S1]. So a negative Bethesda result on its own never excludes immune TTP.

If no antibody is found, congenital TTP is confirmed by activity below 10 IU/dL, no antibody, and biallelic ADAMTS13 variants [S2]. Do not dismiss it in an adult: in the UK TTP Registry, 62% of congenital cases were diagnosed in adulthood [S3]. Siblings should be screened with ADAMTS13 activity and genetics [S4], and prophylaxis should be considered for every patient with congenital TTP [S5].

(1B)
Q4
Set out immediate management. What is your response to the request for platelet transfusion?
4 marks

Model answer

Urgent plasma exchange, ideally within four to eight hours (mortality fell from around 90% to 10–20%). Add corticosteroids and caplacizumab (anti-VWF nanobody blocking the A1 domain–platelet glycoprotein Ib interaction; in HERCULES it shortened time to platelet normalisation and reduced the composite of TTP-related death, recurrence and major thromboembolic events). Add rituximab for immune-mediated disease. Decline the platelet transfusion unless there is life-threatening haemorrhage: platelets feed the microthrombi, and BSH states that platelet transfusion in TTP is associated with a significant increase in mortality. Explain this to the team; central line insertion does not need platelet cover. Give red cells and folate as needed; withhold thromboprophylaxis until platelets are above 50 ×10⁹/L.

Explained step by step

Plasma exchange, now. Start urgent plasma exchange, ideally within four to eight hours of presentation. It removes the patient's plasma and replaces it with donor plasma that contains working ADAMTS13. This is the treatment that changed the death rate in TTP from around 90% to 10–20%.

Add three drugs.

  • Corticosteroids.
  • Caplacizumab: a nanobody (a very small antibody fragment) against VWF. It blocks the A1 domain of VWF from gripping platelet glycoprotein Ib, so platelets stop sticking. In the HERCULES trial it shortened the time to platelet normalisation and reduced a composite of TTP-related death, recurrence and major thromboembolic events.
  • Rituximab for immune-mediated disease: it depletes the autoantibody, reduces relapse and shortens the duration of exchange.

The platelet request: decline it, unless there is life-threatening bleeding. In TTP, platelets are the raw material of the microthrombi. Adding more can feed the clots, and transfusion is associated with a significant increase in mortality (BSH 2023). Do not just refuse the component: explain the reason directly to the requesting team.

Supportive care. Give red cells and folate as needed. Withhold thromboprophylaxis until the platelet count recovers above 50 ×10⁹/L.

Think of it like this
Giving platelets in TTP is like throwing more bricks to a builder who is already blocking the road with walls. You need to stop the builder (plasma exchange, caplacizumab), not supply more bricks.
Common mistake
Issuing the requested platelets because the count is 11 ×10⁹/L, or delaying plasma exchange while waiting for ADAMTS13.
Remember
In TTP, exchange the plasma, do not feed the clot.

What earns the marks

  • Urgent plasma exchange, ideally within four to eight hours
  • Corticosteroids, caplacizumab (anti-VWF, HERCULES), rituximab for immune disease
  • Decline platelet transfusion unless life-threatening haemorrhage: platelets are the substrate of microthrombi
  • Explain to the requesting team; red cells and folate as needed; thromboprophylaxis once platelets above 50 ×10⁹/L
Evidence[11]Scully 2019
Evidence check verified against the sources below
The first eight hours: the numbers behind the plan

BSH gives a 1A recommendation that plasma exchange starts within four to eight hours of a suspected diagnosis [S1]. Plasma exchange is what cut mortality from 90% to 10–20% [S2].

On the platelet request, BSH is blunt: platelet transfusion in TTP is associated with a significant increase in mortality and should be avoided [S3].

Caplacizumab blocks the VWF A1 region from binding platelet GPIb-IX-V [S4]. In HERCULES the composite of TTP-related death, recurrence or a thromboembolic event fell from 49% to 12% [S5]. Early rituximab (within 3 days) reduces the number of exchanges, ICU days, relapse and mortality [S6].

Br J Haematol 2023;203:546-563 PMID 37586700 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19026Daily PEX, typically with spun apheresis, reduces mortality, from 90% to 10%-20%.

  4. [S4] Scully M, Rayment R, Clark A, et al 2023. A British Society for Haematology Guideline: Diagnosis and management of thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura and thrombotic microangiopathies. Br J Haematol 2023;203:546-563 PMID 37586700 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19026It binds to the A1 region of VWF, preventing platelet binding to the GpIb-IX-V receptor.

  6. [S6] Scully M, Rayment R, Clark A, et al 2023. A British Society for Haematology Guideline: Diagnosis and management of thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura and thrombotic microangiopathies. Br J Haematol 2023;203:546-563 PMID 37586700 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19026Early use of rituximab (within 3 days of admission for iTTP) alongside standard care reduces: number of PEX, days on ICU, relapse rates and mortality.
Q5
After three exchanges a repeat ADAMTS13 activity is 35 IU/dL. Interpret this value in context.
4 marks

Model answer

35 IU/dL is partial recovery on treatment: expected and reassuring, not diagnostic. Guideline cut-offs are for presentation (below 10 IU/dL confirms TTP; 10–20 equivocal) and remission (below 20 triggers pre-emptive rituximab); a mid-range value on treatment cannot be read without knowing where the patient is in treatment. It does not mean exchange can stop: continue daily exchange until the platelet count is sustained above 150 ×10⁹/L, with resolving LDH and clinical improvement. With caplacizumab, extra exchanges after remission are no longer routinely needed. Monitor ADAMTS13 in follow-up: a fall to below 20 IU/dL in remission (or a higher level with symptoms) predicts relapse and is the BSH trigger for pre-emptive rituximab.

Explained step by step

What 35 IU/dL means here. Before treatment she was below 5 IU/dL. After three exchanges she is at 35 IU/dL. This is partial recovery on treatment. In this context it is expected and reassuring.

Why the number alone means little. The guideline cut-offs apply to particular moments. At presentation, below 10 IU/dL confirms TTP and 10–20 IU/dL is equivocal; a value of 20 or more before treatment should prompt a search for another diagnosis. In remission, below 20 IU/dL is the trigger for pre-emptive rituximab. A value of 35 after three exchanges fits none of these: it is recovery under treatment, not a diagnosis.

It does not mean stop. The decision to stop plasma exchange is clinical. Continue daily exchange until the platelet count is sustained above 150 ×10⁹/L, with LDH resolving and the patient improving. With caplacizumab, extra exchanges after remission are no longer routinely needed.

Looking ahead. Keep measuring ADAMTS13 during follow-up. If it falls below 20 IU/dL while she is in remission (or a higher level comes with symptoms), this predicts relapse and is the BSH trigger for pre-emptive rituximab.

Think of it like this
A tank that was nearly empty is now one-third full while the tap is running. That is good progress, but you do not turn off the tap until the gauge you trust (the platelet count) is steady at full.
Common mistake
Stopping plasma exchange because ADAMTS13 has risen, or using a mid-range value to diagnose or exclude TTP.
Remember
Mid-range ADAMTS13 on treatment means progress, not permission to stop.

What earns the marks

  • Partial recovery on treatment: expected and reassuring, not diagnostic
  • Guideline cut-offs are timing-specific: <10 confirms at presentation, 10–20 equivocal, <20 in remission triggers rituximab
  • Not a signal to stop: continue daily exchange until platelets sustained above 150 ×10⁹/L, LDH resolving, clinical improvement
  • Follow-up monitoring: below 20 IU/dL in remission predicts relapse; pre-emptive rituximab
Evidence[5]Zheng 2020Practical Haemostasis — ADAMTS13 Assays (activity interpretation bands)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Reading ADAMTS13 numbers: the guideline cut-offs

Only a few thresholds come from guidelines, and they depend on timing.

  • At presentation, below 10 IU/dL: confirms TTP [S1].
  • 10–20 IU/dL: ISTH calls this equivocal; clinical judgement is needed on whether to continue treatment [S2].
  • 20 IU/dL or more before treatment: ISTH advises considering stopping caplacizumab and looking for another diagnosis [S3]; BSH notes ADAMTS13 in HUS is rarely below 20 IU/dL [S4].
  • In remission, below 20 IU/dL: BSH trigger for pre-emptive rituximab [S5].

A value of 35 IU/dL after three exchanges therefore means recovery under treatment. It is not a diagnostic value, and it is not a signal to stop.

  1. [S1] Scully M, Rayment R, Clark A, et al 2023. A British Society for Haematology Guideline: Diagnosis and management of thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura and thrombotic microangiopathies. Br J Haematol 2023;203:546-563 PMID 37586700 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19026ADAMTS13 activity &lt;10 IU/dL has a high sensitivity (97%) and specificity (100%) in distinguishing TTP from other TMAs.
  2. [S2] Zheng XL, Vesely SK, Cataland SR, et al 2020. ISTH guidelines for the diagnosis of thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:2486-2495 PMID 32914582 · doi:10.1111/jth.15006clinical judgment is required for continuing or stopping treatments (eg, TPE, corticosteroids, rituximab, caplacizumab) when plasma ADAMTS13 activity is between 10 and 20 IU/dL (or 10%-20% of normal) (an equivocal result).
  3. [S3] Zheng XL, Vesely SK, Cataland SR, et al 2020. ISTH guidelines for the diagnosis of thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:2486-2495 PMID 32914582 · doi:10.1111/jth.15006If ADAMTS13 is ≥20 IU/dL (or 20% of normal), consider stop caplacizumab and seek other diagnoses.
  4. [S4] Scully M, Rayment R, Clark A, et al 2023. A British Society for Haematology Guideline: Diagnosis and management of thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura and thrombotic microangiopathies. Br J Haematol 2023;203:546-563 PMID 37586700 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19026ADAMTS13 activity in HUS (of any cause) is rarely below 20 IU/dL.
  5. [S5] Scully M, Rayment R, Clark A, et al 2023. A British Society for Haematology Guideline: Diagnosis and management of thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura and thrombotic microangiopathies. Br J Haematol 2023;203:546-563 PMID 37586700 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19026Pre-emptive therapy with Rituximab should be given when ADAMTS13 activity &lt;20 IU/dL or higher levels associated with clinical symptoms. (1B)

Take home from this station

  1. Fragments, high LDH, a negative DAT and severe thrombocytopenia with a normal clotting screen point to TTP, not DIC.
  2. Take the ADAMTS13 sample before any plasma is given, but never wait for the result to start treatment.
  3. Urgent plasma exchange with steroids, caplacizumab and, for immune disease, rituximab is the treatment; platelet transfusion is declined unless bleeding is life-threatening.
  4. Antibody testing separates immune from congenital TTP, and that decides long-term care: immunosuppression versus enzyme replacement.
08
Station 08 · 20 marks

Thrombophilia testing after an unprovoked DVT

What this station tests: A lupus anticoagulant test done while rivaroxaban is in the blood gives a false positive; recognise it, remove the drug properly, and report safely.

The case

A 35-year-old woman with a recent unprovoked proximal deep vein thrombosis is referred for thrombophilia testing. The request states "off anticoagulant". She is in fact taking rivaroxaban 20 mg daily and took her dose four hours before venepuncture. A drug-specific anti-Xa assay calibrated for rivaroxaban reports 178 ng/mL.

In plain wordsA 35-year-old woman had a deep vein thrombosis (DVT) with no obvious cause, and is sent for thrombophilia testing. The form says she is off anticoagulants, but she actually took rivaroxaban four hours before the blood test. Her rivaroxaban level is 178 ng/mL. The first lupus anticoagulant tests look positive; after the drug is removed from the sample in the laboratory, they are normal.
Lupus anticoagulant panel · before DOAC removal
AssayPatientNormal pooled plasma
dRVVT screen78.4 s34.6 s
dRVVT confirm (high phospholipid)44.2 s33.0 s
Silica clotting time, screen / confirm ratio1.58—
Anticardiolipin IgG / anti-β2GPI IgGNegative—
Repeat after locally validated DOAC-removal procedure
AssayResult
Residual rivaroxaban, anti-Xa<10 ng/mL
dRVVT normalised screen / confirm ratio1.04
Silica clotting time ratio1.03

How to read the data

  • Dilute Russell's viper venom time (dRVVT) screen 78.4 s against 34.6 s for normal pooled plasma: markedly prolonged.
  • dRVVT confirm (high phospholipid) 44.2 s against 33.0 s: less prolonged than the screen, so the screen/confirm ratio looks positive.
  • Silica clotting time (SCT) screen/confirm ratio 1.58: also looks positive, in a second test system.
  • Anticardiolipin and anti-β2-glycoprotein I (anti-β2GPI) immunoglobulin G (IgG): negative.
  • Rivaroxaban, a direct oral anticoagulant (DOAC), measured at 178 ng/mL by anti-Xa assay before removal: enough to invalidate clotting-based lupus anticoagulant tests.
  • After DOAC removal: residual rivaroxaban below 10 ng/mL, normalised dRVVT ratio 1.04 and SCT ratio 1.03, both normal.

The five questions

Q1
From the pre-removal data calculate both the normalised dRVVT screen/confirm ratio and the percentage correction. State the usual cut-off applied to each.
4 marks

Model answer

Screen ratio 78.4/34.6 = 2.27; confirm ratio 44.2/33.0 = 1.34. Normalised screen/confirm ratio 2.27/1.34 = 1.69 (positive if above the laboratory's own 99th-centile cut-off; about 1.2 is a typical value). Percentage correction (2.266 − 1.339)/2.266 × 100 = 40.9% (positive if above the local 99th-centile cut-off; about 10% is typical). Both exceeded. Cut-offs set locally on at least 120 normal donors, or transferred from the manufacturer with local verification. Both conventions are in use and the laboratory must state which it reports.

Explained step by step

Screen ratio  = 78.4 / 34.6 = 2.27
Confirm ratio = 44.2 / 33.0 = 1.34
Normalised screen/confirm ratio = 2.27 / 1.34 = 1.69
Percentage correction = (2.266 − 1.339) / 2.266 × 100 = 40.9%
(unrounded ratios; the rounded 2.27 and 1.34 give 41.0%)

Why we divide by normal pooled plasma. Clotting times vary with the reagent and the analyser. Dividing the patient's time by the time for normal pooled plasma, run at the same time, turns a raw time into a ratio. This is called normalising.

Step 1: the two ratios. Screen ratio = 78.4 ÷ 34.6 = 2.27. Confirm ratio = 44.2 ÷ 33.0 = 1.34.
