Step 2: normalised screen/confirm ratio. 2.27 ÷ 1.34 = 1.69. This is generally taken as positive above about 1.2.

Step 3: percentage correction. This asks: by what percentage did the extra phospholipid shorten the result? (2.266 − 1.339) ÷ 2.266 × 100 = 40.9% (use the unrounded ratios; the rounded figures give 41.0%). This is taken as positive above 10%. Both cut-offs are exceeded comfortably.

Where cut-offs come from. Each laboratory must establish them on at least 120 normal donors, or transfer them from the manufacturer with local verification.

The mark-winning point. Both conventions are in use, and a laboratory must state which one it reports. A trainee who can calculate only one will misread half the reports they receive.

Think of it like this
Normalising is like comparing a runner's time with a standard runner on the same track on the same day: wind and track conditions cancel out, and only the real difference is left.
Common mistake
Dividing raw seconds without normalising to normal pooled plasma, or knowing only one of the two conventions.
Remember
Positive means above your own 99th-centile cut-off: know both the ratio and the percentage correction.

What earns the marks

  • Normalised screen/confirm ratio 1.69 (2.27 / 1.34)
  • Percentage correction 40.9%
  • Cut-offs: positive above the local 99th centile (typically about 1.2 for the ratio, 10% for correction); set on at least 120 normal donors or transferred from the manufacturer
  • Both conventions are in use; the laboratory must state which it reports
Evidence[12]Devreese 2020Practical Haemostasis — dRVVT (percentage correction calculation, >10% threshold)
Q2
If this patient were genuinely off all anticoagulants, how would you interpret the pre-removal profile?
4 marks

Model answer

A prolonged dRVVT screen that shortens substantially with excess phospholipid demonstrates phospholipid dependence, which off anticoagulant would support a lupus anticoagulant. The SCT ratio of 1.58 is a positive result in a second test system that uses a different principle. The ISTH requires both systems to be performed, and a lupus anticoagulant is considered positive if either system is positive through screen, mix and confirm. Here both are, although no mixing step is reported.

Explained step by step

What a lupus anticoagulant does in the tube. A lupus anticoagulant is an antibody that interferes with the phospholipid that clotting tests need. So the clotting time is longer in a test with only a little phospholipid (the screen).

What the confirm step does. The confirm test adds excess phospholipid. This swamps the antibody, and the clotting time shortens. A prolonged screen that shortens substantially when phospholipid is added shows phospholipid dependence.

Reading this profile as if she were off all anticoagulants. The dRVVT screen is long and shortens a lot with extra phospholipid, so the result would support a lupus anticoagulant.

Why the second test matters. The International Society on Thrombosis and Haemostasis (ISTH) requires two test systems that work on different principles to be performed, because a lupus anticoagulant can be missed by one system. A positive result in either system, through all three steps (screen, mix, confirm), is enough to call it positive. The SCT ratio of 1.58 is a positive second test. So, if she were truly off anticoagulants, both test systems would point to a lupus anticoagulant.

Think of it like this
A lupus anticoagulant is like someone hiding the cups at a party, so drinks are slow to serve. If you bring a big box of extra cups (excess phospholipid) and service speeds up, you know missing cups were the problem.
Common mistake
Running only one test system (a lupus anticoagulant can be missed), or calling a lupus anticoagulant without completing all three steps (screen, mix and confirm).
Remember
Long screen, shorter confirm, two test systems: lupus anticoagulant, if no drug is present.

What earns the marks

  • Prolonged dRVVT screen that shortens with excess phospholipid
  • This shows phospholipid dependence
  • Off anticoagulant, supports a lupus anticoagulant
  • SCT ratio 1.58 is a positive second test system with a different principle, as the ISTH requires
Evidence[12]Devreese 2020Practical Haemostasis — dRVVT
Evidence check verified against the sources below
What the ISTH two-system rule actually says

The ISTH method is a three-step procedure (screen, mix, confirm) applied in two test systems, the dRVVT and a sensitive APTT [S1]. Both must be performed, because each can miss a lupus anticoagulant that the other detects. But the interpretation rule is permissive: LA is considered positive if one of the two systems is positive through all three steps [S2].

Positivity is judged against the laboratory's own cut-off. The ratio or percentage correction must exceed the 99th centile [S3], established in-house on at least 120 normals or transferred from the manufacturer [S4]. Figures such as 1.2 or 10% are typical local values, not ISTH constants. Note too that this station's data include no mixing step, which ISTH advises running alongside the confirm [S5].

  1. [S1] Devreese KMJ, de Groot PG, de Laat B, et al 2020. Guidance from the SSC for lupus anticoagulant/antiphospholipid antibodies of the ISTH: Update of the guidelines for lupus anticoagulant detection and interpretation. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:2828-2839 PMID 33462974 · doi:10.1111/jth.15047The three-step procedure with two test systems (diluted Russell's viper venom time and activated partial thromboplastin time [aPTT]) is essentially not changed.
  2. [S2] Devreese KMJ, de Groot PG, de Laat B, et al 2020. Guidance from the SSC for lupus anticoagulant/antiphospholipid antibodies of the ISTH: Update of the guidelines for lupus anticoagulant detection and interpretation. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:2828-2839 PMID 33462974 · doi:10.1111/jth.15047LA should be considered positive if one of the two test systems gives a positive result in the three steps (screen-mix-confirm)
  3. [S3] Devreese KMJ, de Groot PG, de Laat B, et al 2020. Guidance from the SSC for lupus anticoagulant/antiphospholipid antibodies of the ISTH: Update of the guidelines for lupus anticoagulant detection and interpretation. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:2828-2839 PMID 33462974 · doi:10.1111/jth.15047Results are suggestive for LA if LA ratio (screen/confirm) or percentage correction is above the 99th centile.
  4. [S4] Devreese KMJ, de Groot PG, de Laat B, et al 2020. Guidance from the SSC for lupus anticoagulant/antiphospholipid antibodies of the ISTH: Update of the guidelines for lupus anticoagulant detection and interpretation. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:2828-2839 PMID 33462974 · doi:10.1111/jth.15047Cutoff values should be established in-house on at least 120 normals, with transference of the manufacturer's cutoffs as an alternative.
  5. [S5] Devreese KMJ, de Groot PG, de Laat B, et al 2020. Guidance from the SSC for lupus anticoagulant/antiphospholipid antibodies of the ISTH: Update of the guidelines for lupus anticoagulant detection and interpretation. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:2828-2839 PMID 33462974 · doi:10.1111/jth.15047We advise simultaneous performance of the mixing and confirmatory step, in each sample with a prolonged screening test.
Q3
Why is that interpretation unsafe here? Describe the mechanism by which rivaroxaban produces this pattern.
4 marks

Model answer

Rivaroxaban was present at 178 ng/mL, close to peak, which invalidates the result. Rivaroxaban inhibits factor Xa; the dRVVT is a factor-X-activating assay (Russell's viper venom activates factor X directly), so it prolongs the screen markedly and concentration-dependently. The confirm still contains drug, so the phospholipid-rich confirm is affected less than proportionately, giving a falsely positive ratio and percentage correction. Mixing studies do not help: DOACs show incomplete correction on mixing, mimicking an inhibitor. The ISTH does not recommend lupus anticoagulant testing during DOAC treatment unless the drug is first removed or neutralised. The 'off anticoagulant' request was a pre-analytical failure.

Explained step by step

The drug was there. Rivaroxaban was measured at 178 ng/mL. She took it four hours earlier, close to the peak level. This invalidates the lupus anticoagulant result.

Where the drug hits the test. Russell's viper venom activates factor X directly. So the dRVVT is a factor-X-activating assay, and the very next step is factor Xa. Rivaroxaban blocks factor Xa. The assay therefore sits squarely in the drug's path. It prolongs the screen markedly, and more drug means more prolongation (concentration-dependent).

Why the ratio becomes falsely positive. The confirm step adds excess phospholipid, but the drug is still in the tube. Look at the numbers: the drug lengthened the screen a lot (ratio 2.27) but the confirm much less (ratio 1.34). Because the screen is affected so much more than the confirm, the result gives a falsely positive ratio and a falsely positive percentage correction. It looks exactly like phospholipid dependence, but it is the drug.

Mixing does not rescue it. DOACs characteristically show incomplete correction on mixing with normal plasma, so they mimic an inhibitor.

The rule and the error. The ISTH does not recommend lupus anticoagulant testing during DOAC treatment unless the drug is first removed or neutralised. The form said 'off anticoagulant' but she was not. That mismatch between the request and reality is a pre-analytical failure, and it is worth a mark.

Think of it like this
The dRVVT is a road that goes straight through factor Xa's town. Rivaroxaban sets up a roadblock in that town, so every car is delayed; the delay looks like a lupus anticoagulant, but it is the roadblock.
Common mistake
Trusting the request form and reporting a lupus anticoagulant without checking for a DOAC.
Remember
Rivaroxaban in the tube: any positive dRVVT is guilty until proved innocent.

What earns the marks

  • Rivaroxaban present at 178 ng/mL, near peak: result invalid
  • Mechanism: Russell's viper venom activates factor X directly; rivaroxaban inhibits factor Xa; screen prolonged concentration-dependently
  • Confirm affected less than the screen, giving falsely positive ratio and percentage correction; mixing shows incomplete correction
  • ISTH: no lupus anticoagulant testing during DOAC treatment; 'off anticoagulant' request is a pre-analytical failure
Evidence[13]Tripodi 2020[12]Devreese 2020Practical Haemostasis — dRVVT (DOAC concentration-dependent effect); DOACs (dRVVT ↑↑↑ with rivaroxaban)
Q4
Interpret the post-removal results. Give two limitations of DOAC-removal agents that temper your confidence.
4 marks

Model answer

Residual rivaroxaban below 10 ng/mL with both ratios normal (dRVVT 1.04, SCT 1.03): no evidence of a lupus anticoagulant; pre-removal positivity explained entirely by drug. Limitation 1: adsorption is incomplete at high drug concentrations (finite capacity), so a residual level must be measured to prove removal. Limitation 2: the procedure may perturb the assay: activated charcoal can adsorb other plasma constituents, shift clotting times and, in some evaluations, weaken detection of a genuine weak lupus anticoagulant (possible false negative). The 2020 ISTH guidance called DOAC adsorbents promising but needing further evaluation. The 2025 ISTH-SSC update lists DOAC removal as a strategy, but warns that removal may be incomplete and that results near the cut-off can shift in either direction. Methods must be locally validated.

Explained step by step

Reading the repeat. After the removal step, residual rivaroxaban is below 10 ng/mL, so the drug has been removed. The normalised dRVVT ratio (1.04) and the SCT ratio (1.03) are both normal. So the specimen shows no evidence of a lupus anticoagulant, and the earlier positive result is explained entirely by the drug.

Limitation 1: removal can be incomplete. The removal agents can only hold a limited amount of drug. At high drug levels, adsorption is incomplete. So removal should be proven, not assumed: measure the residual drug level (the 2025 ISTH update allows this to be limited to samples whose clotting times stay prolonged, but here it is what makes the negative result trustworthy). That is why the result below 10 ng/mL is essential, not decorative.

Limitation 2: the procedure can change the test. Activated-charcoal products can also take up other parts of the plasma. They have been reported to shift clotting times and, in some evaluations, to weaken detection of a genuine weak lupus anticoagulant. This raises the possibility of a false negative.

The official position. In 2020 the ISTH called DOAC adsorbents promising but needing further evaluation; the 2025 ISTH-SSC update lists DOAC removal as a strategy, with the caveats above. Further evaluation was asked for in both lupus anticoagulant-positive and lupus anticoagulant-negative populations. Any method must be locally validated before clinical use.

Think of it like this
A removal agent is like a sponge used to soak up a spill. If the spill is large, the sponge may not get it all; and a rough sponge may also wipe away something you wanted to keep (a weak lupus anticoagulant).
Common mistake
Assuming the removal step worked without measuring the residual drug level.
Remember
Prove the drug has gone, and remember the sponge can wipe away a weak positive.

What earns the marks

  • Residual drug below 10 ng/mL and normal ratios (1.04 and 1.03): no evidence of lupus anticoagulant
  • Pre-removal positivity explained by drug
  • Limitation 1: incomplete adsorption at high concentrations; residual level must be measured
  • Limitation 2: procedure may perturb the assay and weaken detection of a weak lupus anticoagulant (false negative); local validation needed
Evidence[13]Tripodi 2020[12]Devreese 2020Practical Haemostasis — DOACs (DOAC-Stop)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
DOAC removal: what the 2025 ISTH update adds

In 2020 the ISTH did not recommend LA testing on a DOAC unless the drug was removed or neutralised [S1]. The 2025 update keeps removal as an option but adds cautions. Pretreatment can itself change APTT and dRVVT clotting times, and removal may be incomplete, so testing before and after removal helps interpretation [S2]. If results stay positive after removal, residual DOAC cannot be ruled out [S3]. Small shifts after treatment can change the interpretation in either direction, especially near the cut-off [S4].

False negatives have been reported, but in weak positives close to the cut-off [S5]. So this patient's clearly normal post-removal ratios (1.04 and 1.03), with residual drug below 10 ng/mL, are reassuring. Persistence testing is still needed.

  1. [S1] Tripodi A, Cohen H, Devreese KMJ 2020. Lupus anticoagulant detection in anticoagulated patients. Guidance from the SSC for lupus anticoagulant/antiphospholipid antibodies of the ISTH. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:1569-1575 PMID 32619349 · doi:10.1111/jth.14846We do not recommend attempting LA detection in patients on DOACs, unless they are removed or neutralized.
  2. [S2] Devreese KMJ, et al 2025. An update on laboratory detection and interpretation of antiphospholipid antibodies for diagnosis of antiphospholipid syndrome: guidance from the ISTH-SSC. J Thromb Haemost 2025;23:731-744 PMID 39510414 · doi:10.1016/j.jtha.2024.10.022As sample pretreatment might influence clotting times of APTT and dRVVT tests, and DOAC removal may be incomplete, LA testing pre-/ postprocedure can inform the interpretation of results
  3. [S3] Devreese KMJ, et al 2025. An update on laboratory detection and interpretation of antiphospholipid antibodies for diagnosis of antiphospholipid syndrome: guidance from the ISTH-SSC. J Thromb Haemost 2025;23:731-744 PMID 39510414 · doi:10.1016/j.jtha.2024.10.022In case of persisting positive clotting test results after treatment with a DOAC removal agent, residual DOAC interference cannot be ruled out.
  4. [S4] Devreese KMJ, et al 2025. An update on laboratory detection and interpretation of antiphospholipid antibodies for diagnosis of antiphospholipid syndrome: guidance from the ISTH-SSC. J Thromb Haemost 2025;23:731-744 PMID 39510414 · doi:10.1016/j.jtha.2024.10.022Minor changes in LA assay results after adding DOAC removal agents have been observed and can lead to altered interpretation in both directions (negative to positive and vice versa), especially in samples with clotting times around the cutoff value.
  5. [S5] Tripodi A, Cohen H, Devreese KMJ 2020. Lupus anticoagulant detection in anticoagulated patients. Guidance from the SSC for lupus anticoagulant/antiphospholipid antibodies of the ISTH. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:1569-1575 PMID 32619349 · doi:10.1111/jth.14846Also, false-negative LA have been described after treatment with DOAC adsorbents,40,44 although in weak LA positive results approaching the cutoff value.
Q5
Write the final report. What further step is required before antiphospholipid syndrome can be either classified or excluded?
4 marks

Model answer

Report: original sample invalid (rivaroxaban 178 ng/mL causes false-positive phospholipid-dependent assays); after validated DOAC removal (residual below 10 ng/mL) dRVVT 1.04 and SCT 1.03, no lupus anticoagulant detected; anticardiolipin and anti-β2GPI IgG negative; a single specimen cannot classify or exclude antiphospholipid syndrome; anticoagulation must not be altered on this report. Further step: persistence, a positive result confirmed on a second sample at least 12 weeks apart; ideally off anticoagulant, or at trough with the drug level measured on the same specimen. The laboratory cannot interrupt anticoagulation; discuss with the clinician and say so in the report.

Explained step by step

The report (from the model answer). "Lupus anticoagulant testing performed on the original sample is invalid: rivaroxaban was detectable at 178 ng/mL and produces false-positive phospholipid-dependent clotting assays. Following a locally validated DOAC-removal procedure with residual drug <10 ng/mL, the normalised dRVVT ratio is 1.04 and the silica clotting time ratio 1.03 — no lupus anticoagulant detected. Anticardiolipin and anti-β2-glycoprotein I IgG are negative. This single specimen cannot classify or exclude antiphospholipid syndrome. Anticoagulation must not be altered on the basis of this report."

Why one sample is never enough. Antiphospholipid syndrome (APS) demands persistence: a positive result must be confirmed on a second sample at least 12 weeks apart. A single specimen, positive or negative, cannot classify or exclude it.

How to repeat safely. Ideally repeat when she is off anticoagulant. At a minimum, test at trough (just before the next dose) and measure the drug level on the same specimen; the ISTH warns that even low DOAC levels do not guarantee the test is free of drug effect. If stopping anticoagulation is unsafe, discuss with the clinician. The laboratory does not have the standing to interrupt anticoagulation, and the report must say this clearly.

Why this station matters. A false-positive lupus anticoagulant in a patient with unprovoked venous thromboembolism can commit her to indefinite warfarin and exclude her from DOAC therapy for life. The harm is not a wrong number; it is a wrong lifetime.

Think of it like this
One photograph of a car parked on a yellow line does not prove it is always parked there. You need a second photograph, 12 weeks later, to prove the habit (persistence).
Common mistake
Writing a definitive 'APS excluded' or 'APS confirmed' on a single sample.
Remember
APS needs two positives at least 12 weeks apart.

What earns the marks

  • Report states the original sample is invalid because of rivaroxaban
  • Post-removal results: no lupus anticoagulant detected; antibodies negative; single specimen cannot classify or exclude APS
  • Anticoagulation must not be altered on this report; laboratory cannot interrupt anticoagulation
  • Persistence: confirm on a second sample at least 12 weeks apart, ideally off anticoagulant or at trough with drug level measured
Evidence[12]Devreese 2020[13]Tripodi 2020Practical Haemostasis — DOACs
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Repeating the test safely

Antiphospholipid syndrome needs persistence, and the ISTH-SSC sets the retest interval at no less than 12 weeks [S1]. When the repeat sample is taken matters as much as how long after the first. Trough sampling on a DOAC is an accepted alternative to stopping, but the ISTH adds that even low DOAC levels do not guarantee the test is free of drug effect [S2]. So measure the drug level on the same specimen and interpret with care.

This station's error began at the request form. ISTH asks that the patient's anticoagulation be recorded on the request, as it is paramount for interpretation [S3]. Where the drug history is uncertain, a prolonged thrombin time (suggesting dabigatran or heparin) should stop LA testing unless the drug is removed [S4].

  1. [S1] Devreese KMJ, et al 2025. An update on laboratory detection and interpretation of antiphospholipid antibodies for diagnosis of antiphospholipid syndrome: guidance from the ISTH-SSC. J Thromb Haemost 2025;23:731-744 PMID 39510414 · doi:10.1016/j.jtha.2024.10.022The time frame for retesting is no less than 12 weeks, based on expert opinion [5].
  2. [S2] Devreese KMJ, et al 2025. An update on laboratory detection and interpretation of antiphospholipid antibodies for diagnosis of antiphospholipid syndrome: guidance from the ISTH-SSC. J Thromb Haemost 2025;23:731-744 PMID 39510414 · doi:10.1016/j.jtha.2024.10.022Sampling during the trough period of low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH) or DOAC is an alternative [12]. However, even with low DOAC levels, there is no guarantee of a lack of DOAC effect on LA testing [21].
  3. [S3] Tripodi A, Cohen H, Devreese KMJ 2020. Lupus anticoagulant detection in anticoagulated patients. Guidance from the SSC for lupus anticoagulant/antiphospholipid antibodies of the ISTH. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:1569-1575 PMID 32619349 · doi:10.1111/jth.14846We recommend incorporating information on the patient's anticoagulation in the request form as it is paramount for interpretation of LA results.
  4. [S4] Tripodi A, Cohen H, Devreese KMJ 2020. Lupus anticoagulant detection in anticoagulated patients. Guidance from the SSC for lupus anticoagulant/antiphospholipid antibodies of the ISTH. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:1569-1575 PMID 32619349 · doi:10.1111/jth.14846Prolonged TT (suggesting dabigatran or UFH) should be an indication to not perform LA testing, unless the drugs are removed or neutralized.

Take home from this station

  1. Always check whether the patient is on a DOAC before lupus anticoagulant testing; the request form can be wrong.
  2. Rivaroxaban blocks factor Xa and so falsely prolongs the dRVVT screen far more than the confirm, creating a false-positive lupus anticoagulant.
  3. If a DOAC-removal method is used, prove removal with a residual drug level and remember it may hide a weak lupus anticoagulant.
  4. Antiphospholipid syndrome needs persistence on two samples at least 12 weeks apart; a false positive can mean a wrong lifetime of anticoagulation.
09
Station 09 · 20 marks

An INR of 8.4 on a mechanical valve

What this station tests: Can you calculate an international normalised ratio (INR), match warfarin reversal to how urgent the situation is, and recognise that the INR is a tool made only for warfarin?

The case

A 72-year-old woman with a mechanical mitral valve prosthesis, on warfarin with a target INR of 2.5–3.5, attends the anticoagulation clinic having recently completed a course of ciprofloxacin. She has no bleeding. The laboratory reports a prothrombin time of 77.6 s. The geometric mean normal prothrombin time is 12.2 s and the thromboplastin ISI is 1.15. Six hours later she is readmitted with a reduced conscious level; computed tomography shows an acute intracerebral haemorrhage.

In plain wordsA 72-year-old woman with a mechanical mitral heart valve takes warfarin, aiming for an international normalised ratio (INR) of 2.5–3.5. After a course of the antibiotic ciprofloxacin, her INR at clinic is very high, but she is not bleeding. Six hours later she comes back drowsy, and a brain scan (computed tomography, CT) shows bleeding inside the brain. The station follows her from the clinic, through the emergency, to the question of when to restart anticoagulation.

How to read the data

  • Patient prothrombin time (PT) 77.6 s: the raw clotting time in seconds. It is more than six times the normal value, so her blood is taking far too long to clot.
  • Geometric mean normal PT 12.2 s: the laboratory's 'normal' value, worked out from at least 20 normal donors. It is the bottom line of the INR fraction, not a single normal control sample.
  • International sensitivity index (ISI) 1.15: the calibration number of this thromboplastin reagent. In the INR formula it is a power, not a multiplier.
  • Target INR 2.5–3.5: a range around a target of 3.0. BSH sets point targets by valve thrombogenicity and patient risk factors, and the mitral position counts as one, so a higher target is often used. Her INR of 8.4 is far above this range.
  • Recent ciprofloxacin: the most likely trigger. Its interaction with warfarin is well recognised and predictable.
  • No bleeding at clinic, brain bleed six hours later: the same INR needs two very different responses, depending on whether the patient is bleeding.

The five questions

Q1
Calculate the INR from the data given, showing your working. Use INR = (patient prothrombin time ÷ geometric mean normal prothrombin time)^ISI.
4 marks

Model answer

PT ratio = 77.6 ÷ 12.2 = 6.36. INR = (PT ratio)^ISI = 6.36^1.15 = 8.40, so the INR is 8.4. The denominator is the geometric mean normal PT from at least 20 normal donors, not an arithmetic mean and not a single normal control.

Explained step by step

PT ratio = 77.6 / 12.2 = 6.36
INR = (PT ratio)^ISI = 6.36^1.15 = 8.40

The prothrombin time (PT) is a clotting time measured in seconds. On its own, a PT cannot be compared between laboratories, because each laboratory uses its own thromboplastin reagent, and reagents behave differently. The international normalised ratio (INR) was built to remove that difference for patients on warfarin.

Step 1 – make a ratio. Divide the patient's PT by the laboratory's normal PT: 77.6 ÷ 12.2 = 6.36. This tells you how many times longer than normal her blood takes to clot. The 'normal' value must be the geometric mean normal PT, worked out from at least 20 normal donors. It is not a simple (arithmetic) average, and it is not one normal control sample run on the day.

Step 2 – correct for the reagent. Every thromboplastin has an international sensitivity index (ISI). The ISI is found by calibrating the reagent against a World Health Organization (WHO) reference preparation. Raising the ratio to the power of the ISI puts this laboratory's result on to the shared international scale: 6.36^1.15 = 8.40.

Because the ISI here is above 1, the INR (8.4) comes out higher than the simple ratio (6.36). Always work out the ratio first and apply the power second. Write both lines of working: the examiner gives marks for the method, not only the final number.

Think of it like this
Think of currency exchange. The PT ratio is a price in the local currency; the ISI is the conversion that turns it into one international currency, so a result from any laboratory can be compared with any other.
Common mistake
Multiplying the ratio by the ISI instead of raising it to the power of the ISI, or using a single normal control sample as the denominator.
Remember
Ratio first, then power: INR = (PT ÷ geometric mean normal PT)^ISI.

What earns the marks

  • Correct PT ratio: 77.6 ÷ 12.2 = 6.36
  • ISI applied as a power, not a multiplier: 6.36^1.15
  • Correct final answer, INR 8.4, with the working shown
  • States the denominator is the geometric mean normal PT from at least 20 normal donors, not an arithmetic mean or a single control
EvidencePractical Haemostasis — ISI & INR (geometric mean normal prothrombin time)
Q2
State the management at the time of the clinic visit, when she had an INR of 8.4 and no bleeding.
4 marks

Model answer

INR above 8.0 without bleeding: withhold warfarin and give vitamin K 1–5 mg by mouth (BSH 1B). Recheck the INR the next day. A repeat oral dose and the INR at which to restart (commonly below 5.0) follow local protocol, then restart at a reduced maintenance dose. Identify the cause: ciprofloxacin (inhibits cytochrome P450 metabolism of warfarin and displaces protein binding); also ask about reduced dietary vitamin K and adherence. Counsel, document the interaction and arrange earlier review after future antibiotics. No intravenous vitamin K and no prothrombin complex concentrate when she is not bleeding.

Explained step by step

The first question in any high INR is always: is the patient bleeding? At clinic she was not. So the aim is controlled correction – bringing the INR down safely – and not full reversal.

The steps for an INR above 8.0 with no bleeding:

  • Stop warfarin.
  • Give vitamin K 1–5 mg by mouth. Warfarin is a vitamin K antagonist, so giving vitamin K lets the liver start making working clotting factors again. By mouth is enough when there is no bleeding. This is the BSH recommendation (1B).
  • Recheck the INR the next day. Whether to repeat the vitamin K, and the INR at which to restart (commonly below 5.0), follow local protocol: BSH does not fix these.
  • Restart warfarin at a lower maintenance dose.

Why not intravenous vitamin K or prothrombin complex concentrate (PCC)? These are tools for bleeding. She is not bleeding, and she has a mechanical mitral valve, which is at very high risk of clotting if left unprotected (see question 5). Here we want a gentle, controlled fall in the INR, not a sudden switch-off of anticoagulation.

Find the cause. Ciprofloxacin is almost certainly the trigger. It blocks the liver enzymes (cytochrome P450) that break down warfarin, so warfarin builds up. It also pushes warfarin off the blood proteins that carry it, which leaves more free drug. This interaction is well recognised and predictable. Also ask whether she ate less while unwell (less vitamin K in the diet) and whether she has changed how she takes her tablets.

Stop it happening again. Explain the problem to her, record the interaction in her notes, and plan an earlier INR check after any future antibiotic course.

Think of it like this
A car is going too fast downhill. You take your foot off the accelerator and brake gently (stop warfarin, oral vitamin K). You do not pull the handbrake hard (PCC), because that can cause a different crash – a clot on the valve.
Common mistake
Giving prothrombin complex concentrate or intravenous vitamin K to a patient who is not bleeding, or forgetting to find and record the ciprofloxacin interaction.
Remember
No bleeding: withhold warfarin, oral vitamin K, recheck the INR, find the cause.

What earns the marks

  • Withhold warfarin and give oral vitamin K 1–5 mg (BSH 1B)
  • Recheck INR next day; repeat dose and restart threshold per local protocol; restart at a reduced dose
  • Identifies ciprofloxacin as the precipitant and explains the interaction; asks about diet and adherence
  • Counsel, document, earlier review after future antibiotics; no intravenous vitamin K or PCC when not bleeding
Evidence[14]Keeling 2011
Q3
State the management of the intracerebral haemorrhage. Justify your choice of blood product and explain why fresh frozen plasma is not the preferred agent.
4 marks

Model answer

Major bleeding emergency needing immediate, complete reversal. Give four-factor PCC 25–50 units/kg (dose by body weight and INR per the product specification), together with intravenous vitamin K 5 mg (PCC acts within minutes but has a shorter half-life than warfarin, so the INR rebounds without vitamin K). Recheck the INR soon after the PCC infusion and again some hours later (local protocol), because infused factor VII has a half-life of about 6 hours; urgent neurosurgical discussion; control blood pressure. Fresh frozen plasma (FFP) is not preferred: factors at physiological levels, so a large volume (typically 15–30 mL/kg) is needed; slow, incomplete correction; risk of circulatory overload in a 72-year-old, transfusion-related acute lung injury and other reactions. Reserve FFP for when PCC is unavailable.

Explained step by step

Now the situation is completely different. Bleeding inside the brain (intracerebral haemorrhage) while on warfarin is a major bleeding emergency. While she stays anticoagulated, the bleed can keep growing. The aim is now immediate and complete reversal.

Give two things together:

  • Four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate (PCC), dosed by body weight and INR according to the product's specification. It is a small volume of concentrated clotting factors, so it corrects the INR within minutes.
  • Intravenous vitamin K 5 mg. PCC has a shorter half-life than warfarin. As the PCC is cleared, the warfarin is still there, so without vitamin K the INR rises again (it 'rebounds'). Vitamin K lets the liver make its own working factors, so the correction lasts.

Then: recheck the INR soon after the infusion and again some hours later (timings by local protocol), because infused factor VII has a half-life of only about 6 hours. Discuss urgently with neurosurgery. Control the blood pressure.

Why not fresh frozen plasma (FFP)? FFP contains clotting factors at physiological levels – the same strength as normal blood, not concentrated. To correct an INR of 8.4 you need a large volume, typically 15–30 mL/kg. This causes three problems. It is slow: the plasma must be thawed and matched to the patient's blood group. It is incomplete: it corrects the INR less well. It is risky: a large volume can overload the circulation of a 72-year-old, and plasma can cause transfusion-related acute lung injury (TRALI) and other transfusion reactions.

PCC, by contrast, is a small, rapidly available, virally inactivated volume that brings the INR to normal far faster. Keep FFP for situations where PCC is not available.

Think of it like this
PCC is a fire extinguisher: it puts the fire out in minutes. Vitamin K is repairing the faulty wiring so the fire does not restart once the extinguisher is empty. FFP is a garden hose: it helps eventually, but slowly, and it floods the house.
Common mistake
Giving PCC without intravenous vitamin K, so the INR rebounds hours later, or choosing FFP as first-line reversal.
Remember
Brain bleed on warfarin: PCC for speed, vitamin K for staying power.

What earns the marks

  • Recognises a major bleed needing immediate and complete reversal
  • Four-factor PCC 25–50 units/kg, plus intravenous vitamin K 5 mg, with the rebound reason
  • Repeat INR after PCC and again later (factor VII half-life about 6 h); urgent neurosurgery; blood pressure control
  • FFP: physiological levels, large volume 15–30 mL/kg, slow and incomplete, overload and TRALI risk
Evidence[14]Keeling 2011
Evidence check verified against the sources below
PCC plus vitamin K: the BSH reasoning in three sentences

BSH recommends 25–50 units/kg four-factor PCC with 5 mg intravenous vitamin K for major bleeding [S1]. PCC can fully reverse warfarin within about 10 minutes, but the infused factors have a finite half-life, the shortest being factor VII at about 6 hours [S2]. That is why vitamin K must be given with it.

FFP is a dilute source of the same factors. It is not practical to give 15–30 mL/kg quickly, correction is inferior, and BSH does not recommend it for life-threatening bleeding [S3].

Exam point: where intracerebral bleeding is strongly suspected, BSH suggests reversing before the scan or INR result is back [S4].

  1. [S1] Keeling D, Baglin T, Tait C, et al 2011. Guidelines on oral anticoagulation with warfarin - fourth edition. Br J Haematol 2011;154:311-324 PMID 21671894 · doi:10.1111/j.1365-2141.2011.08753.xEmergency anticoagulation reversal in patients with major bleeding should be with 25-50 u/kg four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate and 5 mg intravenous vitamin K (1B).
  2. [S2] Keeling D, Baglin T, Tait C, et al 2011. Guidelines on oral anticoagulation with warfarin - fourth edition. Br J Haematol 2011;154:311-324 PMID 21671894 · doi:10.1111/j.1365-2141.2011.08753.xPCCs are able to completely reverse the warfarin-induced anticoagulation within 10 min but the infused clotting factors have a finite half-life, the shortest of which is FVII at 6 h.
  3. [S3] Keeling D, Baglin T, Tait C, et al 2011. Guidelines on oral anticoagulation with warfarin - fourth edition. Br J Haematol 2011;154:311-324 PMID 21671894 · doi:10.1111/j.1365-2141.2011.08753.xFFP provides a dilute form of the clotting factors and it is not practical to infuse very large volumes of plasma (15-30 ml/kg) rapidly. FFP will produce inferior correction and cannot be recommended for life-threatening bleeding.
  4. [S4] Keeling D, Baglin T, Tait C, et al 2011. Guidelines on oral anticoagulation with warfarin - fourth edition. Br J Haematol 2011;154:311-324 PMID 21671894 · doi:10.1111/j.1365-2141.2011.08753.xPatients on warfarin presenting with a strong suspicion of intracerebral bleed should have their anticoagulation reversed before the results of any investigations (2C).
Q4
The intensivist asks you to use the INR to grade her hepatic synthetic function. Explain why this is inappropriate.
4 marks

Model answer

The INR was developed and calibrated solely to monitor vitamin K antagonists: the ISI is derived against a WHO reference preparation using normal plasmas and plasmas from patients stably anticoagulated on oral anticoagulants, not liver disease plasmas. Liver coagulopathy is different in kind: factor V and fibrinogen fall too, with parallel falls in protein C, protein S and antithrombin, so haemostasis is rebalanced rather than simply impaired. A warfarin-derived ISI on liver plasma gives inter-laboratory variability, which matters because the INR is part of the MELD score; an ISI-liver has been proposed. Report the PT in seconds; a raised INR in liver disease does not predict bleeding and should not justify prophylactic plasma.

Explained step by step

Think of the INR as a ruler that was made for one job only: measuring the effect of vitamin K antagonists such as warfarin.

How the ruler was made. The ISI of each thromboplastin is found by calibrating it against a WHO reference preparation. The calibration uses plasma from normal people and from patients stably anticoagulated on oral anticoagulants. Plasma from patients with liver disease is not part of this. So the ISI is tuned to warfarin plasma, not to liver plasma.

Liver disease is a different kind of problem. It lowers factor V and fibrinogen as well as the vitamin K-dependent factors. At the same time, the natural anticoagulants – protein C, protein S and antithrombin – fall too. Both sides of the balance go down together, so haemostasis is rebalanced rather than simply impaired. The INR does not capture this balance.

Why this matters in practice. When a warfarin-calibrated ISI is applied to liver plasma, different laboratories give different INRs for the same sample (inter-laboratory variability). This happens exactly where the number is used to ration care: the INR is part of the Model for End-Stage Liver Disease (MELD) score, which is used to prioritise liver transplantation. That is why an ISIliver, calibrated on plasma from patients with cirrhosis, has been proposed.

What to do instead. To assess hepatic synthetic function, report the PT in seconds. Remember also that a raised INR in liver disease does not predict bleeding, and it should not be used to justify giving plasma before procedures 'just in case'.

Think of it like this
Using the INR for liver disease is like using a kitchen scale that was calibrated only for flour to weigh sugar: it gives a number, but different kitchens will get different answers, and none of them can be trusted.
Common mistake
Saying only 'the INR is affected by warfarin' without explaining the calibration problem and the rebalanced haemostasis of liver disease.
Remember
INR = warfarin ruler; liver function = PT in seconds.

What earns the marks

  • INR calibrated solely for vitamin K antagonists; ISI derived from normal and warfarin plasmas, not liver plasmas
  • Liver coagulopathy differs: low factor V and fibrinogen, parallel fall in protein C, protein S and antithrombin – rebalanced
  • Inter-laboratory variability matters because INR feeds the MELD score; ISI-liver proposed
  • Report PT in seconds; raised INR does not predict bleeding or justify prophylactic plasma
EvidencePractical Haemostasis — ISI & INR (ISI calibration against vitamin K antagonist plasmas, ISI-liver and the MELD score, using the INR for liver dysfunction is incorrect)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
The evidence behind 'the INR is a warfarin ruler'

Tripodi and colleagues showed the calibration problem directly. Thromboplastins are assigned their ISI using plasma from patients on vitamin K antagonists and from healthy subjects [S1]. When seven thromboplastins measured the same cirrhotic plasmas, the warfarin-calibrated INRs differed significantly. INRs recalibrated with cirrhotic plasmas (INRliver) did not [S2]. Because MELD uses the INR, this variation can change transplant priority.

Lisman and Porte add the clinical point: in liver disease, pro- and anticoagulant pathways fall together, so patients may be in haemostatic balance [S3]. Correcting PT before procedures with blood products is, in their view, not evidence-based [S4].

  1. [S1] Tripodi A, Chantarangkul V, Primignani M, et al 2007. The international normalized ratio calibrated for cirrhosis (INR(liver)) normalizes prothrombin time results for model for end-stage liver disease calculation. Hepatology 2007;46:520-527 PMID 17659574 · doi:10.1002/hep.21732The thromboplastins were previously calibrated by testing plasmas from patients on vitamin K antagonists and healthy subjects to assign the international sensitivity index (ISI(vka)) needed to convert PT into INR.
  2. [S2] Tripodi A, Chantarangkul V, Primignani M, et al 2007. The international normalized ratio calibrated for cirrhosis (INR(liver)) normalizes prothrombin time results for model for end-stage liver disease calculation. Hepatology 2007;46:520-527 PMID 17659574 · doi:10.1002/hep.21732The mean INR(vka) obtained with the 7 thromboplastins were significantly different (P &lt; 0.001). Conversely, the mean INR(liver) were not.
  3. [S3] Lisman T, Porte RJ 2010. Rebalanced hemostasis in patients with liver disease: evidence and clinical consequences. Blood 2010;116:878-885 PMID 20400681 · doi:10.1182/blood-2010-02-261891patients with liver disease may be in hemostatic balance as a result of concomitant changes in both pro- and antihemostatic pathways.
  4. [S4] Lisman T, Porte RJ 2010. Rebalanced hemostasis in patients with liver disease: evidence and clinical consequences. Blood 2010;116:878-885 PMID 20400681 · doi:10.1182/blood-2010-02-261891Although it is still common practice to prophylactically correct hemostatic abnormalities in patients with liver disease before invasive procedures by administration of blood products guided by the prothrombin time and platelet count, we believe that this policy is not evidence-based.
Q5
What governs the timing of restarting anticoagulation in a patient with a mechanical mitral valve, and what is the laboratory's role in the days that follow?
4 marks

Model answer

Timing balances the very high thrombotic risk of an unprotected mechanical mitral valve (the highest-risk position, higher than aortic) against haematoma expansion. Joint decision between neurology or neurosurgery, cardiology and haematology, informed by repeat imaging showing stability, the size and site of the bleed, and whether the cause is controlled. Usually withheld for a period of days, then reintroduced under close supervision, commonly with a heparin bridge, once imaging is stable. Laboratory: serial INRs to confirm reversal and detect rebound; monitor the bridging agent with anti-Xa or APTT; frequent INRs during reintroduction with a defined, probably narrowed target; actively communicate results; flag the ciprofloxacin interaction.

Explained step by step

This is a balance between two dangers. On one side, a mechanical mitral valve left without anticoagulation has a very high risk of clotting. The mitral position is the highest-risk prosthetic position, higher than the aortic. On the other side, restarting anticoagulation too early risks making the brain haematoma bigger.

Who decides, and on what? It is a joint decision between neurology or neurosurgery, cardiology and haematology. They are guided by:

  • repeat imaging showing that the bleed is stable;
  • the size and location of the haemorrhage;
  • whether the cause of the bleed has been controlled.

Usual pattern. Anticoagulation is generally withheld for a period of days. Once imaging is stable, it is reintroduced under close supervision, commonly with a heparin 'bridge' while warfarin is restarted.

The laboratory's role:

  • Serial INRs to confirm full reversal and to watch for rebound, because the warfarin effect lasts longer than the PCC.
  • Monitor the bridging heparin with an anti-Xa assay or the activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT), as appropriate.
  • Frequent INRs during warfarin reintroduction, against a defined and probably narrowed target.
  • Actively communicate results to the team, rather than just filing them.
  • Flag the ciprofloxacin interaction on her record so it is not repeated.

Think of it like this
It is like walking a tightrope between two cliffs: lean too far one way and the valve clots; lean too far the other and the brain bleeds again. The laboratory is the safety spotter, calling out every wobble early.
Common mistake
Giving a fixed number of days from memory instead of explaining the balance of risks, the team decision and the role of repeat imaging.
Remember
Valve clot versus brain bleed: imaging decides, the team agrees, the laboratory watches.

What earns the marks

  • Balance: very high valve thrombosis risk (mitral highest, higher than aortic) versus haematoma expansion
  • Joint neurology or neurosurgery, cardiology and haematology decision, guided by repeat imaging, bleed size and site, cause controlled
  • Withheld for days, then reintroduced under supervision, often with a heparin bridge, once imaging stable
  • Laboratory: serial INRs for rebound, anti-Xa or APTT for bridge, frequent INRs with a narrowed target, active communication, flag the interaction
Evidence[14]Keeling 2011
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Putting numbers on the mitral valve risk

BSH gives a way to size the thrombotic side of the balance. Without anticoagulation, the annual stroke risk is about 4% with an aortic mechanical valve [S1]. With a mitral mechanical valve it is about 12% per year, roughly 1.6 events per 1000 patients for every 5 days off anticoagulation [S2]. BSH adds that observed peri-operative rates have been about ten times these calculated figures [S3].

For intracerebral haemorrhage, the AHA/ASA summary places mechanical valves in the high-thrombotic-risk group, where early resumption of anticoagulation is rated 'reasonable' (class 2a) [S4]. That supports the station's answer: a team decision, guided by repeat imaging, not a fixed number of days.

  1. [S1] Keeling D, Baglin T, Tait C, et al 2011. Guidelines on oral anticoagulation with warfarin - fourth edition. Br J Haematol 2011;154:311-324 PMID 21671894 · doi:10.1111/j.1365-2141.2011.08753.xIf the annual risk of stroke in untreated AF or in a patient with an aortic mechanical heart valve (MHV) is 4% per annum
  2. [S2] Keeling D, Baglin T, Tait C, et al 2011. Guidelines on oral anticoagulation with warfarin - fourth edition. Br J Haematol 2011;154:311-324 PMID 21671894 · doi:10.1111/j.1365-2141.2011.08753.xFor AF with previous stroke or a mitral MHV, the figures are approximately 12% per annum or approximately 1·6 cases per 1000 patients for 5 d without anticoagulation.
  3. [S3] Keeling D, Baglin T, Tait C, et al 2011. Guidelines on oral anticoagulation with warfarin - fourth edition. Br J Haematol 2011;154:311-324 PMID 21671894 · doi:10.1111/j.1365-2141.2011.08753.xHowever, typical rates of peri-operative arterial thromboembolism that have been reported are ten times these calculated figures
  4. [S4] Greenberg SM, Ziai WC, Cordonnier C, et al 2022. 2022 Guideline for the Management of Patients With Spontaneous Intracerebral Hemorrhage (AHA/ASA). Stroke 2022;53:e282-e361 (quoted from the AHA Clinical Update slide summary held in the library) PMID 35579034 · doi:10.1161/STR.0000000000000407Early resumption of anticoagulation is reasonable (2a)

Take home from this station

  1. The INR is (patient PT ÷ geometric mean normal PT) raised to the power of the ISI; here it is 8.4.
  2. The same INR needs a different response: no bleeding means controlled correction with oral vitamin K; a brain bleed means four-factor PCC plus intravenous vitamin K 5 mg.
  3. PCC beats fresh frozen plasma for warfarin reversal because it is small in volume, fast and complete; plasma is slow, bulky and risks circulatory overload.
  4. The INR is a warfarin-only tool; to judge liver synthetic function, report the PT in seconds.
10
Station 10 · 20 marks

Three patients, three mechanisms

What this station tests: Can you tell disseminated intravascular coagulation, liver disease and vitamin K deficiency apart from the pattern of factor levels, when the screening tests all look the same?

The case

Three patients on the same medical take have a prolonged prothrombin time. Patient A has Gram-negative sepsis, patient B has ascites and spider naevi, and patient C has had six weeks of broad-spectrum antibiotics with poor oral intake following bowel surgery. The results have been anonymised and presented to you without the histories attached.

In plain wordsThree patients on the same medical take all have a prolonged prothrombin time (PT). One has Gram-negative sepsis, one has ascites (fluid in the abdomen) and spider naevi, and one has had six weeks of broad-spectrum antibiotics and poor eating after bowel surgery. You are shown only the blood results, without the stories, and must match each result to its mechanism.
Comparative coagulation profiles
TestPatient APatient BPatient CReference
Prothrombin time28 s26 s30 s11–14 s
APTT52 s46 s44 s27–35 s
Clauss fibrinogen0.9 g/L1.4 g/L3.2 g/L2.0–4.0 g/L
D-dimer8500 µg/L900 µg/L210 µg/L<500 µg/L
Platelet count42 ×10⁹/L88 ×10⁹/L260 ×10⁹/L150–400 ×10⁹/L
Factor V22 IU/dL30 IU/dL98 IU/dL50–150 IU/dL
Factor VII25 IU/dL28 IU/dL12 IU/dL50–150 IU/dL
Factor VIII45 IU/dL165 IU/dL130 IU/dL50–150 IU/dL

How to read the data

  • All three have a long PT and a long activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT). These screening tests alone cannot separate the three patients.
  • Patient A – everything used up: fibrinogen 0.9 g/L, D-dimer 8500 µg/L, platelets 42 ×10⁹/L, factor V 22 and factor VII 25 IU/dL, and factor VIII only 45 IU/dL.
  • Patient B – broad but milder fall: factor V 30 and factor VII 28 IU/dL, fibrinogen moderately low at 1.4 g/L, D-dimer mildly raised at 900 µg/L, platelets 88 ×10⁹/L – but factor VIII is high at 165 IU/dL.
  • Patient C – one isolated defect: factor VII is very low at 12 IU/dL, while factor V (98 IU/dL), fibrinogen (3.2 g/L), D-dimer (210 µg/L), platelets (260 ×10⁹/L) and factor VIII (130 IU/dL) are all normal.
  • The three columns that solve the station are factor V, factor VII and factor VIII. Read them together, not one at a time.

The five questions

Q1
Assign each patient to a mechanism and justify each assignment from the data.
4 marks

Model answer

A = disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC): full consumptive picture – fibrinogen 0.9 g/L, D-dimer 8500 µg/L, platelets 42 ×10⁹/L, low factors V and VII; factor VIII 45 IU/dL is inappropriately low for a septic acute-phase response, itself evidence of consumption. B = liver disease: factor V 30 and factor VII low (synthesis of vitamin K-dependent and independent factors impaired); fibrinogen moderately low and D-dimer mildly raised (reduced synthesis, impaired clearance); platelets 88 from hypersplenism and reduced thrombopoietin; factor VIII raised at 165 IU/dL. C = vitamin K deficiency: factor VII 12 IU/dL with factor V normal at 98 IU/dL; fibrinogen, platelets and D-dimer normal; poor intake is the main driver; antibiotics can add to it (some interfere with vitamin K recycling, and loss of gut-flora vitamin K2 may contribute).

Explained step by step

Start from first principles. A clotting factor level can fall for three different reasons:

  • it is used up faster than it is made (consumption, as in disseminated intravascular coagulation, DIC);
  • it is not made, because the liver cells are failing;
  • it is made but not finished, because the liver lacks vitamin K, the cofactor it needs to complete a step called γ-carboxylation.
Each mechanism leaves its own fingerprint on the results.

Patient A – DIC. This is the only profile with the full 'consumption' signature: low fibrinogen (0.9 g/L), very high D-dimer (8500 µg/L), low platelets (42 ×10⁹/L), and low factor V and factor VII. Factor VIII is 45 IU/dL. Sepsis is an acute-phase state, which would normally push factor VIII up. A low value here is inappropriate, and that is itself evidence that it is being consumed.

Patient B – liver disease. Factor V (30 IU/dL) is low alongside factor VII. This means synthesis is failing across both vitamin K-dependent and vitamin K-independent factors. Fibrinogen is moderately low and the D-dimer only mildly raised, consistent with reduced synthesis and impaired clearance. The platelet count of 88 ×10⁹/L fits hypersplenism (a big spleen holding on to platelets) and reduced thrombopoietin. Factor VIII is raised at 165 IU/dL – the key discriminator (see question 3).

Patient C – vitamin K deficiency. This is a selective defect: factor VII is 12 IU/dL, but factor V is entirely normal at 98 IU/dL. Fibrinogen, platelets and D-dimer are all normal, which rules out both consumption and synthetic failure. Six weeks of broad-spectrum antibiotics with poor eating is the classic setting: the diet supplies too little vitamin K and the body's small stores run out quickly. Antibiotics can add to this (some cephalosporins interfere with vitamin K recycling, and loss of gut-flora vitamin K2 may contribute), but poor intake is the main driver.

Think of it like this
Three bakeries are short of bread. In the first, a crowd is grabbing every loaf (DIC – consumption). In the second, the ovens are broken, so every kind of bread is short (liver disease). In the third, the ovens work but one ingredient is missing, so only the bread that needs that ingredient is short (vitamin K deficiency).
Common mistake
Calling patient B DIC because fibrinogen is low and D-dimer raised, without noticing the raised factor VIII and the much milder D-dimer and platelet changes.
Remember
Used up, not made, or not finished: DIC, liver, vitamin K.

What earns the marks

  • A = DIC: low fibrinogen, very high D-dimer, low platelets, low V and VII
  • A: factor VIII 45 IU/dL inappropriately low for sepsis – evidence of consumption
  • B = liver disease: low V and VII, mild changes in fibrinogen and D-dimer, platelets 88, factor VIII raised
  • C = vitamin K deficiency: VII 12 with V normal at 98; fibrinogen, platelets, D-dimer normal; antibiotics and poor intake
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays; Screening Tests
Q2
Which single pair of factor assays best separates liver disease from vitamin K deficiency, and why does it work?
4 marks

Model answer

Factor V paired with factor VII. Factor VII is vitamin K-dependent and has the shortest half-life of the procoagulant factors (four to six hours), so it falls first and furthest in both conditions. Factor V is made in the liver but is not vitamin K-dependent. Vitamin K deficiency: VII low, V normal (the problem is γ-carboxylation, not synthesis). Liver disease: both low (synthetic capacity fails and spares nothing).

Explained step by step

To separate two conditions you need one factor that falls in both, and one that falls in only one of them. Factor VII and factor V do exactly this.

Factor VII – the sensitive marker. Factor VII is vitamin K-dependent. It also has the shortest half-life of the procoagulant factors, four to six hours. So whenever supply stops, for either reason, factor VII falls first and furthest. It tells you that something is wrong, but not what.

Factor V – the control. Factor V is made in the liver but does not need vitamin K. It only falls if the liver itself cannot make proteins.

Put the two together:

  • Vitamin K deficiency: factor VII low, factor V normal. The liver is working; it simply cannot finish the γ-carboxylation step. Patient C: VII 12, V 98.
  • Liver disease: both low. The factory itself is failing and spares nothing. Patient B: VII 28, V 30.

One caution: in DIC both factors are also low, because both are being consumed (patient A). That is why you also need factor VIII and the consumption markers (fibrinogen, D-dimer, platelets) to pick out DIC – see question 3.

Think of it like this
Factor V is a warning light on the factory itself. If factor VII is low but the factor V light is still on, the factory is working and only the vitamin K 'finishing tool' is missing. If both lights are off, the whole factory is down.
Common mistake
Choosing factor VIII or fibrinogen as the pair, or forgetting to say that factor V is not vitamin K-dependent, which is the whole reason the pair works.
Remember
Low VII, normal V: vitamin K. Low VII, low V: liver.

What earns the marks

  • Names the pair: factor V and factor VII
  • Factor VII: vitamin K-dependent, shortest half-life (four to six hours), falls first in both
  • Factor V: made in the liver but not vitamin K-dependent
  • Vitamin K deficiency = VII low, V normal; liver disease = both low
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays (vitamin K-dependent factor behaviour)
Q3
Explain why factor VIII is raised rather than reduced in patient B.
4 marks

Model answer

Factor VIII is not made by hepatocytes; it is produced principally by liver sinusoidal endothelial cells and endothelium elsewhere, so hepatocellular failure does not reduce it. Factor VIII is also an acute-phase protein, and in liver disease its clearance is reduced while von Willebrand factor, which stabilises it, rises. So: low factor VIII in a coagulopathic patient argues for consumption; normal or raised VIII with low V points to liver disease; both low points to DIC.

Explained step by step

At first this looks strange: the liver is failing, yet factor VIII is high. The answer lies in which cells make factor VIII.

Not the hepatocytes. Most clotting factors are made by hepatocytes, the main working cells of the liver. Factor VIII is different. It is made mainly by liver sinusoidal endothelial cells – the cells lining the small blood channels inside the liver – and by endothelium (blood vessel lining) elsewhere in the body. So when the hepatocytes fail, factor VIII production carries on.

Two further pushes upwards:

  • Factor VIII is an acute-phase protein, so illness and inflammation raise it.
  • In liver disease its clearance is reduced, and von Willebrand factor, which protects and stabilises factor VIII in the circulation, rises. More protection plus less removal means a higher level.

Why this matters at the bench. A low factor VIII in a patient with a coagulopathy argues for consumption, not liver failure. Use it with factor V:

  • factor VIII normal or raised + factor V low → liver disease (patient B: VIII 165, V 30);
  • factor VIII low + factor V low → DIC (patient A: VIII 45, V 22).

Think of it like this
Factor VIII is made in a separate workshop from the other clotting factors. When the main factory (the hepatocytes) breaks down, the workshop keeps running – and its products pile up because the collection lorry that removes them comes less often.
Common mistake
Saying factor VIII is 'made outside the liver' – the main source is liver sinusoidal endothelial cells, which are inside the liver but are not hepatocytes.
Remember
Liver fails, VIII stays: low VIII means consumption.

What earns the marks

  • Factor VIII is not made by hepatocytes
  • Made principally by liver sinusoidal endothelial cells and other endothelium
  • Acute-phase protein; reduced clearance in liver disease; raised von Willebrand factor stabilises it
  • Practical use: low VIII suggests consumption (DIC); normal or high VIII with low V suggests liver disease
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays (factor VIII as an acute-phase protein)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Where factor VIII comes from, and when the rule breaks

When human liver cells are separated, factor VIII activity is found in the sinusoidal endothelial cells but not in hepatocytes [S1]. In mice, deleting the factor VIII gene in endothelium alone removes all detectable plasma factor VIII [S2]. In cirrhosis, the high plasma factor VIII is explained by more von Willebrand factor and less of the clearance receptor LRP, not by extra factor VIII synthesis [S3]. A textbook comparison table records factor levels in DIC as reduced for all factors including VIII [S4].

The trap: advanced liver disease that decompensates, often with infection, can itself trigger DIC. It then becomes hard to tell whether falling factors reflect consumption or reduced synthesis [S5]. Use factor VIII as one clue within the whole picture, not as a stand-alone test.

  1. [S1] Shahani T et al. 2014. Human liver sinusoidal endothelial cells but not hepatocytes contain factor VIII. J Thromb Haemost 2014;12:36-42 PMID 24118899 · doi:10.1111/jth.12412Our data demonstrate that LSECs, but not hepatocytes, contain measurable amounts of FVIII:C, and suggest that the former are the main cells producing FVIII in the human liver.
  2. [S2] Fahs SA et al. 2014. A conditional knockout mouse model reveals endothelial cells as the principal and possibly exclusive source of plasma factor VIII. Blood 2014;123:3706-3713 PMID 24705491 · doi:10.1182/blood-2014-02-555151efficient endothelial-KO models display a severe hemophilic phenotype with no detectable plasma FVIII activity
  3. [S3] Hollestelle MJ et al. 2004. Factor VIII expression in liver disease. Thromb Haemost 2004;91:267-275 PMID 14961153 · doi:10.1160/TH03-05-0310elevated plasma FVIII levels in liver cirrhosis are associated with increased hepatic biosynthesis of VWF and decreased expression of LRP, rather than increased FVIII synthesis
  4. [S4] Edwards PT, Miloh T, Soundar EP, Teruya J 2021. Bleeding in Acute and Chronic Liver Disease. In: Management of Bleeding Patients, 2nd ed. Springer 2021:157-163 (book chapter) doi:10.1007/978-3-030-56338-7_14↓All factors including VIII
  5. [S5] Eby C 2021. Bleeding Associated with Disseminated Intravascular Coagulation. In: Management of Bleeding Patients, 2nd ed. Springer 2021 (book chapter) doi:10.1007/978-3-030-56338-7_17it is difficult to interpret whether worsening deficiencies of coagulation factors and regulatory proteins are due to increased consumption due to DIC or decreased synthesis
Q4
Calculate the ISTH disseminated intravascular coagulation score for patient A and state the interpretation. For this laboratory, a D-dimer of 500–5,000 µg/L is a moderate increase and above 5,000 µg/L a strong increase.
4 marks

Model answer

Platelets 42 ×10⁹/L (<50) = 2; D-dimer 8500 µg/L (strong rise) = 3; PT prolongation 28 − 12.5 ≈ 15.5 s (>6 s) = 2; fibrinogen 0.9 g/L (<1.0 g/L) = 1. Total 8. A score of 5 or more is compatible with overt DIC. Repeat daily to track the trend; the score is validated only when an underlying disorder known to be associated with DIC is present – here, sepsis.

Explained step by step

Platelet count   42 ×10⁹/L        (<50)       → 2
Fibrin marker    D-dimer 8500      (strong rise)  → 3
PT prolongation  28 − 12.5 ≈ 15.5s (>6 s)        → 2
Fibrinogen       0.9 g/L           (<1.0 g/L)  → 1
                                          TOTAL     8

The International Society on Thrombosis and Haemostasis (ISTH) score turns four simple laboratory results into points. You add up the points and compare the total with a cut-off.

Work through patient A one test at a time:

  • Platelets 42 ×10⁹/L – below 50, so 2 points.
  • Fibrin marker: D-dimer 8500 µg/L – above 5,000 µg/L, which this laboratory defines as a strong increase, so 3 points.
  • PT prolongation – the normal PT is taken as 12.5 s, the middle of the 11–14 s reference range. 28 − 12.5 = 15.5 s, which is more than 6 s, so 2 points. (Even if you used the top of the range, 14 s, the prolongation would still be well over 6 s.)
  • Fibrinogen 0.9 g/L – below 1.0 g/L, so 1 point.
Total = 2 + 3 + 2 + 1 = 8.

Interpretation. Any score of 5 or more is compatible with overt DIC. Patient A scores 8.

Two rules that earn extra credit:

  • Repeat the score daily. It is designed to follow the trend over time, not to be used once.
  • Use it only when there is an underlying disorder known to cause DIC. The score is validated only in that setting. Here the Gram-negative sepsis supplies it.

Think of it like this
It works like an early-warning score on the ward: several simple observations each add points, a high total raises the alarm, and you repeat it every day to see whether the patient is getting better or worse.
Common mistake
Scoring the raw PT of 28 s instead of the prolongation over normal, or forgetting that the score needs an underlying disorder before it can be applied.
Remember
Platelets, D-dimer, PT, fibrinogen: 5 or more means overt DIC – and score again tomorrow.

What earns the marks

  • Correct points for each item: platelets 2, D-dimer 3, PT 2, fibrinogen 1
  • Correct total of 8, with working shown
  • Interpretation: 5 or more is compatible with overt DIC
  • Repeat daily; valid only with an underlying DIC-associated disorder (sepsis here)
Evidence[16]Taylor 2001
Evidence check verified against the sources below
The 2025 ISTH update to the overt DIC score

The station uses the 2001 ISTH score, which grades the fibrin marker only as no, moderate or strong increase and leaves each laboratory to set its own cut-offs [S1]. The score may be used only when an underlying disorder known to cause DIC is present [S1].

In 2025 the ISTH DIC subcommittee proposed fixed D-dimer cut-offs: more than 3 times the upper normal limit scores 2, and more than 7 times scores 3 [S2]. Platelet count, fibrinogen and PT items are unchanged [S3]. Patient A still scores 3 for D-dimer provided the result exceeds seven times the local upper limit, so the total is unchanged.

Exam point: the subcommittee warns that D-dimer assays are not standardised, so results from different methods are hard to compare [S4]. Always quote the local upper limit when you score.

  1. [S1] Levi M, Toh CH, Thachil J, Watson HG 2009. Guidelines for the diagnosis and management of disseminated intravascular coagulation (BCSH). Br J Haematol 2009;145:24-33 PMID 19222477 · doi:10.1111/j.1365-2141.2009.07600.xThe presence of an underlying disorder known to be associated with DIC is a prerequisite for the use of the algorithm.
  2. [S2] Iba T et al. 2025. Updated definition and scoring of disseminated intravascular coagulation in 2025: communication from the ISTH SSC Subcommittee on DIC. J Thromb Haemost 2025;23:2356-2362 PMID 40216223 · doi:10.1016/j.jtha.2025.03.038we propose that D-dimer be used in the overt DIC score with values of >3 times the upper normal limit be assigned 2 points and >7 times the upper normal limit be assigned 3 points
  3. [S3] Iba T et al. 2025. Updated definition and scoring of disseminated intravascular coagulation in 2025: communication from the ISTH SSC Subcommittee on DIC. J Thromb Haemost 2025;23:2356-2362 PMID 40216223 · doi:10.1016/j.jtha.2025.03.038The other clinical laboratory tests of platelet count, fibrinogen, and prothrombin remain the same.
  4. [S4] Iba T et al. 2025. Updated definition and scoring of disseminated intravascular coagulation in 2025: communication from the ISTH SSC Subcommittee on DIC. J Thromb Haemost 2025;23:2356-2362 PMID 40216223 · doi:10.1016/j.jtha.2025.03.038Different assays and laboratory methods can give variable D-dimer results, making comparisons difficult.
Q5
What single therapeutic intervention confirms the diagnosis in patient C, and how and when would you demonstrate the response?
4 marks

Model answer

Vitamin K 10 mg intravenously (not subcutaneously: absorption is unpredictable). It treats and confirms: the PT shows significant correction within 6–8 hours of intravenous vitamin K and is usually corrected within 24 hours, because the hepatocyte is intact and needs only the cofactor to complete γ-carboxylation. Show the response with repeat PT and factor VII at those times. Failure to correct after adequate parenteral vitamin K indicates hepatocellular disease and should redirect the workup. If bleeding needs immediate reversal, give prothrombin complex concentrate with vitamin K, but take the confirmatory samples first. PIVKA-II (des-γ-carboxy prothrombin), where available, is a sensitive marker of vitamin K deficiency, but it is not specific: it can also be raised in liver disease and hepatocellular carcinoma, so read it alongside a normal factor V.

Explained step by step

In patient C, the liver cells are healthy. They are simply missing the vitamin K they need to finish making some clotting factors (the γ-carboxylation step). So the single intervention is to give vitamin K, 10 mg, intravenously. The subcutaneous route is avoided because absorption is unpredictable.

The treatment is also the test. If the problem really is a lack of vitamin K, the intact liver starts finishing its factors as soon as the cofactor arrives. The PT shows significant correction within 6–8 hours of intravenous vitamin K and is usually corrected within 24 hours.

How to show it: repeat the PT and factor VII at those time points. Factor VII is the best factor to follow because it fell first and furthest (question 2).

If it does not correct: failure to correct after adequate vitamin K given by injection (parenteral) means the liver cells themselves are failing. That is hepatocellular disease, not deficiency, and the workup should change direction.

Two practical points:

  • If she is bleeding and needs immediate reversal, give prothrombin complex concentrate (PCC) alongside vitamin K. But PCC corrects the result straight away and destroys the diagnostic information, so take the confirmatory samples first.
  • Where available, PIVKA-II (protein induced by vitamin K absence-II, also called des-γ-carboxy prothrombin) confirms the diagnosis directly: it is the unfinished form of prothrombin.

Think of it like this
A car will not start. If you put in fuel and it starts at once, the engine was fine and it was only out of fuel (vitamin K deficiency). If it still will not start after a full tank, the engine itself is broken (liver disease).
Common mistake
Giving prothrombin complex concentrate or plasma before taking samples, which corrects the result and removes the diagnostic evidence.
Remember
Vitamin K is both the cure and the test: watch the PT and factor VII at 6–8 and 24 hours.

What earns the marks

  • Vitamin K 10 mg intravenously
  • PT shows significant correction within 6–8 hours and is usually corrected within 24 hours
  • Demonstrate with repeat PT and factor VII at those intervals
  • Failure to correct suggests hepatocellular disease; if PCC needed, take samples first; PIVKA-II confirmatory
EvidencePractical Haemostasis — Screening Tests; 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays (vitamin K-dependent factor behaviour)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
How fast vitamin K works, and why the route matters

UK warfarin guidance states that significant correction of the INR is seen within 6–8 hours of intravenous vitamin K, and that vitamin K should not be given subcutaneously because correction is inconsistent [S1]. A comparative study confirmed faster correction after intravenous than oral vitamin K, with each factor recovering in line with its own half-life, so factor VII recovers first [S2].

Why so fast? Full correction within a day is too quick for new protein synthesis; hepatocytes are thought to hold under-carboxylated precursors that are finished and released as soon as vitamin K arrives [S3]. That is what makes vitamin K a diagnostic test as well as a treatment. In advanced liver disease, by contrast, vitamin K rarely shortens a prolonged PT [S4], so a poor response should redirect the work-up.

  1. [S1] Keeling D et al. 2011. Guidelines on oral anticoagulation with warfarin – fourth edition (BCSH). Br J Haematol 2011;154:311-324 PMID 21671894 · doi:10.1111/j.1365-2141.2011.08753.xSignificant correction of the INR is seen within 6–8 h after intravenous vitamin K use (Watson et al, 2001). Vitamin K should not be given subcutaneously due to inconsistent correction
  2. [S2] Watson HG et al. 2001. A comparison of the efficacy and rate of response to oral and intravenous Vitamin K in reversal of over-anticoagulation with warfarin. Br J Haematol 2001;115:145-149 PMID 11722425 · doi:10.1046/j.1365-2141.2001.03070.xAdministration of Vitamin K by either route was accompanied by changes in the activities of the Vitamin K-dependent clotting factors that reflected their respective biological half-lives.
  3. [S3] Eby C 2021. Bleeding and Vitamin K Deficiency. In: Management of Bleeding Patients, 2nd ed. Springer 2021:187-192 (book chapter) doi:10.1007/978-3-030-56338-7_18The pharmacokinetics of replenishment of fully gamma-carboxylated factors X, IX, VII, and II are not consistent with de novo protein synthesis which would take several days.
  4. [S4] Eby C 2021. Bleeding and Vitamin K Deficiency. In: Management of Bleeding Patients, 2nd ed. Springer 2021:187-192 (book chapter) doi:10.1007/978-3-030-56338-7_18vitamin K1 supplements rarely shorten a prolonged PT/INR in patients with advanced liver disease

Take home from this station

  1. In this station, a long PT has three mechanisms: factors used up (DIC), not made (liver disease) or made but not finished (vitamin K deficiency).
  2. Factor V with factor VII separates liver disease from vitamin K deficiency, because factor V is made in the liver but does not need vitamin K.
  3. Factor VIII is not made by hepatocytes, so it stays normal or rises in liver disease; a low factor VIII points to consumption.
  4. The ISTH DIC score needs an underlying cause, is positive at 5 or more, and should be repeated daily.
11
Station 11 · 20 marks

The patient who will not anticoagulate

What this station tests: Do you know when the activated partial thromboplastin time misleads heparin monitoring, and how acute illness and anticoagulant drugs distort antithrombin results?

The case

A 44-year-old woman with extensive bilateral pulmonary emboli and pelvic sepsis is receiving intravenous unfractionated heparin. The dose has been escalated to 35,000 units per 24 hours and the activated partial thromboplastin time remains below target. A simultaneous anti-Xa level is drawn from a peripheral vein.

In plain wordsA 44-year-old woman has large clots in both lungs (bilateral pulmonary emboli) and an infection in the pelvis (pelvic sepsis). She is on intravenous unfractionated heparin (UFH). The dose has been pushed up to 35,000 units a day, but the activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) is still below target. An anti-Xa level, taken at the same time from a peripheral vein, tells a different story.
Anticoagulation and related assays
TestResultTarget / reference
APTT ratio1.41.5–2.5
Anti-Xa, unfractionated-heparin calibrated0.62 U/mL0.30–0.70 U/mL
Factor VIII310 IU/dL50–150 IU/dL
Clauss fibrinogen6.8 g/L2.0–4.0 g/L
C-reactive protein180 mg/L<5 mg/L
Antithrombin activity48 IU/dL80–120 IU/dL

How to read the data

  • APTT ratio 1.4 (target 1.5–2.5): just below target. On its own it suggests too little heparin.
  • Anti-Xa, UFH-calibrated, 0.62 U/mL (target 0.30–0.70): inside the target range. By this measure, the heparin is working.
  • Factor VIII 310 IU/dL and fibrinogen 6.8 g/L, with C-reactive protein (CRP) 180 mg/L: a strong acute-phase response driven by sepsis. A very high factor VIII shortens the APTT.
  • Antithrombin 48 IU/dL (reference 80–120): low, but measured during acute thrombosis, sepsis and heparin treatment, so acquired causes must be considered first.
  • Simultaneous sample from a peripheral vein: the two tests were taken at the same moment, so the discordance is real and not a timing difference.

The five questions

Q1
Explain the discordance between the activated partial thromboplastin time and the anti-Xa level.
4 marks

Model answer

The APTT is artefactually shortened by the acute-phase response, so it under-represents true anticoagulation. Factor VIII 310 IU/dL and fibrinogen 6.8 g/L are driven by sepsis (CRP 180 mg/L); factor VIII is a major determinant of the APTT, and a markedly raised level shortens it, masking adequate heparin effect. The anti-Xa assay measures heparin-mediated inhibition of factor Xa directly and is not affected by factor VIII or fibrinogen; at 0.62 U/mL she is therapeutic. There is no true pharmacological heparin resistance: the drug is working and the wrong instrument is being read.

Explained step by step

Two tests give two different answers. The APTT says 'not enough heparin'. The anti-Xa says 'just right'. To decide which to believe, ask what each test actually measures.

The APTT is a clotting time. It depends on the heparin, but also on the patient's own clotting factors. Factor VIII is one of the major factors that sets the APTT. When factor VIII is very high – here 310 IU/dL – the plasma clots faster and the APTT becomes shorter. Her sepsis (CRP 180 mg/L) has driven this acute-phase rise, together with a fibrinogen of 6.8 g/L.

So in her plasma, heparin pushes the APTT longer while the high factor VIII pulls it shorter. The result looks 'too short' even though she has enough heparin. The APTT is artefactually shortened and under-represents her true anticoagulation.

The anti-Xa assay measures the drug's effect directly. It measures how much heparin is inhibiting factor Xa. It is not affected by factor VIII or fibrinogen. Her level of 0.62 U/mL lies within 0.30–0.70 U/mL, so she is therapeutically anticoagulated.

Conclusion: this is not true heparin resistance in the pharmacological sense. The drug is working; the wrong instrument is being read.

Think of it like this
A car's speedometer reads too low because oversized tyres have been fitted. The car is travelling at the right speed, but the gauge says 'too slow'. If you keep pressing the accelerator to make the gauge look right, you crash.
Common mistake
Calling this heparin resistance and recommending more heparin, without noticing the raised factor VIII and the therapeutic anti-Xa.
Remember
High factor VIII fools the APTT, not the anti-Xa.

What earns the marks

  • APTT artefactually shortened by the acute-phase response
  • Factor VIII 310 IU/dL (and fibrinogen 6.8 g/L) from sepsis; high factor VIII shortens the APTT
