  • Anti-Xa measures heparin inhibition of factor Xa directly, unaffected by factor VIII or fibrinogen; 0.62 U/mL is therapeutic
  • Not true pharmacological heparin resistance: the drug works, the wrong test is being read
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — Anti-Xa Assays (APTT under- and over-estimation of heparin effect)
Q2
Which result should guide dosing in this patient, and why?
4 marks

Model answer

The anti-Xa level. It should guide dosing whenever the APTT is unreliable: raised factor VIII, a prolonged APTT at baseline before heparin, a lupus anticoagulant or an inherited factor deficiency. Escalating the dose to chase an APTT ratio of 1.5–2.5 here would cause genuine over-anticoagulation and bleeding. Provisos: the assay must be calibrated for unfractionated heparin (curves are not interchangeable between heparins); and check whether the local assay adds exogenous antithrombin – if so, it reports heparin activity her own plasma cannot deliver and masks the antithrombin deficiency.

Explained step by step

Use the anti-Xa level. It measures the drug's effect directly, and in this patient the APTT has been shown to be misleading.

When is the APTT unreliable? The anti-Xa should guide dosing in any of these situations:

  • a raised factor VIII (as here);
  • an APTT that was already prolonged at baseline, before heparin started;
  • a lupus anticoagulant;
  • an inherited factor deficiency.

Why it matters for safety. She is already therapeutic by anti-Xa. If the team keeps raising the dose to push the APTT ratio into 1.5–2.5, she will become genuinely over-anticoagulated and may bleed.

Two provisos.

  • Right calibration. The anti-Xa assay must be calibrated for unfractionated heparin. Calibration curves are not interchangeable between different heparins.
  • Does the kit add antithrombin? Heparin works through antithrombin, and hers is low (48 IU/dL). Some anti-Xa assays add extra (exogenous) antithrombin to the test. Such an assay supplies what her plasma lacks, so it reports heparin activity that her own plasma cannot actually deliver – and it hides the antithrombin deficiency completely. Check what your local assay does.

Think of it like this
Testing a car's engine on a laboratory bench with fresh fuel from the workshop: the engine performs well on the bench, but on the road, with the poor fuel in the car's own tank, it may not. An assay that adds antithrombin is that bench test.
Common mistake
Choosing the anti-Xa without the provisos: not checking that it is calibrated for unfractionated heparin, or not asking whether the kit adds antithrombin.
Remember
When the APTT is unreliable, dose by anti-Xa – but know your kit.

What earns the marks

  • Anti-Xa should guide dosing
  • Lists situations where APTT is unreliable: raised factor VIII, baseline prolonged APTT, lupus anticoagulant, inherited factor deficiency
  • Chasing the APTT would cause over-anticoagulation and bleeding
  • Provisos: UFH-specific calibration; check whether the assay adds exogenous antithrombin
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — Anti-Xa Assays (drug-specific calibration, causes of sub- and supra-therapeutic levels)
Q3
Does an antithrombin activity of 48 IU/dL establish congenital deficiency? List the acquired causes operating here and state how and when you would confirm a congenital defect.
4 marks

Model answer

No. Acquired causes are operating and are far more common: consumption in acute extensive thrombosis; sepsis with capillary leak and increased consumption; and heparin therapy, which lowers antithrombin (by about 0.3 U/mL, roughly 30%, in the classic study, recovering within 2–3 days of stopping). Other acquired causes: liver disease, nephrotic syndrome, DIC, asparaginase, oestrogens, pregnancy. Confirm when well, after at least 3 months of anticoagulation for the acute event, off heparin and not on an anticoagulant that interferes with the chosen assay, and confirm any low result on a second sample: functional (chromogenic activity) assay first; if low, antigen assay to separate type I (both low) from type II (activity low, antigen normal), with subtyping (reactive-site, heparin-binding-site, pleiotropic); family studies and, where indicated, SERPINC1 sequencing. Never label lifelong thrombophilia from an acute-phase sample.

Explained step by step

No. An antithrombin of 48 IU/dL measured during acute illness cannot prove a congenital (inherited) deficiency. Several acquired causes are acting on her at the same time, and acquired causes are far more common.

Acquired causes operating here:

  • Consumption in acute, extensive thrombosis – antithrombin is used up by the ongoing clotting.
  • Sepsis – capillary leak and increased consumption.
  • Heparin therapy itself – heparin itself lowers antithrombin, by about 0.3 U/mL (roughly 30%) in the classic study, and levels recover 2–3 days after stopping.
Other general acquired causes to list: liver disease, nephrotic syndrome, disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC), asparaginase, oestrogens and pregnancy.

When to retest. Wait until she is well, off heparin and off any anticoagulant that interferes with the assay – after at least 3 months of anticoagulation for the acute event.

How to confirm, step by step:

  • Functional assay first (chromogenic activity).
  • If activity is low, add an antigen assay. Type I (quantitative) deficiency: activity and antigen both low – too little protein. Type II (qualitative) deficiency: activity low, antigen normal – enough protein, but it does not work. Type II is subtyped into reactive-site, heparin-binding-site or pleiotropic defects.
  • Confirm with family studies and, where indicated, sequencing of the antithrombin gene, SERPINC1.

Golden rule: never give anyone a lifelong thrombophilia label from a sample taken in the acute phase.

Think of it like this
Measuring a bank balance on the day of a big emergency spend: the balance is low because money is going out fast, not because the person is poor. Check again on a normal day before deciding.
Common mistake
Labelling her with congenital antithrombin deficiency on an acute sample, or forgetting that heparin itself lowers antithrombin.
Remember
Low antithrombin in acute thrombosis on heparin: acquired until proven otherwise – retest when well.

What earns the marks

  • No – acute-phase sample cannot establish congenital deficiency
  • Acquired causes here: consumption in extensive thrombosis, sepsis, heparin (lowers it by about 30%, recovering 2–3 days after stopping); plus general list
  • Retest when well, after at least 3 months, off heparin; confirm a low result on a second sample
  • Functional assay, then antigen (type I vs type II and subtypes), family studies, SERPINC1 sequencing
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — Antithrombin Assays
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Why one low antithrombin in hospital proves little

In the classic study of continuous intravenous heparin, antithrombin fell progressively and returned to normal two to three days after heparin was stopped [S1]. UK guidance lists liver disease, DIC, nephrotic syndrome, severe sepsis, recent thrombosis, heparin therapy and asparaginase as causes of a low antithrombin [S2], and advises testing for deficiencies only after 3 months of anticoagulation for an acute thrombosis [S3]. A low result must be confirmed on two or more separate samples before a deficiency is diagnosed [S4].

The sequence is therefore: wait; retest with an activity assay that the current drug does not affect; confirm on a second sample; then use antigen testing and, where it will change management, gene analysis.

  1. [S1] Marciniak E, Gockerman JP 1977. Heparin-induced decrease in circulating antithrombin-III. Lancet 1977;2:581-584 PMID 71399 · doi:10.1016/s0140-6736(77)91429-5Plasma-AT-III returned to normal two to three days after heparin was stopped.
  2. [S2] Arachchillage DJ et al. 2022. Thrombophilia testing: A British Society for Haematology guideline. Br J Haematol 2022;198:443-458 PMID 35645034 · doi:10.1111/bjh.18239Liver disease Disseminated intravascular coagulation Nephrotic syndrome Severe sepsis Recent thrombosis Heparin therapy L-asparaginase therapy
  3. [S3] Arachchillage DJ et al. 2022. Thrombophilia testing: A British Society for Haematology guideline. Br J Haematol 2022;198:443-458 PMID 35645034 · doi:10.1111/bjh.18239Testing for deficiencies of physiological anticoagulants should be performed only after 3 months of anticoagulation for acute thrombosis (Grade 2B).
  4. [S4] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776A reduction in antithrombin, PC or PS should be confirmed on two or more separate samples.
Q4
She later transitions to rivaroxaban. A repeat antithrombin activity performed on a factor-Xa-based method reports 92 IU/dL. Explain this result. Which method should have been used?
4 marks

Model answer

Falsely normal. Rivaroxaban is a direct factor Xa inhibitor; in a factor-Xa-based antithrombin assay, residual factor Xa is the readout. The drug inhibits factor Xa independently of antithrombin, so less residual factor Xa is detected and the assay credits antithrombin – antithrombin is over-estimated and true deficiency can be missed. A thrombin-based (anti-IIa) method using bovine thrombin should have been used (human-thrombin assays are not recommended because heparin cofactor II interferes); alternatively, test after the drug has been removed from the sample with a DOAC-removal agent. Rule: anti-IIa (bovine thrombin) method with anti-Xa drugs, anti-Xa method with direct thrombin inhibitors such as dabigatran – choose the substrate the drug does not act on. Best practice: avoid thrombophilia testing on a direct oral anticoagulant (DOAC); if unavoidable, state the drug and method on the report.

Explained step by step

How a factor-Xa-based antithrombin assay works. Factor Xa is added to the patient's plasma. The antithrombin in the plasma neutralises some of it. The machine then measures the factor Xa that is left over (the residual factor Xa). The less factor Xa left over, the more antithrombin the machine assumes is present.

What rivaroxaban does to this. Rivaroxaban is a direct oral anticoagulant (DOAC) that blocks factor Xa directly, without needing antithrombin. In the test tube it removes factor Xa too. The machine cannot tell who did the work, so it credits all of it to antithrombin. Antithrombin is over-estimated, and the result of 92 IU/dL is falsely normal. A true deficiency can be missed entirely.

The correct method: a thrombin-based (anti-IIa) antithrombin assay. Rivaroxaban does not act on thrombin, so it does not interfere.

Learn the rule as a pair:

  • anti-Xa drug (such as rivaroxaban) → use an anti-IIa method;
  • direct thrombin inhibitor (such as dabigatran) → use an anti-Xa method.
Always choose the substrate the drug does not act on. Best practice is to avoid thrombophilia testing on DOAC therapy altogether. If it cannot be avoided, write the drug and the method on the report.

Why it matters clinically. The falsely normal 92 IU/dL could be used to discharge a woman who genuinely has congenital antithrombin deficiency from follow-up. She would lose family screening and pregnancy planning – both important for a woman of 44.

Think of it like this
You are counting how many mice a cat catches by counting the mice left in the room. If someone secretly sets mousetraps as well, fewer mice are left, and you wrongly credit the cat. Rivaroxaban is the hidden mousetrap; antithrombin is the cat.
Common mistake
Accepting 92 IU/dL as proof that the earlier low result was purely acquired, without recognising the rivaroxaban interference.
Remember
Pick the substrate the drug does not touch: anti-Xa drug, anti-IIa assay.

What earns the marks

  • Result is falsely normal: antithrombin over-estimated
  • Mechanism: residual factor Xa is the readout; rivaroxaban inhibits factor Xa independently of antithrombin
  • Correct method: thrombin-based (anti-IIa) assay; symmetrical rule with dabigatran and anti-Xa method
  • Avoid thrombophilia testing on DOACs or state drug and method; harm: missed true deficiency
Evidence[7]Baker 2020Practical Haemostasis — DOACs (antithrombin substrate interference); Antithrombin Assays
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Choosing the antithrombin assay when the patient is on a DOAC

Antithrombin activity is measured by incubating plasma and heparin with thrombin (factor IIa) or factor Xa and reading the enzyme left over [S1]. A drug that inhibits the same enzyme is mistaken for antithrombin: direct thrombin inhibitors falsely raise IIa-based results, and direct Xa inhibitors falsely raise Xa-based results [S2]. The ISTH lists argatroban, bivalirudin and dabigatran for IIa-based assays, and rivaroxaban, apixaban and edoxaban for Xa-based assays [S3].

The UK nuance: assays using human thrombin are not recommended because heparin cofactor II interferes [S4], and bovine thrombin or Xa-based assays should be used instead [S5]. For a patient on rivaroxaban the answer is therefore a bovine-thrombin assay, or drug-removal pretreatment, and the laboratory must know which enzyme its own kit uses [S6].

  1. [S1] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776Thrombin (FIIa) or FXa is incubated with diluted plasma and heparin, and residual enzyme activity is measured using a chromogenic substrate.
  2. [S2] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776Antithrombin may be overestimated in FIIa-based assays in the presence of DTI, and by FXa-based assays in the presence of DFXaI.
  3. [S3] Van Cott EM et al. 2020. Recommendations for clinical laboratory testing for antithrombin deficiency; Communication from the SSC of the ISTH. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:17-22 PMID 31894660 · doi:10.1111/jth.14648falsely normal/elevated results (e.g. argatroban, bivalirudin, dabigatran in factor IIa-based assays; rivaroxaban, apixaban, edoxaban, but not betrixaban in Xa-based assays)
  4. [S4] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776Heparin cofactor II influences human FIIa-based assays and therefore these are not recommended.
  5. [S5] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776Bovine FIIa and human or bovine FXa-based assays should be used.
  6. [S6] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776Laboratories must be aware of the target enzyme used in the local AT activity assay (Xa or IIa) and if patients are on DOACs before testing.
Q5
Set out the management of heparin resistance in this patient.
4 marks

Model answer

First establish whether resistance is real: here it is not – the anti-Xa is therapeutic – so stop escalating, switch monitoring to anti-Xa, maintain the current dose and document why the APTT is not being followed. If resistance is genuine (rising heparin requirement with a subtherapeutic anti-Xa): treat the cause, principally source control of sepsis; recognise heparin binding to acute-phase proteins, high factor VIII and raised heparin clearance; consider antithrombin concentrate if antithrombin is very low and heparin cannot be made to work (limited evidence, not routine); consider switching to argatroban or bivalirudin, which do not depend on antithrombin. Throughout: exclude sampling error from a heparinised line, confirm the infusion is running at the prescribed rate, and reassess for heparin-induced thrombocytopenia if platelets fall.

Explained step by step

Step 1 – is the resistance real? In this patient it is not. The anti-Xa is therapeutic (with the proviso from question 2 that the assay does not add its own antithrombin). So:

  • stop escalating the dose;
  • switch monitoring to the anti-Xa assay;
  • keep the current dose;
  • write clearly in the notes why the APTT is no longer being followed, so the next team does not restart the chase.

Step 2 – if resistance is genuine. Genuine heparin resistance means a rising heparin requirement with a subtherapeutic anti-Xa. Then:

  • Treat the underlying cause – mainly source control of the pelvic sepsis.
  • Understand the contributors: heparin binds to acute-phase proteins (so less is free to act), factor VIII is high, and heparin is cleared faster.
  • Consider antithrombin concentrate if antithrombin is very low and heparin cannot be made to work. The evidence for this is limited and it is not routine.
  • Consider switching to an anticoagulant that does not depend on antithrombin – argatroban or bivalirudin. This sidesteps the problem entirely and is the more practical solution in most centres.

Throughout, check the basics:

  • exclude sampling error from a heparinised line;
  • confirm the infusion is actually running at the prescribed rate;
  • reassess for heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (HIT) if the platelet count falls.

Think of it like this
Before calling a plumber because the water pressure seems low, check the gauge is not faulty, the tap is fully open and the pipe is not kinked. Only then replace the pump – or, if the pump keeps failing, switch to a supply that does not need it.
Common mistake
Jumping straight to antithrombin concentrate or a drug switch without first showing that the resistance is real.
Remember
Prove it, treat the cause, then bypass antithrombin if you must.

What earns the marks

  • Establish whether resistance is real: here it is not – stop escalating, monitor by anti-Xa, keep dose, document
  • If genuine: treat cause (sepsis source control); recognise acute-phase protein binding, high factor VIII, raised clearance
  • Consider antithrombin concentrate (limited evidence, not routine) or switch to argatroban or bivalirudin
  • Exclude line sampling error, check the infusion rate, reassess for HIT if platelets fall
EvidencePractical Haemostasis — Anti-Xa Assays (causes of sub- and supra-therapeutic levels)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
What counts as heparin resistance?

There is no agreed definition. One widely quoted definition is a need for more than 35,000 units a day to reach anticoagulation, but the target level is not specified [S1]. Whether a patient is called resistant depends on the test used, the target and the definition [S2].

The key trial enrolled patients with venous thromboembolism who needed 35,000 units or more in 24 hours and randomised them to monitoring by anti-Xa or by APTT [S3]. Recurrence was similar in the two groups [S4], and the authors concluded that dose escalation can be avoided if the heparin level is therapeutic [S5].

That is this patient: an APTT made short by factor VIII and fibrinogen, with a therapeutic anti-Xa. She has apparent, not true, heparin resistance.

  1. [S1] Levy JH, Connors JM 2021. Heparin Resistance — Clinical Perspectives and Management Strategies. N Engl J Med 2021;385:826-832 PMID 34437785 · doi:10.1056/NEJMra2104091One reported definition suggests that the need for more than 35,000 U per day to achieve anticoagulation constitutes heparin resistance, but the targeted level of anticoagulation is not specified.
  2. [S2] Levy JH, Connors JM 2021. Heparin Resistance — Clinical Perspectives and Management Strategies. N Engl J Med 2021;385:826-832 PMID 34437785 · doi:10.1056/NEJMra2104091The identification of heparin resistance depends on the laboratory test used, the degree of anticoagulation targeted, and the definition of resistance used.
  3. [S3] Levine MN et al. 1994. A randomized trial comparing activated thromboplastin time with heparin assay in patients with acute venous thromboembolism requiring large daily doses of heparin. Arch Intern Med 1994;154:49-56 PMID 8267489patients with acute deep vein thrombosis, pulmonary embolism, or axillary vein thrombosis who required 35,000 U or more of intravenous heparin by continuous infusion during the previous 24 hours
  4. [S4] Levine MN et al. 1994. A randomized trial comparing activated thromboplastin time with heparin assay in patients with acute venous thromboembolism requiring large daily doses of heparin. Arch Intern Med 1994;154:49-56 PMID 8267489Three (4.6%) of 65 patients in the anti-factor Xa group experienced recurrent venous thromboembolism compared with four (6.1%) of 66 patients in the APTT group
  5. [S5] Levine MN et al. 1994. A randomized trial comparing activated thromboplastin time with heparin assay in patients with acute venous thromboembolism requiring large daily doses of heparin. Arch Intern Med 1994;154:49-56 PMID 8267489In such patients, dosage escalation can be avoided if the heparin level is therapeutic.

Take home from this station

  1. A very high factor VIII from the acute-phase response shortens the APTT and hides adequate heparin effect; the anti-Xa is not affected.
  2. When the APTT is unreliable, dose unfractionated heparin by a UFH-calibrated anti-Xa, and know whether your kit adds antithrombin.
  3. A low antithrombin during acute thrombosis, sepsis and heparin therapy is acquired until proven otherwise; retest when well.
  4. Choose the antithrombin assay substrate the drug does not act on: anti-IIa with anti-Xa drugs, anti-Xa with dabigatran.
12
Station 12 · 20 marks

Umbilical bleeding, normal coagulation screen

What this station tests: Can you recognise severe factor XIII deficiency as the cause of life-threatening bleeding with a completely normal coagulation screen, diagnose it with the right assay, and manage it safely?

The case

A male neonate, born to first-cousin parents, bleeds persistently from the umbilical stump on day five of life. At four months he presents with an intracranial haemorrhage after minor trauma. Every screening test is normal, on two separate occasions.

In plain wordsA baby boy, whose parents are first cousins, keeps bleeding from the umbilical stump on day five of life. At four months he has a bleed inside the skull (intracranial haemorrhage) after only a minor knock. Every routine clotting test is normal, and stays normal when repeated.
Coagulation screen · repeated on two occasions
TestResult
Prothrombin timeNormal for age
Activated partial thromboplastin timeNormal for age
Thrombin timeNormal
Clauss fibrinogen2.6 g/L
Platelet count and morphologyNormal
von Willebrand screenNormal

How to read the data

  • Prothrombin time (PT) and activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) normal for age: all the clotting factors these tests measure are present and working. The problem is not a classic factor deficiency such as haemophilia.
  • Thrombin time normal and Clauss fibrinogen 2.6 g/L: there is enough fibrinogen, and thrombin turns it into fibrin at a normal speed.
  • Platelet count and morphology normal, von Willebrand screen normal: the common platelet and von Willebrand causes are not shown here.
  • Repeated on two occasions: the normal results are real, not a sampling accident. The bleeding history must be explained by something the screen cannot see.
  • The clinical clues carry the diagnosis: umbilical stump bleeding, intracranial haemorrhage after minor trauma, and first-cousin parents.

The five questions

Q1
What single diagnosis best explains severe bleeding with an entirely normal coagulation screen in this clinical setting, and why do the screening tests miss it?
4 marks

Model answer

Severe congenital factor XIII deficiency. Delayed umbilical stump bleeding is highly characteristic (70–80% of symptomatic cases); intracranial haemorrhage, the most serious complication, occurs in up to 30% of untreated patients; consanguinity fits autosomal recessive inheritance. Screening tests all measure time to initial fibrin clot formation and stop there. Factor XIII acts after that endpoint: activated by thrombin, it cross-links fibrin alpha- and gamma-chains and incorporates alpha2-antiplasmin, giving strength and resistance to fibrinolysis. The clot forms at a normal rate but does not hold.

Explained step by step

Start with the clues. Bleeding from the umbilical stump in the first days of life is highly characteristic of factor XIII (FXIII) deficiency: it happens in 70–80% of symptomatic cases. Intracranial haemorrhage is the most serious complication and occurs in up to 30% of untreated patients. The parents are first cousins, which fits an autosomal recessive disorder: the child needs a faulty gene from both parents, and related parents are more likely to carry the same one.

What the screening tests actually measure. The prothrombin time (PT), activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT), thrombin time and Clauss fibrinogen all do the same basic thing. They start the clotting process and time how long it takes for the first fibrin clot to appear. Once the clot appears, the clock stops. The test does not ask how strong the clot is.

Where factor XIII works. Factor XIII acts after that moment. Thrombin activates it, and it then:

  • cross-links the fibrin alpha- and gamma-chains, tying the fibrin strands firmly together;
  • builds alpha2-antiplasmin into the clot, which protects the clot from being broken down (fibrinolysis).
Together these give the clot mechanical strength and resistance to breakdown.

Putting it together. A patient with no factor XIII forms a clot at a completely normal speed, so every screening test is normal. But the clot is weak and is broken down too easily. It simply does not hold, and the patient bleeds.

Think of it like this
Building a brick wall without mortar: the bricks go up at normal speed, so the wall looks fine when the timer stops, but it falls over at the first push. The screening tests only time the bricklaying.
Common mistake
Reassuring because the screen is normal, or naming a platelet or von Willebrand disorder when the umbilical bleeding points so strongly to factor XIII.
Remember
Normal clot speed, weak clot: umbilical bleeding plus normal screen means factor XIII.

What earns the marks

  • Names severe congenital factor XIII deficiency
  • Uses the clues: umbilical stump bleeding (70–80%), intracranial haemorrhage (up to 30% untreated, the most serious complication), consanguinity and autosomal recessive inheritance
  • Explains that screening tests stop at the time to first fibrin clot
  • Explains factor XIII action after that point: cross-links fibrin alpha- and gamma-chains, incorporates alpha2-antiplasmin, gives strength and resistance to fibrinolysis
Evidence[15]Kohler 2011
Q2
Describe the principle of the clot solubility test and explain why it must not be used to exclude the diagnosis.
4 marks

Model answer

Plasma is clotted and the clot incubated in a denaturing or lytic agent (5 M urea, dilute monochloroacetic acid or acetic acid), typically for 24 hours. A cross-linked clot resists dissolution; a clot lacking factor XIII cross-links dissolves. It is grossly insensitive: abnormal only at very low activity, roughly 1–5% depending on the method (thrombin/acetic acid methods detect levels up to about 10 U/dL), so normal in every heterozygote and in all clinically relevant partial deficiency. Poorly standardised (agent, concentration, incubation time, fibrinogen) and low fibrinogen can give a false positive. The ISTH recommends abandoning clot solubility tests in favour of a quantitative assay.

Explained step by step

The principle. The patient's plasma is clotted. The clot is then placed in a chemical that tries to dissolve it: 5 M urea, dilute monochloroacetic acid or acetic acid. It is usually left for 24 hours. A normal clot, held together by factor XIII cross-links, resists the chemical and stays intact. A clot without these cross-links dissolves.

Why a normal result means little: it is grossly insensitive. The test only becomes abnormal at very low factor XIII activity: roughly 1–5% depending on the method (thrombin/acetic acid methods detect up to about 10 U/dL). So it picks up only the most severe cases. It is normal in every heterozygote (carrier) and in all clinically relevant partial deficiency. A patient can have a real bleeding problem and still pass this test.

It is poorly standardised. The result changes with:

  • which lysing agent is used, and its concentration;
  • how long the clot is incubated;
  • the fibrinogen concentration of the sample.
A low fibrinogen on its own can make the clot dissolve, giving a false positive.

The official position. The International Society on Thrombosis and Haemostasis (ISTH) recommends that clot solubility tests should be abandoned in favour of a quantitative assay. A test that misses everything except the most severe cases cannot be used to say 'no factor XIII deficiency'.

Think of it like this
It is like a smoke detector that only sounds when the whole house is already on fire. Silence does not mean there is no fire.
Common mistake
Accepting a normal clot solubility test as excluding factor XIII deficiency.
Remember
Clot solubility catches only the worst cases: a normal result excludes nothing.

What earns the marks

  • Principle: plasma clot incubated in 5 M urea, dilute monochloroacetic acid or acetic acid, typically 24 hours; non-cross-linked clot dissolves
  • Abnormal only at very low activity (about 1–5%, method-dependent); normal in heterozygotes and in clinically relevant partial deficiency
  • Poorly standardised (agent, concentration, time, fibrinogen); low fibrinogen gives false positives
  • ISTH recommends abandoning it for a quantitative assay
Evidence[15]Kohler 2011Practical Haemostasis — Factor XIII Assays (clot solubility insensitivity)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
How insensitive is the clot solubility test?

In a UK NEQAS study, over 95% of laboratories using clot solubility tests correctly identified normal plasma and plasma from severe untreated deficiency [S1]. The problem lies just above that. The calcium/urea method detected factor XIII only between 1 and 5 U/dL [S2], while thrombin/acetic acid detected levels up to at least 10 U/dL [S3]. For samples with mild or moderate reduction, including patients on prophylaxis, laboratories could not agree on the interpretation [S4].

Low or abnormal fibrinogen can also make a clot dissolve and give a false positive [S5]. UK guidance therefore says these tests are poorly standardised, may detect only severe deficiency and are not recommended [S6]. A normal result excludes nothing.

  1. [S1] Jennings I et al. 2003. Problems relating to the laboratory diagnosis of factor XIII deficiency: a UK NEQAS study. J Thromb Haemost 2003;1:2603-2608 PMID 14675096 · doi:10.1046/j.1538-7836.2003.00469.xOver 95% of centers were able to diagnose severe familial FXIII deficiency in previously untreated patients and to identify samples from normal subjects.
  2. [S2] Jennings I et al. 2003. Problems relating to the laboratory diagnosis of factor XIII deficiency: a UK NEQAS study. J Thromb Haemost 2003;1:2603-2608 PMID 14675096 · doi:10.1046/j.1538-7836.2003.00469.xWe have demonstrated that the calcium/urea method is sensitive to between 1 and 5 U dL−1 of FXIII
  3. [S3] Jennings I et al. 2003. Problems relating to the laboratory diagnosis of factor XIII deficiency: a UK NEQAS study. J Thromb Haemost 2003;1:2603-2608 PMID 14675096 · doi:10.1046/j.1538-7836.2003.00469.xacetic acid is sensitive to at least 10 U dL−1 FXIII in plasma
  4. [S4] Jennings I et al. 2003. Problems relating to the laboratory diagnosis of factor XIII deficiency: a UK NEQAS study. J Thromb Haemost 2003;1:2603-2608 PMID 14675096 · doi:10.1046/j.1538-7836.2003.00469.xFor samples from subjects with mild/moderate reduction in FXIII levels (either through prophylaxis or acquired deficiency), there was a lack of consensus in interpretation amongst solubility test users.
  5. [S5] Jacobs JW et al. 2026. Factor XIII Deficiency: A Review of Biology, Testing, and Treatment. Clin Hematol Int 2026;8:10-25 PMID 41583548 · doi:10.46989/001c.155180Hypofibrinogenemia and dysfibrinogenemia can cause false positives, whereas increased fibrinogen may reduce sensitivity and contribute to false negative results.
  6. [S6] Mumford AD et al. 2014. Guideline for the diagnosis and management of the rare coagulation disorders (UKHCDO/BCSH). Br J Haematol 2014;167:304-326 PMID 25100430 · doi:10.1111/bjh.13058Clot solubility FXIII assays are poorly standardized, may be sensitive only to severe F13D and are not recommended (Kohler et al, 2011).
Q3
What assay is recommended for diagnosis, and what further testing establishes the subtype?
4 marks

Model answer

Quantitative factor XIII activity assay: first-line ammonia release assay (measures ammonia liberated as FXIIIa cross-links a substrate) or an amine incorporation assay. Ammonia release methods must include a plasma blank, or they over-read at low activity and can report roughly 5–11% when the true level is about zero (published examples: 5.6% → <1.0% after blanking, Lawrie 2010; 6% and 11% → 0%, Smock and Moser 2023), within a reported overestimation of 2–15%. Subtype with antigen assays: A2B2 heterotetramer, then A and B subunits separately. Low or absent A with normal B = FXIII-A deficiency (great majority); low B with secondarily reduced A = rarer, milder FXIII-B deficiency. Confirm with F13A1 or F13B sequencing; screen the family; in an older patient with acquired bleeding, add an inhibitor assay.

Explained step by step

Step 1 – measure activity with a quantitative assay. The recommended first-line method is an ammonia release assay. When activated factor XIII (FXIIIa) cross-links a substrate, ammonia is released. Measuring the ammonia tells you how much factor XIII activity is present. An amine incorporation assay is an alternative.

The trap: the plasma blank. Ammonia release methods must include a plasma blank. Without it, they over-read at low activities. They can report roughly 5–11% when the true level is about zero: published examples are 5.6% falling to below 1.0% after blanking (Lawrie 2010) and 6% and 11% falling to 0% (Smock and Moser 2023). Here that would wrongly make a severe deficiency look milder, and could change how seriously the child is treated.

Step 2 – find the subtype with antigen assays. Factor XIII in plasma is a heterotetramer: two A subunits and two B subunits (A2B2). Measure the A2B2 complex, then the A and B subunits separately:

  • A absent or low, B normal: FXIII-A deficiency. This is the great majority of cases.
  • B low, A reduced secondarily: FXIII-B deficiency. This is rarer and has a milder phenotype.

Step 3 – confirm and extend. Confirm with gene sequencing of F13A1 or F13B, and screen the family. In an older patient with bleeding that has appeared later in life (acquired), add a factor XIII inhibitor assay.

Think of it like this
Activity tells you whether the engine runs; the antigen tests open the bonnet to see which part is missing; sequencing reads the part number.
Common mistake
Using an ammonia release assay without a plasma blank, so a patient with no factor XIII is reported as having about 5–11%.
Remember
Activity first (with a blank), then A and B antigens, then the gene.

What earns the marks

  • Quantitative factor XIII activity assay: ammonia release first-line, or amine incorporation
  • Plasma blank required: otherwise over-reads at low activity (about 5–11% when the true level is ~0; 2–15% reported)
  • Antigen assays: A2B2, then A and B subunits; interprets A versus B deficiency patterns
  • F13A1 or F13B sequencing, family screening; inhibitor assay if acquired in an older patient
Evidence[15]Kohler 2011[18]Smock 2023[19]Lawrie 2010[20]Ajzner 2004Practical Haemostasis — Factor XIII Assays (ammonia release assays and the plasma blank requirement, subunit antigen testing)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
The plasma blank, and why the low end matters

Ammonia release assays measure the ammonia released as activated factor XIII cross-links its substrate, but some ammonia comes from reactions that do not involve factor XIII. Without correction this can over-estimate activity, by 2–15% in published reports, which matters most when true activity is low [S1]. UK guidance warns that the ammonia release assay may overestimate activity unless a plasma blank is subtracted [S2], and asks for an assay that measures activity accurately below 0.1 iu/ml for diagnosis and monitoring [S3]. In one validation, blank-corrected results correlated poorly with a reference laboratory that did not correct [S4].

Practical point: before accepting a 'mild' result in a child with umbilical bleeding, ask whether the method subtracts a blank.

  1. [S1] Smock KJ, Moser KA 2023. Automation of a Factor XIII Activity Assay Utilizing a Plasma Blank Measurement. Methods Mol Biol 2023;2663:693-706 PMID 37204746 · doi:10.1007/978-1-0716-3175-1_46Reactions generating FXIII-independent ammonia production can lead to overestimation of FXIII activity by a few percent (overestimation by 2-15% is reported in the literature), which could be clinically significant in samples with low activity
  2. [S2] Mumford AD et al. 2014. Guideline for the diagnosis and management of the rare coagulation disorders (UKHCDO/BCSH). Br J Haematol 2014;167:304-326 PMID 25100430 · doi:10.1111/bjh.13058The ammonia release assay may overestimate FXIII activity in plasma, unless there is subtraction of a plasma blank (Ajzner & Muszbek, 2004).
  3. [S3] Mumford AD et al. 2014. Guideline for the diagnosis and management of the rare coagulation disorders (UKHCDO/BCSH). Br J Haematol 2014;167:304-326 PMID 25100430 · doi:10.1111/bjh.13058Diagnosis and monitoring of F13D requires a FXIII activity assay that enables accurate measurement of FXIII activity &lt;0.1 iu/ml (2C).
  4. [S4] Smock KJ, Moser KA 2023. Automation of a Factor XIII Activity Assay Utilizing a Plasma Blank Measurement. Methods Mol Biol 2023;2663:693-706 PMID 37204746 · doi:10.1007/978-1-0716-3175-1_46poor correlation due to lack of plasma blank correction at the reference laboratory
Deep dive: the plasma blank, explained in depth

An ammonia release assay cannot tell where its signal came from. The plasma blank measures the part that did not come from factor XIII, so that it can be subtracted.

1. How the assay turns factor XIII into a number

The analyser never sees factor XIII. It watches a chain of three reactions and works backwards [18, 19].

  1. Activation. Thrombin and calcium turn the patient's factor XIII into active factor XIIIa. Fibrin made by the thrombin speeds this up, and a small peptide stops that fibrin from clotting, so the cuvette stays clear for the light reading.
  2. The enzyme reaction. Factor XIIIa is a transglutaminase. It joins glycine ethyl ester to a glutamine-containing peptide, and every join releases one ammonium ion (NH₄⁺).
  3. The detection reaction. Glutamate dehydrogenase uses that ammonium to turn α-ketoglutarate into glutamate, and uses up one NADPH as it does so (NADH in the Berichrom kit). NADPH absorbs light at 340 nm, so as it is used up, the absorbance falls.

So: more factor XIII → more ammonia → more NADPH used → faster fall in absorbance → higher reported activity. The analyser measures only the last step and assumes all of it was caused by factor XIII.

2. Where the error comes from

That assumption is not quite true. Plasma also contains reactions that make ammonia or use up NADPH without factor XIIIa: Lawrie and colleagues describe “some ammonia-producing and NADH-consuming reactions independent of FXIIIa” [19], and Smock and Moser call it “FXIII-independent ammonia production” [18]. The analyser cannot tell this background from real factor XIII activity, so it adds it to the result.

3. What the blank actually is

The patient's own plasma is run twice [18, 19, 20]:

RunWhat is in the cuvetteWhat it measures
TestPlasma + activator + detection reagentsFactor XIII signal plus background
BlankThe same plasma and reagents plus iodoacetamide, which switches off factor XIIIaBackground only

True factor XIII activity = test result − blank result

It must be the patient's own plasma, not a reagent blank, because the background comes from the plasma and differs between patients: in Smock and Moser's 12 patients it ranged from 1% to 8% [18].

4. Why it matters at low levels, not normal ones

The background is a roughly fixed amount, a few percentage points, not a fixed proportion. A constant offset is lost in a big number and dominates a small one.

  • Overestimation of 2–15% is reported in the literature; the median blank in Smock and Moser's series was 6% [18].
  • In Smock and Moser's Specimen 12 the true activity was 0%, but the reading was 6% before correction and 11% at a reference laboratory that did not blank [18].
  • Cini and colleagues found blank subtraction lowered the under-20% group from 10.1% to 2.4% (p<0.0001), and could reveal severe deficiencies (<5%) “otherwise undiagnosed” [21].
  • Practical Haemostasis: without a blank, results are wrong “in the low activity range [<5–10%]”.
Without blankWith blank
Patient 15.6<1.0
Patient 39.44.9
Patient 512.59.4
Patient 9 (carrier)70.268.9
Normal pool86.686.8

Lawrie et al. 2010, Table 1: factor XIII activity (u/dL); bars drawn to one scale, 0 to 90.

5. Why it changes patient care

  • Wrong severity. A child with less than 1% looks moderately affected at 6–11%. Lawrie and colleagues warn the overestimation “can potentially affect the clinical management of these patients” [19].
  • Decision thresholds sit in this range. Smock and Moser cite a sharp rise in spontaneous major bleeding below 15 IU/dL [18], and the UK guideline targets a prophylaxis trough of 10–20 IU/dL [17]. A background of 5–8 points is a large share of those numbers.
  • A trough can look adequate when it is not. This follows from the numbers but is not stated in the papers: an unblanked trough of 14% could truly be 6–8%.
  • Carriers are barely affected. At 50–70% the error is trivial (Lawrie, patient 9).

6. Practical laboratory rules

  • Blank every result below 20%. Smock and Moser validated reflex blanking below 30% and settled on below 20% [18]; Lawrie and colleagues found it “particularly important” below 10% [19].
  • Use the kit's inhibitor reagent (the Technochrom kit's R4 contains iodoacetamide) or locally prepared iodoacetamide [18, 21]. Lawrie and colleagues used 1 mmol/L [19].
  • Report the floor honestly. In Smock and Moser's validation the limit of blank was 2.72% and the limit of detection 4.65%, so results below 5% are reported as “<5%” [18].
  • Citrated plasma only. Serum and fibrinogen-free samples read falsely low; grossly lipaemic samples read high and icteric samples low [18].
  • The UK guideline agrees: the ammonia release assay “may overestimate FXIII activity in plasma, unless there is subtraction of a plasma blank” [17].

The evidence, as published

Exam-ready summaryAmmonia release assays measure the rate of NADPH (or NADH) use at 340 nm and assume it all comes from factor XIIIa releasing ammonia. Plasma also makes ammonia and uses NADPH by routes that do not involve factor XIII, which adds a roughly constant few percent. A plasma blank runs the patient's own plasma with iodoacetamide, which switches off factor XIIIa, to measure that background alone, and it is then subtracted. The error is trivial in the normal range but critical below about 10–20%: without a blank, severe deficiency (<1%) can be reported as 5–11%, misleading severity classification and prophylaxis.
Sources[18]Smock 2023[19]Lawrie 2010[20]Ajzner 2004[21]Cini 2016[17]Mumford 2014
Q4
Activity is <1%. Set out long-term management, including the target trough and the additional consideration if a female relative who is also severely deficient later becomes pregnant.
4 marks

Model answer

Lifelong regular prophylaxis is mandatory because of the intracranial haemorrhage risk. UK guidance (UKHCDO 2014): factor XIII concentrate 20–40 iu/kg every 28 days, adjusted to keep the trough factor XIII activity at 0.1–0.2 iu/ml (10–20 IU/dL), measured just before the next dose. Consider recombinant FXIII-A2 for A-subunit deficiency not previously exposed to plasma products; plasma or cryoprecipitate only if no concentrate. Monitor for inhibitors; intracranial haemorrhage is an emergency, with replacement before imaging; alert card; no intramuscular injections. Pregnancy in an affected woman: factor XIII is needed for implantation and placental maintenance, and untreated severe deficiency causes recurrent first-trimester miscarriage, so monitor closely, dose every 14–21 days to keep activity above 0.2 iu/ml (20 IU/dL), and give an extra 10–40 iu/kg in established labour or before caesarean section. Heterozygous carriers (FXIII activity about 0.2–0.7 iu/ml) are usually asymptomatic, although some have mild bleeding symptoms.

Explained step by step

Prophylaxis is mandatory, not optional. With activity below 1%, the risk of intracranial haemorrhage is high. This child needs regular replacement for life, not just treatment when he bleeds.

Why it works so well. Factor XIII concentrate stays in the blood for a long time: the UK guideline quotes a half-life of 7 days for the plasma-derived product. So one dose lasts weeks, and dosing every 28 days is enough to protect the child.

The UK target. The UKHCDO guideline recommends starting factor XIII concentrate at 20–40 iu/kg every 28 days, then adjusting the dose to keep the trough factor XIII activity at 0.1–0.2 iu/ml (10–20 IU/dL). The trough is the lowest level, measured immediately before the next dose.

Which product.

  • Recombinant factor XIII-A2 is worth considering where the defect is in the A subunit and the child has not been exposed to plasma products. It is unsuitable for B-subunit deficiency.
  • Otherwise a plasma-derived factor XIII concentrate.
  • Plasma or cryoprecipitate only if no concentrate is available.

Safety measures. Monitor for inhibitor development. Treat any intracranial haemorrhage as an emergency: give replacement immediately, before imaging. Issue an alert card, and avoid intramuscular injections.

The pregnant female relative who is herself severely deficient. Factor XIII is needed for the embryo to implant and for the placenta to be maintained. Untreated severe deficiency causes recurrent first-trimester miscarriage. So prophylaxis is escalated, not merely continued: the guideline advises close monitoring, dosing every 14–21 days to keep factor XIII activity above 0.2 iu/ml (20 IU/dL), and an extra 10–40 iu/kg once she is in established labour or before a caesarean section. By contrast, obligate heterozygote carriers, with activity around 50%, are generally asymptomatic and do not need prophylaxis.

Think of it like this
Prophylaxis is like topping up a slowly leaking water tank once a month: because the leak is slow (long half-life), occasional refills keep the level above the danger line. In pregnancy the tank must be kept fuller.
Common mistake
Simply continuing the usual prophylaxis in pregnancy instead of escalating it, or offering on-demand treatment only.
Remember
Every 28 days, trough 10–20; in pregnancy every 2–3 weeks, above 20.

What earns the marks

  • Regular lifelong prophylaxis is mandatory; concentrate every 28 days is feasible because of the long half-life
  • UK target: 20–40 iu/kg every 28 days, adjusted to a trough of 0.1–0.2 iu/ml (10–20 IU/dL) measured before the next dose
  • Product choice: recombinant FXIII-A2 for A-subunit deficiency (not B), plasma-derived concentrate, plasma or cryoprecipitate only if none; inhibitor monitoring, replacement before imaging, alert card, no intramuscular injections
  • Pregnancy: escalate to every 14–21 days to keep activity above 0.2 iu/ml, extra 10–40 iu/kg for delivery; reason is implantation and placental maintenance
Evidence[17]Mumford 2014
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Factor XIII and pregnancy

Activated factor XIII contributes to tissue repair and to embryonic implantation [S1]. Before prophylaxis, pregnancy loss in women with severe deficiency was reported at more than 90%; with prophylaxis there are now many reports of successful term pregnancies [S2]. Factor XIII activity also falls during normal pregnancy [S3], which is why replacement has to be stepped up.

UKHCDO recommends close monitoring, prophylaxis every 14–21 days to keep activity above 0.2 iu/ml, and an extra 10–40 iu/kg once in established labour or before caesarean section [S4]. There is no evidence to define target levels during delivery [S5], so the extra dose is judged on the interval since the last infusion.

  2. [S2] Mumford AD et al. 2014. Guideline for the diagnosis and management of the rare coagulation disorders (UKHCDO/BCSH). However, with prophylaxis there are now numerous reports of successful term pregnancies (Sharief & Kadir, 2013).
  3. [S3] Mumford AD et al. 2014. Guideline for the diagnosis and management of the rare coagulation disorders (UKHCDO/BCSH). Br J Haematol 2014;167:304-326 PMID 25100430 · doi:10.1111/bjh.13058FXIII activity decreases during normal pregnancy (Sharief et al, 2013).
  4. [S4] Mumford AD et al. 2014. Guideline for the diagnosis and management of the rare coagulation disorders (UKHCDO/BCSH).
