  5. [S5] Mumford AD et al. 2014. Guideline for the diagnosis and management of the rare coagulation disorders (UKHCDO/BCSH). Br J Haematol 2014;167:304-326 PMID 25100430 · doi:10.1111/bjh.13058There is no evidence to inform target FXIII activities during delivery.
Q5
Name three other disorders that cause a significant bleeding phenotype with a normal routine coagulation screen.
4 marks

Model answer

Any three of: alpha2-antiplasmin deficiency or plasminogen activator inhibitor-1 deficiency (hyperfibrinolytic, delayed rebleeding, respond to tranexamic acid); mild platelet function disorders such as secretion and storage pool defects (normal count and film; need light transmission aggregometry); vascular and connective tissue disorders such as hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia and Ehlers–Danlos syndrome; mild von Willebrand disease (missed on a single screen because of ABO group, stress and acute-phase response; repeat testing); dysfibrinogenaemia with bleeding (thrombin time often but not invariably abnormal).

Explained step by step

All of these share one feature: the routine screen asks 'how fast does the first clot form?' and in each disorder the answer is 'normally'. The problem lies somewhere the screen does not look.

  • Alpha2-antiplasmin deficiency and plasminogen activator inhibitor-1 (PAI-1) deficiency. These are the brakes on clot breakdown. Without them the clot forms normally but is dissolved too early (hyperfibrinolysis). The typical story is delayed rebleeding after bleeding had first stopped. They respond to tranexamic acid.
  • Mild platelet function disorders, including secretion and storage pool defects. The platelet count and blood film are normal, so they are easily missed. Light transmission aggregometry (LTA) is needed.
  • Vascular and connective tissue disorders, such as hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia (HHT) and Ehlers–Danlos syndrome. The blood is normal; the vessel wall or supporting tissue is the problem.
  • Mild von Willebrand disease. It is readily missed on a single screen, because levels are influenced by ABO blood group, stress and the acute-phase response. Repeat testing is needed.
  • Dysfibrinogenaemia with a bleeding phenotype. The thrombin time is often abnormal, but not always.

The principle to teach. A normal coagulation screen never excludes a bleeding disorder. When the bleeding history is convincing, the history outranks the screen: investigate further rather than reassure.

Think of it like this
A normal coagulation screen is like a car passing a test that only checks whether the engine starts. The brakes, the tyres or the bodywork can still fail on the road.
Common mistake
Listing disorders that do prolong the screen, such as haemophilia A or B, or forgetting that a single normal von Willebrand screen can miss mild disease.
Remember
When the history is convincing, the history outranks the screen.

What earns the marks

  • Alpha2-antiplasmin or PAI-1 deficiency (hyperfibrinolysis, delayed rebleeding, tranexamic acid)
  • Mild platelet function disorders, needing light transmission aggregometry
  • Vascular or connective tissue disorders (HHT, Ehlers–Danlos); mild von Willebrand disease needing repeat testing; dysfibrinogenaemia
  • Any three correctly named, ideally with why the screen misses each
EvidenceConsultant laboratory practice, as set out in the examiner's key; no published guideline is cited for this answer.

Take home from this station

  1. Umbilical stump bleeding with a normal coagulation screen is factor XIII deficiency until proved otherwise.
  2. The screening tests stop at the first fibrin clot; factor XIII works afterwards, making the clot strong and resistant to breakdown.
  3. Diagnose with a quantitative factor XIII activity assay (with a plasma blank), never with a clot solubility test, and subtype with A and B antigen assays.
  4. Severe deficiency needs lifelong prophylaxis every 28 days to a trough of 10–20 IU/dL (UKHCDO), escalated in pregnancy to keep activity above 20 IU/dL.
13
Extension 13 · 20 marks

A long APTT at a pre-employment medical

What this station tests: Can you recognise a lupus anticoagulant that mimics factor VIII deficiency, read non-parallel dilution results as assay interference, and avoid wrongly labelling a healthy man with haemophilia?

The case

A 28-year-old asymptomatic man has an activated partial thromboplastin time of 74 s on pre-employment screening. He has never bled after surgery or dental extraction and has no family history. Prothrombin time and thrombin time are normal.

In plain wordsA healthy 28-year-old man has a long activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) of 74 s on a pre-employment blood test. He has had surgery and dental extractions without bleeding, and no one in his family bleeds. The laboratory work-up shows an inhibitor that behaves like a lupus anticoagulant, and one-stage factor VIII results that change with dilution.
Investigations
InvestigationResult
1:1 mix, immediate61 s
1:1 mix, incubated 2 h60 s
Factor VIII, one-stage, 1:1018 IU/dL
Factor VIII, one-stage, 1:2046 IU/dL
Factor VIII, one-stage, 1:4082 IU/dL
Factor VIII, chromogenic91 IU/dL
dRVVT screenProlonged
dRVVT confirm (high phospholipid)Corrects
Factor IX, XINormal

How to read the data

  • APTT 74 s with normal prothrombin time (PT) and thrombin time: the problem affects only the APTT pathway. Fibrinogen conversion and the PT pathway are normal.
  • 1:1 mix 61 s immediately and 60 s after 2 hours: the APTT does not correct when normal plasma is added, so an inhibitor is present. It does not get worse with incubation, so it acts immediately, not slowly over time.
  • One-stage factor VIII (FVIII) 18, 46 and 82 IU/dL at 1:10, 1:20 and 1:40: the answer climbs as the sample is diluted. A true factor level should stay the same. This is non-parallelism.
  • Chromogenic FVIII 91 IU/dL: the true FVIII level is normal.
  • Dilute Russell's viper venom time (dRVVT) prolonged, correcting with high phospholipid: the inhibitor depends on phospholipid, which is the hallmark of a lupus anticoagulant.
  • Factors IX and XI normal; no bleeding history: no evidence of a true deficiency in the APTT pathway.

The five questions

Q1
What is the most likely explanation for the prolonged activated partial thromboplastin time?
4 marks

Model answer

A lupus anticoagulant. Three findings converge: no bleeding phenotype despite APTT 74 s and previous haemostatic challenges; a 1:1 mix that fails to correct and is identical immediate and incubated, indicating an immediate-acting inhibitor rather than the time-dependent behaviour of a factor VIII autoantibody; and a prolonged dRVVT that corrects with added phospholipid, showing phospholipid dependence.

Explained step by step

The answer is a lupus anticoagulant (LA). It is reached by putting three separate findings together.

Finding 1 – no bleeding. His activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) is 74 s, yet he has had surgery and dental extractions without bleeding. A clotting test that looks this abnormal in a man who has passed real haemostatic challenges suggests the problem is in the test tube, not in his body.

Finding 2 – the mixing study. Mixing his plasma 1:1 with normal plasma supplies any missing factor. If a factor were simply missing, the APTT would correct. It does not (61 s), so something in his plasma is inhibiting the test. The result is the same immediately and after 2 hours of incubation (60 s). That means an immediate-acting inhibitor. Most factor VIII autoantibodies are time-dependent, so the incubated mix is usually longer than the immediate one. But some inhibitors in acquired haemophilia A act quickly, so an identical immediate and incubated mix does not on its own exclude them; the phospholipid-dependent dRVVT and the normal chromogenic factor VIII settle it.

Finding 3 – phospholipid dependence. The dilute Russell's viper venom time (dRVVT) is prolonged, and it corrects when extra phospholipid is added in the confirm step. An inhibitor that is overcome by extra phospholipid is the defining feature of a lupus anticoagulant.

No bleeding, an immediate non-correcting inhibitor, and phospholipid dependence: together these point to a lupus anticoagulant.

Think of it like this
A lupus anticoagulant is like a sticker on a car's sensor that makes the dashboard warning light come on. The light (the APTT) is alarming, but the engine (his real clotting) is fine.
Common mistake
Calling it acquired haemophilia because the mix does not correct, without noticing that the incubated mix is no worse and that he has never bled.
Remember
Non-correcting, not time-dependent, phospholipid-dependent, no bleeding: lupus anticoagulant.

What earns the marks

  • Names lupus anticoagulant
  • No bleeding phenotype despite APTT 74 s and previous surgery and dental extraction
  • 1:1 mix fails to correct and is identical immediate and incubated: immediate-acting, so usually not a time-dependent factor VIII autoantibody (some act quickly)
  • dRVVT prolonged and corrected by high phospholipid: phospholipid dependence
Evidence[12]Devreese 2020Practical Haemostasis — dRVVT
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Mixing studies: useful, but not decisive

A 50:50 mix that fails to correct suggests an immediate-acting inhibitor, most often a lupus anticoagulant [S1]. A mix that corrects is not fully reassuring either: some factor VIII inhibitors are slow-acting, so an APTT done straight after mixing may correct, and simple dilution of the antibody can bring the result into range [S2]. Equally, some factor VIII inhibitors in acquired haemophilia A act quickly, and immediate or incubated mixing results are often not clear-cut [S3].

What settles this case is phospholipid dependence. The dRVVT confirm step adds excess phospholipid, which only a very strong lupus anticoagulant can overcome [S4]. Put that next to a normal chromogenic factor VIII and no bleeding history, and the diagnosis is secure.

  1. [S1] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776A lack of correction in mixing studies suggests the presence of an immediate-acting inhibitor, the most common of which is LA.
  2. [S2] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776some FVIII inhibitors are slow-acting (and therefore mixing may correct if the APTT is performed immediately after mixing) and that the dilution of the antibody in a 50:50 mixing study may also normalise the result into the reference range
  3. [S3] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776as are some FVIII inhibitors in acquired haemophilia A. The results of immediate or incubated mixing studies are often not clear-cut
  4. [S4] Devreese KMJ et al. 2020. Guidance from the ISTH SSC for lupus anticoagulant/antiphospholipid antibodies: update of the guidelines for lupus anticoagulant detection and interpretation. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:2828-2839 PMID 33462974 · doi:10.1111/jth.15047a strong LA sufficiently potent to overcome the excess of PL added in the confirmatory step
Q2
Name and interpret the pattern shown by the three one-stage dilutions.
4 marks

Model answer

Non-parallelism. Apparent FVIII rises steeply with dilution (18, 46, 82 IU/dL), approaching the true value at the highest dilution, because dilution progressively neutralises the lupus anticoagulant and its inhibitory effect on the phospholipid-dependent assay falls away. The results are analytically invalid and must not be reported as factor levels. The pattern is diagnostically informative: it is the signature of assay interference, seen with lupus-sensitive reagents in high-titre lupus anticoagulant and not a feature of specific factor inhibitors.

Explained step by step

Name the pattern: non-parallelism. In a one-stage factor assay the laboratory tests the patient's plasma at several dilutions and reads each result against a standard curve. If the patient's plasma behaves like the standard, every dilution gives the same factor level once the dilution is allowed for. The lines are then 'parallel'.

What happens here. The apparent factor VIII (FVIII) climbs steeply as the sample is diluted: 18, then 46, then 82 IU/dL. At the highest dilution it gets close to the true value (the chromogenic result of 91 IU/dL).

Why. The one-stage assay depends on phospholipid. The lupus anticoagulant interferes with that phospholipid step. Each time the plasma is diluted, there is less lupus anticoagulant in the tube, so it is progressively neutralised. Its inhibitory effect falls away, and the apparent factor activity rises.

What to do with the numbers. These results are analytically invalid. They must not be reported as factor levels. But the pattern itself is useful: it is the signature of assay interference. Published evidence shows that non-parallelism of this kind is seen with lupus-sensitive reagents in high-titre lupus anticoagulant, and is not a feature of specific factor inhibitors.

Think of it like this
It is like trying to hear someone over loud music. Turn the music down (dilute the lupus anticoagulant) and their voice (the true factor VIII) comes through more and more clearly.
Common mistake
Reporting the 1:10 result (18 IU/dL) as the patient's factor VIII level, or averaging the three results.
Remember
Factor level rises with dilution: interference, not deficiency.

What earns the marks

  • Names non-parallelism
  • Describes the rise with dilution (18, 46, 82 IU/dL) towards the true value
  • Explains dilution progressively neutralises the lupus anticoagulant in a phospholipid-dependent assay
  • Results analytically invalid, not reportable; pattern signals interference, typical of high-titre lupus anticoagulant, not specific factor inhibitors
Evidence[6]Ruinemans-Koerts 2010[23]Friedman 2023Practical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays
Deep dive: non-parallelism, checked against the original sources

Parallel lines mean the factor result is valid. A lupus anticoagulant changes the shape of the patient line; specific factor inhibitors, in the original study, did not.

1. The parallel-line bioassay in one minute

Each dilution of the patient's plasma is tested against the same reference curve. Ruinemans-Koerts and colleagues put it simply: “Parallelism of the curves is an indication of correct factor analysis. The horizontal distance between the lines represents the difference in potency” [6]. If the patient line has a different slope, the factor result changes with dilution and “should be regarded as incorrect” [6].

2. What the original study actually tested

One-stage factor VIII and IX assays were run with three APTT reagents: two lupus-sensitive (PTT-LA, APTT-SP) and one insensitive (Actin) [6]. Plasmas contained:

  • high- and low-titre lupus anticoagulants;
  • type 1 factor VIII inhibitors, 19.5 and about 2 NBU/ml;
  • type 2 factor VIII inhibitors from acquired haemophilia, 51.5 and about 2 NBU/ml;
  • a factor IX inhibitor, about 0.5 NBU/ml.

Result: non-parallelism appeared only with lupus-sensitive reagents and only in high-titre lupus anticoagulants. Actin, or a low-titre lupus anticoagulant, gave parallel lines. Every specific factor VIII and IX inhibitor gave parallel curves, whatever the incubation time or temperature [6].

3. Why the two kinds of antibody behave differently

As a lupus-anticoagulant plasma is diluted into a fixed volume of APTT reagent, the antibody is “disproportionately neutralised” by the reagent's phospholipid, so the apparent factor activity rises [6]. A specific inhibitor would only cause non-parallelism if it could let go of the factor; the factor VIII inhibitors studied bind it essentially irreversibly, so diluting the plasma does not release activity [6].

4. We re-checked the published numbers

Slopes were calculated from the clotting times printed or plotted in each source (points read from graphs to about ±2 s). A slope ratio near 1 means parallel.

5. Where the sources disagree

  • Friedman and colleagues' case shows a non-parallel line in a patient with an acquired factor VIII inhibitor (Fig. 80.1, slope ratio 0.57 on the authors' own axis) and lists specific inhibitors among the causes [23]. It is a textbook teaching case; Ruinemans-Koerts is the primary laboratory study.
  • Kurt and Fang report a strong factor VIII inhibitor (126 BU) with non-parallel factor IX, XI and XII assays, a meeting abstract [24]. A likely explanation, not stated by the authors, is that the substrate plasma in those assays contains factor VIII, which the inhibitor neutralises.
  • The Practical Haemostasis worked example contradicts itself: its graph points (55, 59, 64, 69 s) are parallel, but its raw-data table (55, 59, 73, 80 s) is not.
  • Kinetics labels: Practical Haemostasis calls type I inhibitors “first-order” and type II “second-order”. The original description gives type I as second-order with complete inactivation, and type II as complex with incomplete inactivation [22].

6. The laboratory pathway the authors proposed

  1. Test one dilution first. If the result is above the lower reference limit, report it.
  2. If it is low (for example FVIII below 50 IU/dL), test several dilutions.
  3. Non-parallel: use a chromogenic assay and test for a lupus anticoagulant.
  4. Parallel: the result can be reported, but a specific inhibitor is not excluded: do a 1:1 mix with normal pool and incubate (Figure 1D says 1 h at 37 °C; the text says 2 h for factor VIII deficiency). If the mix does not normalise, quantify with the Nijmegen-Bethesda assay; even if it does, a type 2 factor VIII inhibitor is still possible [6].

The evidence, as published

Exam-ready summaryIn the one-stage factor assay, a valid result gives patient and reference lines that are parallel; the horizontal gap between them is the potency difference. In the original study, non-parallelism appeared only with high-titre lupus anticoagulants tested with lupus-sensitive reagents: the antibody is neutralised by the reagent phospholipid as the plasma is diluted, so the apparent factor level rises and the slope can even reverse. Specific factor VIII and IX inhibitors, including acquired type 2 antibodies, gave parallel lines because they bind irreversibly. So parallel lines do not exclude a factor inhibitor: do a mixing study and a Nijmegen-Bethesda assay. Non-parallel lines: report the chromogenic or lupus-insensitive result and test for a lupus anticoagulant.
Sources[6]Ruinemans-Koerts 2010[22]Gawryl 1982[23]Friedman 2023[24]Kurt 2024Practical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays; Inhibitor Assays
Q3
Why should this man not be labelled as having mild haemophilia A?
4 marks

Model answer

True FVIII deficiency gives a coherent, parallel dilution response (same value at every dilution), corrects fully on 1:1 mixing, and correlates with a bleeding history, which is absent here. Above all, chromogenic FVIII is normal at 91 IU/dL. A wrong label has lifelong consequences: unnecessary treatment and product exposure, insurance and employment implications, incorrect genetic counselling, and the real thrombotic risk factor left undetected. Caveat: non-parallel lines do not always mean interference; when the true level is below 1 IU/dL you cannot dilute nothing, giving grossly prolonged, near-identical clotting times, distinguished by severity and phenotype.

Explained step by step

A true factor VIII deficiency looks quite different.

  • It gives a coherent, parallel dilution response: the same value at every dilution.
  • It corrects fully in a 1:1 mix, because mixing supplies the missing factor.
  • It goes with a bleeding history. This man has had surgery and dental extractions with no bleeding at all.

Above all, the chromogenic factor VIII is normal at 91 IU/dL. That is a direct measurement largely unaffected by the lupus anticoagulant. Together with the non-parallel one-stage results, the proven lupus anticoagulant and the absence of bleeding, there is no deficiency to label. (A reverse discrepancy, low one-stage but normal chromogenic factor VIII, is described in some genuine haemophilia A, so the chromogenic result is strong evidence rather than proof on its own.)

Why it matters so much. Labelling him with mild haemophilia A would be a serious error with lifelong consequences:

  • unnecessary treatment and exposure to clotting factor products;
  • insurance and employment problems, which matter here because this was a pre-employment test;
  • incorrect genetic counselling for him and his family;
  • and, perversely, the real finding – a thrombotic risk factor – left undetected.

One caveat to teach. Non-parallel lines do not always mean interference. When the true factor level is below 1 IU/dL, 'you cannot dilute nothing'. The clotting times are grossly prolonged and nearly identical at every dilution. That pattern is told apart by how severe the prolongation is and by the clinical phenotype, which in severe deficiency is a bleeding one.

Think of it like this
A wrong diagnosis label is like a wrong entry on a credit record: it follows the person for life, closes doors, and hides the real issue.
Common mistake
Treating any non-parallel curve as interference without remembering the 'cannot dilute nothing' pattern of severe deficiency.
Remember
Normal chromogenic FVIII, no bleeding, non-parallel lines: not haemophilia.

What earns the marks

  • True deficiency: parallel dilutions, full correction on mixing, bleeding history (all absent here)
  • Chromogenic FVIII normal at 91 IU/dL
  • Consequences of a wrong label: treatment and product exposure, insurance and employment, genetic counselling, missed thrombotic risk
  • Caveat: below 1 IU/dL 'cannot dilute nothing'; distinguished by severity and phenotype
Evidence[6]Ruinemans-Koerts 2010Practical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays (apparent factor deficiency, the 'cannot dilute nothing' caveat)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
One-stage and chromogenic factor VIII can disagree in both directions

Chromogenic factor VIII assays tend to be insensitive to lupus anticoagulants [S1], which is why they rescue this case. But the two methods do not always agree for other reasons. Up to 10% of non-severe haemophilia A has a normal APTT and normal one-stage factor VIII with a reduced chromogenic result [S2], and the opposite discrepancy has also been described [S3]. BSH therefore advises a chromogenic assay alongside the one-stage assay whenever non-severe haemophilia A is possible [S4], and asks laboratories to know how lupus anticoagulants affect their own reagents and to check parallelism [S5].

Prevention: the ISTH advises a routine APTT reagent with minimal lupus sensitivity, to avoid needless work-up of healthy people like this man [S6].

  1. [S1] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776These assays tend to be LA-insensitive.
  2. [S2] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776Up to 10% of non-severe haemophilia A cases have a normal APTT and normal FVIII by OSCA, but a reduced FVIII by CA.
  3. [S3] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776The opposite discrepancy (FVIII reduced in OSCA but normal in CA) has also been described.
  4. [S4] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776If non-severe haemophilia A is a possibility a two-stage chromogenic assay (CA) for FVIII should be performed in addition to an OSCA
  5. [S5] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776Laboratories should locally determine the effect of lupus anticoagulant on the PT and APTT reagents used in OSCA. Results should be scrutinised for parallelism
  6. [S6] Devreese KMJ et al. 2020. Guidance from the ISTH SSC for lupus anticoagulant/antiphospholipid antibodies: update of the guidelines for lupus anticoagulant detection and interpretation. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:2828-2839 PMID 33462974 · doi:10.1111/jth.15047For routine APTT testing, a reagent with minimal response to LA should be used, to avoid unnecessary evaluations for aPL in otherwise asymptomatic individuals.
Q4
Explain the value of the chromogenic assay here, and give one alternative laboratory approach that would achieve the same end.
4 marks

Model answer

The chromogenic assay is a defined two-stage system with optimised factor IXa, factor X, calcium and phospholipid, generating factor Xa that cleaves a chromogenic substrate. Phospholipid is in excess and the endpoint is colorimetric, not clot-based, so it is largely resistant to the phospholipid-dependent interference that invalidates the one-stage assay; it gives the true FVIII level and resolves the discrepancy. Alternative: repeat the one-stage assay with a lupus anticoagulant-insensitive APTT reagent (high phospholipid); parallel dilution curves mean valid results.

Explained step by step

How the chromogenic assay works. It is a defined two-stage system. The reagents supply optimised amounts of factor IXa, factor X, calcium and phospholipid. The patient's factor VIII drives the making of factor Xa. That factor Xa then cuts a chromogenic substrate, which releases a colour. The more colour, the more factor VIII.

Why it is not fooled by the lupus anticoagulant. Two reasons:

  • the phospholipid is supplied in excess, so the lupus anticoagulant cannot block it all;
  • the endpoint is a colour, not a clot.
So it is largely resistant to the phospholipid-dependent interference that spoils the one-stage assay. It gives the true factor VIII level (here 91 IU/dL) and resolves the discrepancy.

Alternative approach. Repeat the one-stage assay using a lupus anticoagulant-insensitive APTT reagent: one with a high phospholipid content. If the dilution curves are then parallel, the results are valid.

Laboratory practice point. Practical Haemostasis advises that low-lupus-sensitivity reagents should be used for one-stage factor assays unless a lupus anticoagulant has already been excluded.

Think of it like this
The one-stage assay is a lock the lupus anticoagulant can jam. The chromogenic assay uses so many spare keys (excess phospholipid) and reads a colour instead of the lock, so the jam no longer matters.
Common mistake
Suggesting simply repeating the same one-stage assay at more dilutions, rather than changing to a method or reagent that resists the interference.
Remember
Excess phospholipid and a colour endpoint: chromogenic sees past the lupus anticoagulant.

What earns the marks

  • Chromogenic: two-stage system with factor IXa, factor X, calcium, phospholipid; factor Xa cleaves a chromogenic substrate
  • Phospholipid in excess and colorimetric endpoint: largely resistant to lupus anticoagulant interference
  • Gives the true FVIII level and resolves the discrepancy
  • Alternative: one-stage assay with a lupus anticoagulant-insensitive (high phospholipid) APTT reagent; parallel curves mean valid
EvidencePractical Haemostasis — 1-Stage APTT Factor Assays (Comment 6 on lupus anticoagulant-insensitive reagents)
Q5
Write the final report. What is the one clinical question you must answer before deciding whether he needs any follow-up?
4 marks

Model answer

Report: lupus anticoagulant detected (dRVVT prolonged with phospholipid-dependent correction; 1:1 mix immediate, non-correcting). One-stage FVIII non-parallel and analytically invalid, not to be interpreted as factor levels. Chromogenic FVIII normal at 91 IU/dL. No evidence of factor VIII deficiency or a bleeding disorder. Lupus anticoagulant is a thrombotic, not haemorrhagic, risk marker; confirm with repeat at least 12 weeks apart plus anticardiolipin and anti-beta2-glycoprotein I antibodies. Clinical question: any personal or family history of thrombosis (or pregnancy morbidity in a woman)? If none: repeat at 12 weeks, explanation, thromboprophylaxis advice in high-risk periods, screen for secondary causes; no anticoagulation or aspirin; tell him clearly this is not a bleeding disorder.

Explained step by step

The report should say: "Lupus anticoagulant detected: dRVVT prolonged with phospholipid-dependent correction, and a 1:1 mix showing immediate, non-correcting inhibition. One-stage factor VIII assays show marked non-parallelism across dilutions and are analytically invalid; these results must not be interpreted as factor levels. Chromogenic factor VIII is normal at 91 IU/dL. There is no laboratory evidence of factor VIII deficiency and no evidence of a bleeding disorder. A lupus anticoagulant is a thrombotic, not a haemorrhagic, risk marker. Confirmation requires a repeat sample at least 12 weeks apart, together with anticardiolipin and anti-beta2-glycoprotein I antibodies."

Why each part matters. The report explains the finding, cancels the misleading factor VIII numbers, gives the true level, says plainly that he does not have a bleeding disorder, and sets out the confirmation needed. A lupus anticoagulant causes a long clotting time in the tube, but in the body it is linked with clotting (thrombosis), not bleeding.

The one clinical question: is there any personal or family history of thrombosis? (In a female patient, also ask about pregnancy morbidity.) That answer determines everything that follows.

If he is truly asymptomatic. An isolated lupus anticoagulant found by chance at a pre-employment check usually needs no intervention beyond:

  • repeat testing at 12 weeks;
  • a clear explanation;
  • advice on thromboprophylaxis during future high-risk periods such as surgery or immobility;
  • screening for a secondary cause: systemic lupus erythematosus, infection, drugs.
He needs neither anticoagulation nor aspirin on this result alone. He must be told clearly that this is not a bleeding disorder, otherwise the referral letter will keep that wrong impression alive.

Think of it like this
The report is like a weather correction: 'The storm warning was a sensor fault. There is no flood risk. There is, however, a separate frost risk to keep an eye on.'
Common mistake
Starting aspirin or anticoagulation on a single incidental lupus anticoagulant in an asymptomatic man, or failing to state in the report that there is no bleeding disorder.
Remember
Lupus anticoagulant: long clotting time in the tube, clotting risk in the body; ask about thrombosis.

What earns the marks

  • Report: lupus anticoagulant detected with the supporting evidence; one-stage results invalid; chromogenic FVIII 91 IU/dL normal
  • States clearly no factor VIII deficiency and no bleeding disorder; lupus anticoagulant is a thrombotic risk marker
  • Confirmation: repeat at least 12 weeks apart with anticardiolipin and anti-beta2-glycoprotein I antibodies
  • Clinical question: personal or family history of thrombosis (pregnancy morbidity in a woman); if none, no anticoagulation or aspirin, thromboprophylaxis advice, screen for secondary cause
Evidence[12]Devreese 2020Practical Haemostasis — dRVVT
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Aspirin for an asymptomatic lupus anticoagulant? UK and EULAR differ

UK guidance (BSH 2024) does not recommend routine low-dose aspirin or hydroxychloroquine for primary prevention in asymptomatic antibody carriers [S1]. It suggests thromboprophylaxis in high-risk situations such as surgery or immobility for anyone with persistently positive antibodies [S2].

EULAR 2019 differs: for asymptomatic carriers with a high-risk profile, low-dose aspirin (75–100 mg daily) is recommended [S3], and that profile includes a lupus anticoagulant found on two or more occasions at least 12 weeks apart [S4]. BSH acknowledges the EULAR position while noting the lack of evidence of benefit [S5].

For a UK examiner the BSH answer is expected, but a strong candidate names the difference, and nothing is decided until persistence is confirmed.

  1. [S1] Arachchillage DJ et al. 2024. Guidelines on the investigation and management of antiphospholipid syndrome (BSH). Br J Haematol 2024;205:855-880 PMID 39031476 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19635Routine use of LDA or hydroxychloroquine for primary prevention of thrombosis in asymptomatic aPL carriers is not recommended (2B).
  2. [S2] Arachchillage DJ et al. 2024. Guidelines on the investigation and management of antiphospholipid syndrome (BSH). Br J Haematol 2024;205:855-880 PMID 39031476 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19635We suggest that all individuals found to have persistently positive aPL with or without additional vascular risk factors receive thromboprophylaxis in high-risk situations such as following surgery or prolonged immobility (2C).
  3. [S3] Tektonidou MG et al. 2019. EULAR recommendations for the management of antiphospholipid syndrome in adults. Ann Rheum Dis 2019;78:1296-1304 PMID 31092409 · doi:10.1136/annrheumdis-2019-215213In asymptomatic aPL carriers (not fulfilling any vascular or obstetric APS classification criteria) with a high-risk aPL profile with or without traditional risk factors, prophylactic treatment with low-dose aspirin (LDA) (75–100 mg daily) is recommended.
  4. [S4] Tektonidou MG et al. 2019. EULAR recommendations for the management of antiphospholipid syndrome in adults. Ann Rheum Dis 2019;78:1296-1304 PMID 31092409 · doi:10.1136/annrheumdis-2019-215213The presence (in 2 or more occasions at least 12 weeks apart) of lupus anticoagulant (measured according to ISTH guidelines)
  5. [S5] Arachchillage DJ et al. 2024. Guidelines on the investigation and management of antiphospholipid syndrome (BSH). Br J Haematol 2024;205:855-880 PMID 39031476 · doi:10.1111/bjh.19635Despite the lack of evidence of benefit of aspirin in primary prevention of thrombosis, EULAR guidelines recommend LDA for individuals with a high-risk aPL profile with or without traditional risk factors.

Take home from this station

  1. A long APTT with no bleeding history, a non-correcting immediate mix and phospholipid-dependent dRVVT correction is a lupus anticoagulant, not haemophilia.
  2. One-stage factor results that rise with dilution are non-parallel and invalid; never report them as factor levels.
  3. The chromogenic factor VIII assay, or a lupus anticoagulant-insensitive APTT reagent, gives the true factor level.
  4. A lupus anticoagulant is a thrombotic risk marker; the deciding clinical question is any history of thrombosis.
14
Extension 14 · 20 marks

A pre-operative screen that cancelled surgery

What this station tests: Can you recognise heparin contamination of a sample taken from a central line, prove it, stop it being misread as a lupus anticoagulant, and put right the harm the false result caused?

The case

A pre-operative patient with no anticoagulant prescription and no bleeding history has a coagulation screen drawn from an indwelling central venous catheter. The results prompt cancellation of surgery and a referral for lupus anticoagulant testing. A repeat sample is taken by clean peripheral venepuncture.

In plain wordsA patient due for surgery, who takes no anticoagulant and has never bled, has a clotting screen taken from a central venous catheter. The results are so abnormal that surgery is cancelled and a lupus anticoagulant test is requested. A repeat sample taken from a vein in the arm is completely normal.
Line sample versus peripheral venepuncture
TestCentral linePeripheralReference
Prothrombin time13 s12 s11–14 s
Activated partial thromboplastin time118 s31 s27–35 s
Thrombin time>180 s16 s12–17 s
Reptilase time18 s17 s15–20 s
Clauss fibrinogen3.0 g/L3.2 g/L2.0–4.0 g/L

How to read the data

  • Thrombin time over 180 s from the line, 16 s from the arm: the loudest signal. Something in the line sample is blocking thrombin.
  • Activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) 118 s from the line, 31 s from the arm: markedly prolonged in the line sample only.
  • Prothrombin time (PT) 13 s and 12 s: normal on both. The PT is relatively insensitive to heparin.
  • Reptilase time 18 s and 17 s, fibrinogen 3.0 and 3.2 g/L: both normal on both samples. The fibrinogen itself is normal in amount and in function.
  • Same patient, different collection route: the abnormality follows the sampling route, not the patient.

The five questions

Q1
What is the most likely explanation for the first sample?
4 marks

Model answer

Heparin contamination of the sample from a central venous catheter that has been heparin-locked or flushed. The pattern is definitive: grossly prolonged thrombin time, markedly prolonged APTT, near-normal PT (relatively heparin-insensitive, partly because reagents contain heparin neutralisers), and complete normality on a peripheral sample from the same patient. No anticoagulant prescription and no bleeding history: the abnormality travelled with the sampling route, not the patient.

Explained step by step

The answer is heparin contamination. Central venous catheters (CVCs) are often heparin-locked or flushed with heparin to stop them blocking. A sample drawn back through the line can carry that heparin into the tube.

The pattern fits heparin exactly:

  • Thrombin time grossly prolonged (over 180 s): heparin blocks thrombin very strongly.
  • APTT markedly prolonged (118 s).
  • PT near-normal (13 s): the PT is relatively insensitive to heparin, partly because PT reagents contain heparin neutralisers.

The clincher. A clean sample taken from a peripheral vein of the same patient is completely normal. A real clotting disorder would not disappear when the needle moves to the arm.

Check the story. The patient has no anticoagulant prescription and no bleeding history. The abnormality travelled with the sampling route, not with the patient.

Think of it like this
It is like testing the water in a glass that still has washing-up liquid in it. The water looks bad, but the tap is fine; it was the glass.
Common mistake
Accepting the line sample as showing a real coagulopathy, or jumping to lupus anticoagulant without noticing the normal peripheral sample.
Remember
The abnormality followed the line, not the patient.

What earns the marks

  • Heparin contamination from a heparin-locked or flushed central venous catheter
  • Grossly prolonged thrombin time and APTT with near-normal PT
  • PT relatively heparin-insensitive (reagents contain heparin neutralisers)
  • Peripheral sample entirely normal; no anticoagulant or bleeding history: route, not patient
Evidence[3]Kitchen 2021Practical Haemostasis — Pre-Analytical Variables; Thrombin Time
Q2
Explain the discrepancy between the thrombin time and the reptilase time, and name one further test that would prove your explanation rather than merely support it.
4 marks

Model answer

Heparin, through antithrombin, inhibits thrombin, so the thrombin time is grossly prolonged. Reptilase, a snake venom enzyme from Bothrops atrox, cleaves fibrinopeptide A from fibrinogen directly and is not inhibited by antithrombin–heparin or direct thrombin inhibitors, so the reptilase time is normal: thrombin inhibition with normal fibrinogen. Proof: treat plasma with protamine sulphate or heparinase and repeat (correction of thrombin time and APTT demonstrates heparin), or measure anti-Xa on the line sample (detectable in a patient on no heparin is conclusive). Note: dabigatran gives the same thrombin time/reptilase pattern.

Explained step by step

Thrombin time. This test adds thrombin to the plasma and times how long fibrinogen takes to form a clot. Heparin works through antithrombin to inhibit thrombin. So with heparin present, the added thrombin is blocked and the thrombin time is grossly prolonged.

Reptilase time. Reptilase is a snake venom enzyme from Bothrops atrox. It cuts fibrinopeptide A from fibrinogen directly, doing thrombin's job without being thrombin. Antithrombin–heparin cannot inhibit it, and neither can direct thrombin inhibitors. So the reptilase time stays normal.

What the pair tells you. Long thrombin time plus normal reptilase time means the fibrinogen is normal in structure and amount, and the defect is thrombin inhibition.

Proving heparin, not just suggesting it.

  • Treat the plasma with protamine sulphate or heparinase and repeat. If the thrombin time and APTT correct, heparin is shown directly.
  • Or measure an anti-Xa level on the line sample. A detectable level in a patient on no heparin is conclusive.

The differential to remember. A prolonged thrombin time with a normal reptilase time is also the signature of dabigatran. Heparin is distinguished by protamine or heparinase correction and a detectable anti-Xa; dabigatran by the drug history and a calibrated dilute thrombin time. The pattern is not heparin-specific.

Think of it like this
Heparin is a guard who stops only one locksmith (thrombin). The reptilase is a different locksmith the guard does not recognise, so the door (fibrinogen) still opens normally.
Common mistake
Treating a long thrombin time with a normal reptilase time as specific for heparin, forgetting dabigatran.
Remember
Long thrombin time, normal reptilase: thrombin is blocked; protamine or anti-Xa proves heparin.

What earns the marks

  • Heparin acts through antithrombin to inhibit thrombin: thrombin time prolonged
  • Reptilase (Bothrops atrox) cleaves fibrinopeptide A directly, not inhibited by antithrombin–heparin: reptilase time normal
  • Interpretation: thrombin inhibition with normal fibrinogen
  • Proof: protamine sulphate or heparinase correction, or detectable anti-Xa on the line sample
EvidencePractical Haemostasis — Thrombin Time; Reptilase Time
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Long thrombin time, normal reptilase time: the differential

Reptilase (batroxobin, from Bothrops atrox) clots fibrinogen by releasing fibrinopeptide A, so it bypasses thrombin altogether [S1]. A prolonged thrombin time with a normal reptilase time therefore means thrombin is being inhibited. The textbook differential is unfractionated heparin, direct thrombin inhibitors such as dabigatran [S2], and, rarely, heparin-like anticoagulants in malignancy [S3]. Protamine binds and neutralises unfractionated heparin, so correction with protamine points to heparin specifically [S4].

UK DIC guidance recommends using the thrombin time with a reptilase time to exclude heparin contamination when the APTT is prolonged [S5]. Building that pair into a laboratory rule before authorisation stops a contaminated result reaching the surgeon.

  1. [S1] Practical Haemostasis (teaching website, local mirror) — Reptilase Time the snake Bothrops atrox and which cleaves Fibrinogen at Arg16-Gly17 releasing Fibrinopeptide A (FpA) and generating Fibrin
  2. [S2] Practical Haemostasis (teaching website, local mirror) — Reptilase Time Presence of direct Thrombin inhibitors ↑ Normal
  3. [S3] Practical Haemostasis (teaching website, local mirror) — Thrombin Time Heparin-like anticoagulants have been reported (rarely) in patients with malignancies or other disorders, leading to a prolonged Thrombin Time but a normal Reptilase Time.
  4. [S4] Practical Haemostasis (teaching website, local mirror) — Thrombin Time Protamine Sulphate is a highly cationic protein that binds to Unfractionated Heparin neutralising its anticoagulant activity.
  5. [S5] Levi M, Toh CH, Thachil J, Watson HG 2009. Guidelines for the diagnosis and management of disseminated intravascular coagulation (BCSH). Br J Haematol 2009;145:24-33 PMID 19222477 · doi:10.1111/j.1365-2141.2009.07600.xIt does not have a place in the agreed scoring system but may be used along with a reptilase time to exclude heparin contamination of samples in patients with prolonged APTT.
Q3
Why must lupus anticoagulant not be reported from the first sample?
4 marks

Model answer

Heparin invalidates phospholipid-dependent clotting assays: it prolongs the dRVVT and silica clotting time and produces non-correcting mixing studies, so it can give a false-positive lupus anticoagulant. ISTH: some reagents contain heparin neutralisers effective to about 1.0 U/mL, which is why contamination can pass unnoticed, but heparin in a line sample can far exceed this, and the ISTH advises that a prolonged thrombin time should be an indication not to perform lupus anticoagulant testing unless the heparin is removed or neutralised. Practical Haemostasis: heparin prolongs the dRVVT, and a prolonged thrombin time in a suspected antiphospholipid sample should prompt a reptilase time, as a prolonged thrombin time is very rare in genuine antiphospholipid syndrome. Reporting it risks lifelong anticoagulation in a patient with no thrombotic history.

Explained step by step

Heparin fakes the tests. Lupus anticoagulant (LA) testing uses phospholipid-dependent clotting assays, such as the dilute Russell's viper venom time (dRVVT) and the silica clotting time. Heparin prolongs both. It also produces mixing studies that do not correct, because the heparin in the patient's plasma inhibits the normal plasma too. Heparin therefore gives false-positive screening and mixing results, and at high concentrations it makes every result uninterpretable, so a lupus anticoagulant can be neither reported nor excluded: a false-positive lupus anticoagulant.

Why heparin sometimes slips through. The International Society on Thrombosis and Haemostasis (ISTH) notes that some commercial reagents include heparin neutralisers, effective to about 1.0 U/mL. This is why contamination sometimes passes unnoticed. But a line sample can carry far more heparin than that, and the ISTH advises that a prolonged thrombin time should be an indication not to perform lupus anticoagulant testing unless the heparin is removed or neutralised.

The laboratory warning sign. Practical Haemostasis makes the operational point: heparin prolongs the dRVVT, and a prolonged thrombin time in a sample sent for suspected antiphospholipid syndrome should prompt a reptilase time to identify heparin. A prolonged thrombin time is very rare in genuine antiphospholipid syndrome.

The stakes. Reporting a lupus anticoagulant from this sample could commit a patient with no thrombotic history to lifelong anticoagulation, with all its bleeding risk, on the basis of a contaminated tube.

Think of it like this
Testing for a lupus anticoagulant in a heparin-contaminated sample is like checking for a gas leak while someone is cooking on a gas stove next to the detector: the alarm will sound, but it tells you nothing.
Common mistake
Assuming the reagent's heparin neutraliser makes the result safe to report.
Remember
Heparin in the tube means any lupus anticoagulant result is uninterpretable.

What earns the marks

  • Heparin prolongs dRVVT and silica clotting time and gives non-correcting mixes: false-positive lupus anticoagulant
  • ISTH: reagent heparin neutralisers work to about 1.0 U/mL; a prolonged TT means do not test for LA (ISTH)
  • Prolonged thrombin time in suspected antiphospholipid syndrome should prompt a reptilase time; prolonged thrombin time very rare in genuine antiphospholipid syndrome
  • Consequence: risk of unnecessary lifelong anticoagulation
Evidence[13]Tripodi 2020Practical Haemostasis — dRVVT (Comment 3: a prolonged thrombin time in suspected antiphospholipid syndrome should prompt a reptilase time to confirm heparin)
Evidence check verified against the sources below
When not to test for lupus anticoagulant

ISTH guidance advises that when a patient's drug or clinical history is unknown, the laboratory should first run a PT, APTT and thrombin time [S1]. A prolonged thrombin time, suggesting heparin or dabigatran, is an indication not to perform lupus anticoagulant testing unless the drug is removed or neutralised [S2]. Some lupus reagents contain heparin neutralisers, but only up to about 1.0 U/mL [S3].

The nuance: heparin clearly gives false-positive screening and mixing results [S4], yet in one study heparin affected the dRVVT at supra-therapeutic levels without producing a false-positive result in the full three-step procedure [S5]. So the correct word for a contaminated sample is not 'positive' but uninterpretable, and the request should be cancelled.

  1. [S1] Tripodi A et al. 2020. Lupus anticoagulant detection in anticoagulated patients. Guidance from the ISTH SSC. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:1569-1575 PMID 32619349 · doi:10.1111/jth.14846Whenever testing for LA in patients, whose pharmacological and/or clinical history is unknown, we recommend routine laboratory testing such as prothrombin time (PT), activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) and thrombin time (TT).
  2. [S2] Tripodi A et al. 2020. Lupus anticoagulant detection in anticoagulated patients. Guidance from the ISTH SSC. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:1569-1575 PMID 32619349 · doi:10.1111/jth.14846Prolonged TT (suggesting dabigatran or UFH) should be an indication to not perform LA testing, unless the drugs are removed or neutralized.
  3. [S3] Tripodi A et al. 2020. Lupus anticoagulant detection in anticoagulated patients. Guidance from the ISTH SSC. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:1569-1575 PMID 32619349 · doi:10.1111/jth.14846Some commercial tests include in their composition heparin-neutralizers able to quench unfractionated or low molecular weight heparin up to 1.0 U/mL.
  4. [S4] Tripodi A et al. 2020. Lupus anticoagulant detection in anticoagulated patients. Guidance from the ISTH SSC. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:1569-1575 PMID 32619349 · doi:10.1111/jth.14846UFH clearly affects LA assays, especially APTT-based tests, with false-positive screening and mixing results.
  5. [S5] Devreese KMJ et al. 2020. Guidance from the ISTH SSC for lupus anticoagulant/antiphospholipid antibodies: update of the guidelines for lupus anticoagulant detection and interpretation. J Thromb Haemost 2020;18:2828-2839 PMID 33462974 · doi:10.1111/jth.15047unfractionated heparin (UFH) and enoxaparin affect the dRVVT at supra-therapeutic anti-Xa levels but did not lead to false-positive LA in the three step procedure
Q4
What pre-analytical measures prevent recurrence?
4 marks

Model answer

Collect by clean peripheral venepuncture wherever possible (the most effective measure). If a line must be used, follow a validated protocol: stop any infusion, flush the line with saline, withdraw and discard a volume larger than the line's dead space into a labelled discard tube, then take the citrate sample and label it with the collection site. Observe the order of draw (blood cultures first, then the citrate tube, before any tube with other additives); fill to the mark to keep the 1:9 ratio. Do not sample through a heparin-bonded catheter. Educate phlebotomy, theatre and critical care staff. In the laboratory, treat an isolated grossly prolonged thrombin time with normal reptilase time as a contamination flag prompting a query before authorisation.

Explained step by step

The single most effective measure: collect coagulation samples by a clean peripheral venepuncture wherever possible.

If a line really must be used, follow a validated protocol:

  • stop any infusion running through it;
  • flush the line with saline, then withdraw a volume larger than the line's dead space into a labelled discard tube before taking the sample;
  • label the specimen clearly with the collection site, so the laboratory knows how to interpret it.

Get the tube right. Observe the order of draw: take blood cultures first, then the citrate tube, before tubes containing other additives. Fill it to the mark, so the 1:9 ratio of citrate to blood is kept. Do not draw coagulation samples through a heparin-bonded catheter.

People and systems. Educate phlebotomy, theatre and critical care staff, because this is a recurrent and entirely preventable error.

A safety net in the laboratory. Treat an isolated, grossly prolonged thrombin time with a normal reptilase time as a contamination flag. It should prompt a query before the result is authorised, so a contaminated result never reaches the surgeon in the first place.

Think of it like this
Sampling through a heparin-locked line is like taking a water sample from a hose that has just been used to spray weedkiller. Use a clean tap, or at least run the hose to waste first and label where the sample came from.
Common mistake
Listing only 'take a peripheral sample' and forgetting the laboratory's own role in flagging the pattern before authorising.
Remember
Arm not line; if line, stop, discard, label; lab flags long thrombin time with normal reptilase.

What earns the marks

  • Clean peripheral venepuncture wherever possible
  • Line protocol: stop infusion, saline flush, discard more than the dead space, label collection site; no heparin-bonded catheter sampling
  • Order of draw (citrate first) and fill to the mark for the 1:9 ratio
  • Staff education and a laboratory contamination flag (long thrombin time, normal reptilase) before authorisation
Evidence[3]Kitchen 2021Practical Haemostasis — Pre-Analytical Variables
Evidence check verified against the sources below
Sampling from a line: what the guidelines actually say

BSH: do not use heparin-contaminated venous lines; where this is unavoidable because of poor venous access, flush the line with saline and use a labelled discard tube [S1]. ICSH: no discard is needed for a straight needle, but it is needed when blood is taken through butterfly needles or indwelling catheters [S2], and the discard should typically be larger than the dead space of the tubing [S3]. ICSH also describes a classic route of contamination: concentrated heparin transferred onto a catheter port by a blood-gas syringe and then drawn into the citrate sample [S4].

Neither guideline fixes a single discard volume, so follow a locally validated protocol, and always label the sample with the collection site so the laboratory can interpret it.

  1. [S1] Baker P et al. 2020. Guidelines on the laboratory aspects of assays used in haemostasis and thrombosis (BSH). Br J Haematol 2020;191:347-362 PMID 32537743 · doi:10.1111/bjh.16776Do not use heparin-contaminated venous lines. Where this is unavoidable because of poor venous access, flush the line with saline and use a labelled discard tube.
  2. [S2] Kitchen S et al. 2021. ICSH recommendations for collection of blood samples for coagulation testing. Int J Lab Hematol 2021;43:571-580 PMID 34097805 · doi:10.1111/ijlh.13584There is no requirement to discard the first volume of blood collected prior to collecting coagulation samples via the same needle, except when blood is collected through butterfly needles or indwelling catheters
  3. [S3] Kitchen S et al. 2021. ICSH recommendations for collection of blood samples for coagulation testing. Int J Lab Hematol 2021;43:571-580 PMID 34097805 · doi:10.1111/ijlh.13584it is advisable that the air or the infusion fluids contained in the tubing have been completely discarded (eg using an appropriate discard volume, typically larger than their dead space, ahead of that collected for coagulation testing)
  4. [S4] Kitchen S et al. 2021. ICSH recommendations for collection of blood samples for coagulation testing. Int J Lab Hematol 2021;43:571-580 PMID 34097805 · doi:10.1111/ijlh.13584One rare example can occur when a blood gas syringe is connected to the port of a catheter leading to transfer of highly concentrated heparin onto the port.
Q5
Write the final report and state the communication action required.
4 marks

Model answer

Report: line sample invalid; grossly prolonged thrombin time with normal reptilase time and near-normal PT consistent with heparin contamination from the line. Peripheral repeat entirely normal (PT 12 s, APTT 31 s, thrombin time 16 s, fibrinogen 3.2 g/L). No evidence of a coagulopathy and no indication to postpone surgery. Lupus anticoagulant request cancelled as heparin invalidates these assays; re-refer on clinical grounds if indicated. Communication: telephone the surgical team directly and record the conversation; withdraw the original report; raise an incident for the cancelled procedure; feed back to the ward on line sampling.

Explained step by step

The report should say: "The coagulation screen taken from the central venous catheter is invalid: the pattern of a grossly prolonged thrombin time with a normal reptilase time and a near-normal prothrombin time is consistent with heparin contamination from the line. A repeat sample by peripheral venepuncture is entirely normal (prothrombin time 12 s, activated partial thromboplastin time 31 s, thrombin time 16 s, fibrinogen 3.2 g/L). There is no evidence of a coagulopathy and no indication to postpone surgery. The request for lupus anticoagulant testing has been cancelled, as heparin invalidates these assays; please re-refer on clinical grounds if indicated."

Why the report is written this way. It says the first result is invalid and why, gives the true results, answers the surgeon's real question (can surgery go ahead?), and closes the lupus anticoagulant request while leaving the door open if there is a genuine clinical reason.

Communication – a filed amendment is not enough. The invalid result cancelled an operation, so:

  • telephone the surgical team directly, and record the conversation;
  • withdraw the original report, rather than leaving both versions on the record;
  • raise an incident to capture the cancelled procedure;
  • feed back to the ward on line-sampling technique.

Think of it like this
If a false fire alarm has emptied a building, you do not just change the logbook; you tell the people outside directly that it is safe to go back in, and then find out why the alarm went off.
Common mistake
Issuing a quiet amended report and assuming the surgical team will notice it, or leaving the invalid and valid reports both on the record.
Remember
Invalid result cancelled surgery: phone, withdraw, report the incident, feed back.

What earns the marks

  • Report states line sample invalid and consistent with heparin contamination, with the reasoning
  • Gives the normal peripheral results and states no coagulopathy, no reason to postpone surgery
  • Cancels the lupus anticoagulant request, with re-referral on clinical grounds if indicated
  • Telephones the surgical team and documents it; withdraws the original report; incident report; ward feedback
Evidence[3]Kitchen 2021[13]Tripodi 2020Practical Haemostasis — Pre-Analytical Variables

Take home from this station

  1. A grossly prolonged thrombin time and APTT with a near-normal PT from a central line, and a normal peripheral sample, means heparin contamination.
  2. A long thrombin time with a normal reptilase time shows thrombin inhibition; protamine or heparinase correction, or a detectable anti-Xa, proves heparin.
  3. Never report a lupus anticoagulant from a heparin-contaminated sample; heparin produces a false positive.
  4. When a false result has caused harm, telephone the team, withdraw the original report and raise an incident.

Where a claim was overstated or not supported, the answer, the examiner key and the slides were corrected (38 questions changed). 41 short evidence notes were added where a candidate could reasonably be asked for more.
